# os64 Technical Debt Ledger

*The single sortable index of every known debt, harvested from the design
docs. Each subsystem doc remains the SOURCE OF TRUTH for its own debts (full
rationale, code sites, gotchas live there); this file is the cross-cutting
worklist you scan on a fix day to answer "what's the highest-value cheap fix
right now?" — a question no single doc can answer.*

**Maintenance rule:** when a debt is paid, delete its row here AND update the
owning section in its source doc (the two must not drift). When a review adds
a debt to a subsystem doc, add its row here too. Severity/cost are
hobby-scale judgment calls — re-rank freely.

**A row here is work that WILL be done** — every one names a gate, and the
gate firing is what schedules it. A finding that is correct and is NOT going
to be fixed belongs in `DECLINED.md` instead, which carries the bar for
saying so. Putting a declination here as a debt with an impossible gate is
how a worklist fills with things nobody intends to do.

- Severity: **Hole** (real defect exploitable/crashing) · **Robustness**
  (turns silent failures into loud ones, or prevents a known future crash) ·
  **Feature-gate** (blocks a planned feature) · **Cleanup** (honesty,
  cruft, perf-at-scale).
- Cost: **XS** (minutes) · **S** (an hour) · **M** (a session) · **L**
  (multi-session / discipline-wide).

| **The r8125 watchdog does not handle a verdict that changes kind in place** (Codex, PR #307 round 10, booked rather than fixed because the review budget was spent — the two rows here outrank ordinary debts for that reason). With TX_WEDGED reported and the receive side also silent, the first resumed completion turns the verdict straight into DEAF; `r8125_check_stalled` sees "still a fault, already reported" and does nothing — no recovery line for the wedge, the one-shot never re-arms, and a dead receive path is never reported. Fix: when `verdict != stall_kind` and both are faults, log the old kind's recovery and report the new one in the same pass. Log-only: no frame, ring or counter is touched by either branch | Robustness | XS | r8125.c `r8125_check_stalled` / r8125_ring.h |
| **`tx_since_rx` is 32-bit and wraps** (Codex, PR #307 round 10, same booking). About 54 hours of transmit-only traffic at the P5's rate wraps it to zero; the next transmit restarts the deaf clock, the verdict reads NONE for two seconds and the watchdog prints a false "frames are arriving again" before reporting DEAF once more. Fix: saturate the count at `R8125_DEAF_FRAMES` in `r8125_transmit` (the rule only asks "at least this many"). Log-only, as above | Robustness | XS | r8125.c `r8125_transmit` |
| **`file_io x2 (concurrent)` reported one exit of 0 once** (2026-08-28, QEMU GUI boot with three GUI apps running, 1 in ~12 suite runs that day). The fixture's only exit-0 path is `main` returning normally, which it never does on success (badge 0x0F11E60D) — so a 0 is either an exit code lost between task exit and `os64_wait`, or a burial handing back a stale status. Unread; two clean reruns followed. If it returns, the question is which of the pair's two tasks reported 0 and what its own serial line said | Flake | S | userland/tests/testrun/testrun.c run_concurrent_pair; kernel/test/elf/file_io.c |
| **`memory_test` flakes on FAIL_NEEDLE_STUCK (0xF3EE0009)** — twice in seven QEMU `testrun`s, 2026-08-27. Step 5 takes a memory snapshot, touches 256KB, takes another, and demands `free` fell and `used` rose. Nothing else is held still: the previous fixture's burial (kworker) can hand back more than 256KB between the two snapshots, and the needle moves the wrong way for a reason that has nothing to do with the syscall under test. Cure: compare against a DELTA the test owns — allocate, snapshot, touch, snapshot, and require `used` grew by at least the probe size MINUS whatever `free` gained, or simply retry the step a few times; a suite that cries wolf gets ignored | Flake | XS | kernel/test/elf/memory_test.c step 5 |
| **`test_task_teardown_leak` can be fooled by an allocation that is nobody's task's** — seen 2026-09-05 on the virtio rig with an `os64get` download in the LATE phase: two consecutive windows each "lost" exactly 65,584 bytes (= one 64KB block-cache line plus a 48-byte header) with the burial count and the TCP bracket both clean, so the verdict was "a task leaks 32KB". A clean rerun and the e1000 rig said a task costs nothing. The block cache grows lazily by 64KB lines as files are touched — logd's own log file crossing a line boundary inside the window is enough — and the test's three brackets (burials, allocation stillness, TCP heap moves) cannot see it. A fourth bracket on the cache's line count (`/sys/cache` already publishes it) would turn that window DIRTY instead of FAIL, which is the verdict it earned. Sibling of the memory_test flake row | Flake | XS | the next time it fires on a machine that was doing something | test_main.c teardown_leak_attempt / block_cache.c line growth |
| **`task_teardown_leak` cries wolf when somebody is USING the machine** (2026-09-02). **Chris's diagnosis, which is the authoritative one: the test was written to run in POST-BOOT, where one core is executing and nothing else is going on.** It measures the machine-wide free-page count across a spawn→exit→burial cycle and demands the delta be zero — an assertion that is only meaningful on a quiescent machine — and it moved to the LATE phase on 2026-08-29, which is precisely the phase that runs beside a live userland. The move bought an 18-second boot; the bill is a test whose premise no longer holds where it runs. Seen while driving os64get by hand at the husk prompt: attempt 1 reported 53200 bytes lost (26600/task), attempt 2 reported 1461144 (730572/task), and it failed on its own rule that "the loss REPEATED across 2 independently measured windows, so it is not the machine". The 27× spread is the tell — a real leak in `task_create`/`task_destroy` is a fixed cost per task, not a number that swings that far between two windows eight seconds apart. **The control settles it: the same build booted with nothing typed into it measured `free 8391047560 -> 8391047560` twice, byte-identical, and passed.** Two windows a few seconds apart inside ONE burst of human activity are not independent of each other, so repeating the measurement cannot separate a leak from a bystander. Cures, in order of honesty: measure a DELTA the test owns rather than the machine's total (the `memory_test` row wants the same thing for the same reason — a position-free pointer on purpose: rows move); widen TL_DIRTY so a window that saw any other task spawn is discarded rather than counted; or move it back to post-boot and pay the seconds. Until then, read a late-phase failure of this test as a question, not a verdict — and reproduce it on an idle boot before believing it | Flake | S | soon (Chris, 2026-09-02); or the next touch to the late phase | `kernel/test/test_main.c` `teardown_leak_attempt` / DEBTS § memory_test |

One controller works; per-controller state is the fix | Correctness | M | ahci.c init_AHCI_device |
| AHCI still identity-maps in kKernelPML4 ONLY: the ABAR window, each port's command-list base, and `ahciDiskBuffer` (a kmalloc'd buffer addressed by its PHYSICAL address). The NVMe window left that road on 2026-09-06 after it killed the P5 — a doorbell rung under a task's CR3 on a block-cache miss is a kernel #PF, and the ELF loader's direct opens ring one on every spawn whose inode block is not cached. AHCI has the identical shape and dies the same way from any task-context path. Convert to HHDM aliases like every other driver's window; testable on q35's built-in ICH9 (`-device ide-hd`) | Correctness | S | ahci.c init_AHCI_device, ahci_port_rebase |
| AHCI driver cannot WRITE (read op only) — a SATA disk root runs read-only via the 2026-08-08 write-less-device demotion until the write half exists. | ~~Allocator OOM: silent `cli; hlt` → panic naming size~~ **PAID 2026-08-25** (Fable's review of PR #29 rd15): both halts were under the allocator's own lock, so exhaustion parked one core dark and then wedged every other core at its next allocation, interrupts off, with no line anywhere. Now `panic()` by name with the size. The finding that paid it: rd15 built a fallback for `kmalloc()` returning NULL, which cannot happen in this kernel — the allocator never returns "no memory" and kmalloc adds kHHDMOffset regardless. ~44 `kmalloc(...) == NULL` guards in the tree are the same theatre; harmless, but a sweep that deletes them (or the contract line in kmalloc.h that makes them visibly redundant) is a small cleanup for a quiet day. "Largest free extent" in the message is still a nice-to-have | Robustness (PAID) | — | allocator.c `allocate_memory_at_address_internal` |
| Delete debug residue: `>= 200000000` stub in allocator + `kmalloc_dma` twin | Cleanup | XS | MEMORY #3 |
| **`/proc/self` resolves but is never LISTED — and PROC.md still says it doesn't exist.** Found by Chris 2026-08-13: `ls /proc` shows only numeric task IDs, `ls /proc/self` works fine. `proc_parse_path` handles "self" as a special component; `proc_read_dir`'s `PROC_NODE_ROOT` case walks kTaskList and emits decimal IDs only, so the name is openable and invisible. Nothing in the code says that is deliberate, and PROC.md's "What is deliberately NOT here" section STILL reads "**No `/proc/self`**… it arrives when a program demands it" — a program did, it shipped 2026-08-09, and the doc was never struck. **The doc correction is not a judgment call; the listing is.** Two real arguments: LIST IT because names in this house do not lie — a directory answering to a name it refuses to show is the same species of small dishonesty as a log level that misreports its message, and the next person will also find it by accident and wonder what else is hidden. DON'T LIST IT because /proc is processes ONLY, Plan 9 style, and Plan 9 has no `self` at all: it is an alias, not a process, so listing it stops `ls /proc \| wc -l` from being a process count. ("Linux lists it" is not an argument here.) Note the practical objection does NOT apply — `top` already skips non-numeric entries on purpose (`// any future non-task names`), so either choice is safe for the enumerators. Cost is a synthesized entry ahead of the numeric walk plus the doc fix | Cleanup (+ a ruling) | XS | procfs.c `proc_read_dir` / PROC.md § "What is deliberately NOT here" |
| `#DF` IST emergency stack — turn stack-death triple faults into panics | Robustness | S–M | ABI (deferred) |
| **Nothing running says which scheduler it booted with** (2026-10-17). `SCHED=` decides tickless or periodic, and the only record is the command line in the log — where a misspelt value is also the only clue: `SCHED=PERIODIC` (the value is case-sensitive) was ignored on the P5 and the boot ran tickless while everyone believed otherwise. Today's tell is indirect: the compositor is pinned only under periodic (`gui_compositor_affinity`), so its `/proc` thread `affinity` reads `any` under tickless — inference from a rule that may change. Cure: one `scheduler: tickless\|periodic` line in `/sys/cpu/count`, beside the other machine-wide CPU facts | Observability | XS | sysfs.c (`/sys/cpu/count`); kernel_commandline.c `SCHED=` |

## Security / correctness

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Ring 3 can change the GS base the kernel reads its per-CPU storage through.** The kernel never runs `swapgs`: every entry (syscall, the timer and other ISRs, exceptions) reads core-local storage through the live GS base, and loading a GS selector is not privileged in ring 3, so a program can replace that base. The next entry then reads its CLS fields from a user address: proven in QEMU as a kernel #PF in `_schedule_ap` with `GS_BASE 0` (the cold read of PR #340, 2026-10-24). Nothing os64 ships writes GS outside the kernel (no selector load, no `wrgsbase`, `CR4.FSGSBASE` off, no thread-local storage), and no compiler emits a GS load, so only code written to do it reaches it: a regular debt on a one-user system (Chris, 2026-10-25). The fix is the standard discipline: the kernel's pointer in `IA32_KERNEL_GS_BASE`, `swapgs` on every entry from and exit to CPL 3, with the NMI/#MC/#DF entries designed first | Robustness | M | before ring 3 gets thread-local storage in GS, or `CR4.FSGSBASE` is turned on | PR #340's cold read |
| `mp_schedStack` USER bit (`0x7`→`0x3`) | Hole | XS | none | ABI § Mem-prot #1 |
| SMEP enable (CPUID.(07H,0):EBX bit 7) | Robustness | S | none | ABI § Mem-prot #2 |
| SMAP enable + `stac`/`clac` bracketing (CPUID bit 20) | Robustness | L | when copy helpers are the ONLY user-access path (argv setup in task.c is the other) | ABI § Mem-prot #3 |
| ~~Page-0 / VA-0 NULL guard — unmap the low identity window so kernel NULL derefs trap (mind the AP trampoline)~~ **PAID 2026-09-07 by Codex, PR #70**: an explicit absent leaf in every root, `paging_map_page` refusing the first page before any walk, the #PF handler refusing it below VMA lookup, `user_range_accessible` refusing it before a memcpy; preboot test `page_zero_unmapped` (PANIC policy). The trampoline question answered itself: Limine parks APs in long mode, no os64 real-mode code needs VA 0. The BIOS data area is read through a momentary HHDM alias of physical page 0 that is unmapped after the read — left mapped, it would have turned the lazy HHDM's failed-walk tripwire (`0 \| kHHDMOffset`) into a silent read (Fable's post-merge review) | Robustness (PAID) | — | done | paging.c paging_init_null_guard / smp.c mp_find_tables |
| Drop IOPL=3 for ring 3 (currently ring 3 can execute `out`) | Hole | S | once fixtures stop using `out` | ABI (deferred) |
| **`/sys/clipboard`'S PENDING COPY HAS NO LOCK** (booked 2026-09-21; the sibling of Codex #112 finding 1, which was filed against the console-font writer that was modelled on it). Spawn redirection shares one file object between parent and child, and a handle's pin protects the object's lifetime, not the order of its writes. Two `clipboard_append` calls on one `snarf_pending_t` that both reach the growth path each copy, swap and `kfree` the same `bytes` — a double free in the kernel heap, reachable from ring 3 with `prog > /sys/clipboard` where `prog` also writes through the inherited handle from a second thread or child. The cure is the one `console_font_append` now carries: a per-pending lock, allocate and free OUTSIDE it, swap pointers UNDER it (which is already `clipboard.c`'s stated rule for the store; the pending was simply never brought under it) | Hole | S | none — do it with the next clipboard touch, or alone | clipboard.c clipboard_append / console_font.c console_font_append |
| ~~**A CLOSE THAT FAILS IS NEVER HEARD IN RING 3**~~ **PAID 2026-09-22** (booked the day before, found by loading a bad console font with `cp` and getting `$? = 0`). Chris's ruling was "not 0", and the vocabulary doctrine supplied the number: the boundary owns -1 and -2, so the file family's first own code, **`OS64_CLOSE_NOT_COMMITTED` = -3 in the new `abi/include/os64/file.h`**, is what `close` answers when the handle is gone but what was written did not land — a FAT flush that failed, or `/sys/console/font` refusing a font. `handle_close_rc` carries the filesystem's answer up (the old `bool handle_close` is a wrapper for the callers with nowhere to put it); `syscall_close` maps it. `cp`'s "cannot close" line is reachable now, and `/tests/closetest` proves the answers apart. Codex's round on it found the one door left: a close raced by an operation IN FLIGHT on another thread of the same task — the pin holds the file, the unpin does the real close with nowhere to answer to. Close cannot wait for it (a pinned read on a device can block indefinitely) and cannot answer 0 honestly, so it answers `OS64_CLOSE_DEFERRED` (-5 — Codex's second round caught -4 colliding with the system-wide `OS64_INTERRUPTED`, which callers retry on): the verdict is that thread's and went to the log; sync first if it matters. Holders and pins live in ONE word (`vfs_file.holds`) so an unpin drops both in a single atomic step and a closer's own subtraction reads both from the same instant — the same round caught a two-counter version with a window between them. The third round found the one operation that was not counted as one: a spawn's redirection share, a holder until the child's slot exists, which a failed load released with a real close nobody heard — so a share is held as an operation (pin and hold) until `handle_share_install` makes it the child's handle, and `handle_unshare` releases it as one; closetest proves both ends | Correctness (PAID) | — | done | handle.c handle_close_rc / syscall.c syscall_close / abi/include/os64/file.h |
| **`tools/tlsproxy.py` is a STOPGAP and is hardened like one.** Native TLS serves direct HTTPS; the optional terminating helper remains for explicitly configured proxy use on a LAN. Four findings against it were DEFERRED by Chris on 2026-09-03 for exactly that reason — "I'd rather not put a bunch of work into a temporary solution" — and are listed here so the deferral is a decision with a record rather than a silence: (1) `--verbose` logs the origin's reason phrase raw, where every other log site goes through `visible()` — a controlled origin can drive the operator's terminal; (2) a repeated identical `Content-Length` reaches `getheader()` as `"5, 5"` and is forwarded as one field, which os64get refuses, so a legal https reply fails through the proxy that succeeds direct; (3) `INTERIM_MAX` cannot count `100 Continue`, because CPython's `HTTPResponse.begin()` skips consecutive 100s in its own unbounded loop before `final_response` ever sees one — an origin that never stops saying 100 holds a worker; (4) a client that disconnects after the head is not noticed — `send()` swallows the broken pipe and `deliver()` streams the whole upstream body to nobody, and declared-length replies bypass the spool slots, so this is a worker-and-upstream sink per request. None reaches the guest's correctness: (1) and (3) and (4) cost the proxy's host, (2) costs one fetch a retry direct. **Chris retained the optional helper for this integration slice; CONNECT tunneling is deferred until needed. These host-tool debts remain deferred, not fixed by native TLS. Delete this row with the helper if its support is retired.** | Robustness (host tool) | S | if tlsproxy outlives its stopgap role | Codex #52 rd6, deferred by Chris 2026-09-03 |

## Kernel robustness

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **The post-boot write tests need a quiet root, and a log on root breaks that by chance** (booked 2026-10-19). The `TEST_POLICY_RO` write gauntlets (`ext2_published_insert_failure`, `ext2_orphan` and their siblings, test_main.c) measure ROOT: they take its free inode and block counts, work, and require them back exactly, and the fault seam counts every write reaching the mount, whoever makes it. logd drains during the post-boot phase, so with `LOGD=` on root (`/var/log/os64.log`) its appends land inside a test's window on an unlucky boot: `writes=17/10` (the seam failed one of logd's writes instead of the test's) or `BLOCK LEAK: 2 lost` (logd's allocation). The failure then DEMOTES EVERY MOUNT to read-only for that boot (`vfs_demote_all_mounts_readonly`): os64get cannot stage, editors cannot save, and services that write at startup (sshd) fail. Nothing on disk is wrong. The P5 hit it on 2026-10-19 (two of its last three boots); Chris moved the log back to `/home` and archives into `/var/log`. Cure, lean: hold logd's reads at "nothing new" for the post-boot phase (the rings keep the lines, the claim stays alive), so root is quiet while it is measured; the alternative, measuring a partition nothing else writes, stops testing root | Correctness of the TEST | S | a log, or any writer, on root at boot | the P5, 2026-10-19 (`/var/log/os64.log` FAIL lines) |
| **THE MSRs os64 INHERITS RATHER THAN OWNS** (booked 2026-10-18, the day SPEC_CTRL turned out to be one: firmware had given every second thread SSBD, and os64 ran the compositor at a third of its speed on odd APICs for weeks because nothing wrote the register). The rule that came out of it — a per-thread register the OS's behaviour depends on is written by the OS on every thread — has four more candidates, all readable today through `/sys/cpu/<n>/probe`. (1) **Frequency control is unowned**: `CPPC_ENABLE=0`, `CPPC_REQ=0` on the P5 (Intel's HWP has the same shape) — the SMU decides alone, and today boosts on its own (APERF/MPERF read 1.16-1.25x nominal), but an Intel part with HWP present and unenabled can sit at a low ratio forever. First step is the instrument: effective MHz per thread from two APERF/MPERF reads, in `/sys/cpu/<n>` and top; then a `performance =` setting beside `speculation =` only if the numbers ask for it. (2) **Machine checks are off**: CR4.MCE clear, MCi_CTL banks never enabled, so an uncorrectable hardware error is a silent reset instead of a `#MC` with the bank status on the glass — a tripwire the house wants. (3) **Instruments the hardware offers free**: AMD IRPerf (one HWCR bit, then instructions retired) with APERF gives IPC per thread — one `cat` would have shown "thread 1 runs at 0.4 IPC" at the start of the hunt, not the end; DEBUGCTL's LBR stack records the last sixteen branches before a kernel fault, a free call chain where the stack walk gives up. (4) **Niceties with no consumer**: TSC-deadline LAPIC timer (no calibration, exact leases), TSC_AUX so `rdtscp` names the core to ring 3, x2APIC past 255 APIC IDs. MTRRs, HWCR, SYSCFG and Zen's unit-config bits stay inherited on purpose: they are firmware's map of the machine, and every OS keeps them | Robustness | S each (1-3), XS each (4) | a consumer, or the next P5 slowness nobody can see; (1)'s instrument first because it is the eye the rest are judged with | smp_core.c spec_ctrl block; nmi_probe.c MSR table; Chris's saved copy of the 2026-10-18 inventory |
| **UBSAN IN THE KERNEL — the compiler already knows about undefined behaviour, and os64 never asks it.** `-fsanitize=undefined` turns the things the C standard leaves undefined — signed overflow, a shift wider than the type, a misaligned pointer, a null dereference, an array index the compiler can bound — into runtime reports at the site. THE REASON IT MATTERS IS NOT TIDINESS: UB is not "behaves oddly", it is "the optimizer is ENTITLED to assume you never do this", so the failure mode is a check the compiler deletes rather than a wrong answer you can see. **This is the CHEAP half of the sanitizer family and the only one that fits a freestanding kernel today**: it needs no shadow memory and no allocator hooks — just a handful of `__ubsan_handle_*` functions, four lines each, calling `printd`. Linux ships exactly this as CONFIG_UBSAN. Turn it on for a boot, read what falls out, then decide whether it lives behind a cmdline token or a build flag. **ITS BIG SIBLING, KASAN, IS A REAL ARC AND NOT THIS ROW**: byte-granular poisoning needs shadow memory (1/8 of all RAM), allocator hooks and early-boot ordering — and os64 already runs the PAGE-granular ancestor, since the lazy HHDM unmaps freed physical memory so a use-after-free faults (CLAUDE.md names it DEBUG_PAGEALLOC-style). KASAN is that same instinct at byte resolution, which is what catches a three-byte overrun off the end of a row. **HOW THIS GOT BOOKED:** Codex ran the libimage host suite under AddressSanitizer and found a heap-buffer-overflow in our own test fixture, in a run that was printing PASS (PR #30 rd3). The host harness is the ONLY place in this tree where the sanitizers can be linked at all — a freestanding kernel has no runtime for them — which is both why that catch was possible and why the kernel has been flying without any of it | Robustness | S (UBSan) / L (KASAN) | a quiet day — it is a read-what-falls-out slice, not a fix-list | Codex #30 rd3; Chris asked what the flag actually does, 2026-08-25 |
| **Task teardown v1 SHIPPED 2026-08-06 (the graveyard shift) — residuals only.** `task_destroy()` exists: kworker buries COLLECTED corpses (retValCollected via wait/reap, autoReap by decree, or orphans) in a two-phase sweep (unlink pass N, free pass N+1 — one-pass deferral, with no guaranteed sleep), freeing per-thread stacks (the 1MB+64KB per command), thread_t + TID + syscall scratch, path/cwd, static elf_image_t + its open backing file (un-haunts ext2's rm-refusal), and the task_t. **2026-08-13: three of the four residuals PAID, and the survivor is now COUNTED rather than merely admitted.** (3) page-table pages died with the paging arena (a8c952c — `arena_destroy` at burial takes the PML4 and every PDPT/PD/PT in one motion; the pool's bump allocator no longer has anything to do with a task's tables). (2) argv AND env blobs are freed — the row's own dragon warning, "env is CoW-inherited by children", was FALSE and had always been: `env_inherit` (env.c) does a plain memcpy into a fresh block and env.h says so in writing, so every task's environment is exclusively its own. (4) mmaps/shared_objects dlist nodes + every `vma_t` are freed, and burial now RELEASES the one shared_object reference `task_create` took (`shared_object_release`) — one edge on the main image, identified by identity, NOT one per list node: `task->shared_objects` holds the whole closure while the task owns a single reference, so per-node release would drive every library's count negative on the second burial (the pairing rule is written in shared_object.h). refcount is therefore now a true count of live holders; UNLOAD-at-zero is deliberately NOT implemented — retention is its own slice, same split ruled for the block read cache. Also found and paid the same day: the ring-3 exit trampoline page (one `kmalloc_aligned` page per user task whose kernel pointer was a LOCAL that went out of scope) — 4KB per command, on no ledger and in no notes doc. **2026-08-15: the last residual is PAID — burial reclaims the VMA backing pages.** The deferral's bill arrived on the P5: each buried task's booked pages pinned `in_use` rows in the allocator's status table forever (dead-owner rows can never merge or compact), `watch -n 1 "ps -ef"` minted ~18 rows/sec, every allocator op is O(rows) (the machine visibly slowed), and at ~5,500s the then-unchecked table walked off its own end onto the kernel PML4 — the logd #PF that birthed the watchpoint subsystem. The ownership question the deferral waited on was answerable without refcounts TODAY: the shared_object page cache is os64's only page-sharing mechanism and `so->page_phys[]` is its authoritative registry, so the burial walk frees every resident frame the registry disclaims (`task_frame_is_shared_object_cache` — written 8/13 as ledger honesty, promoted 8/15 to reclaim guard) and retains the cache's. The page-refcount ruling stays booked to the fork/CoW arc — task-to-task sharing of anonymous pages is the day a registry stops being enough. THE PROOF grew teeth: `task_teardown_leak` (in the LATE phase since 2026-08-29) spawns `/tests/glutton` (a deliberately fat fixture — multiple text pages, dirtied .data, 4 bss pages, 4 heap pages it never unmaps) and asserts a spawn→exit→burial cycle's allocator delta is ZERO **and** that `kTaskVmaReclaimedBytes` climbed by at least the fixture's data+bss+heap floor (so a demand-paging break can't pass vacuously). Also still true: `task_create` must validate before building (`elf_can_load`) — task_destroy only buries the fully-born; a mid-construction unwind path is separate work. And lockless /proc walks need an explicit reader-lifetime guarantee (snapshot-under-lock or generation counts) | Residuals paid; row survives for the two "also still true" items | S | mid-construction unwind: when task_create grows a failure path worth having | `task.c` undertaker / notes doc |
| **A ZOMBIE KEEPS ITS WHOLE ADDRESS SPACE UNTIL SOMEBODY COLLECTS IT — so a parent that is slow to collect holds a dead child's memory hostage.** Unix frees a process's memory at `exit()`; what is left for the parent is a tombstone (the exit status and a few hundred bytes of bookkeeping). os64 frees NOTHING at death: `task_exit_teardown` closes handles and windows, and everything else (VMA backing pages, page tables via the arena, thread stacks, the shared-object references) waits for `task_destroy`, which kworker runs only on a COLLECTED corpse. Collection is the parent's `wait`/`reap`, and husk collects a finished `&` job only when it draws its next prompt (`jobs_poll`, husk.c). Found on the P5 2026-10-06: three `scribe 1GB-file &` from one gterm's husk, all closed, and 3.5 GB stayed allocated because nothing was typed into that terminal again (Chris was in another gterm, and with no taskbar the first one could not be restored). `/proc/<pid>/status` showed the three as `zombie`, exit 0, threads retired, kworker running its passes; the log showed husk 73 never ran another command after the third spawn. **This is not a husk bug and not an allocator bug** — bash also reports "Done" at the next prompt; the difference is what the zombie holds while it waits. **Cure (agreed with Chris, design note first):** split `task_destroy` into STRIP and FREE. kworker strips any retired corpse, collected or not: VMA backing, page tables, stacks, trampoline, argv/env, ELF image, shared-object references. The `task_t` keeps pid, name, parent and retVal until it is collected, and FREE then releases the struct, the TID and the list links. The careful part is every reader that can meet a stripped corpse: `/proc/<pid>/maps`, `mem`, `heap` and `thread`, `ps`, the parent's `wait`/`reap`, and the orphanage. They must see "no address space", never freed memory, which ties into the lockless-/proc item in the teardown row above. Fixture: spawn a fat child as a background job, let it exit without collecting it, and assert the allocator has its frames back after one kworker pass | Hole | M | none — every slow collector pays (a husk sitting at a prompt, a script that backgrounds and never waits, any parent that never calls `wait`) | `task.c` `task_destroy` / `task_reap_eligible_zombies`; husk.c `jobs_poll`; P5 2026-10-06 |
| **FIXED 2026-08-13 — the graveyard race: an unlocked list append leaked whole tasks.** `task_enqueue_dead_child` was a read-modify-write on shared state with NO lock: read `parent->deadChildTail`, write `tail->deadChildNext`, store the new tail. Two siblings exiting on two cores at the same instant both read the same tail, the second's `->deadChildNext` write silently discarded the first's, and the last store won — leaving ONE child on no list at all. Not a zombie (a zombie is reachable and deliberate) but INVISIBLE: kworker's sweep only ever walks these lists, so an unlisted corpse can never be examined, collected, or buried, and every byte it owns leaks for the rest of the boot. Reproduced in `test_dynamic_linking`, which spawns two tasks that exit on the same tick on different APs and lost one of them ROUGHLY HALF THE TIME — for as long as that test has existed. The symptom was pure absence: no panic, no error, one task quietly missing from a list nobody prints. Fix: `kDeadChildLock`, one global irqsave spinlock over every deadChild list (global not per-task because the orphanage touches two parents' lists in one motion, and a per-task ordering is a deadlock waiting for its first mistake). Lock order is kDeadChildLock → scheduler queue lock, safe BY CONSTRUCTION since nothing in scheduler.c touches a deadChild list; the two calls that would have inverted it — the parent wake, and `*exitCode`'s store to a caller pointer that can fault — are deliberately performed after the release. Verified 9/9 boots clean against a ~50% pre-fix failure rate. Left behind as a tripwire: the sweep now REPORTS a collected-but-unburiable corpse on DEBUG_TASK instead of silently stepping over it every 2s forever | Hole (FIXED) | — | done | `task.c` `task_enqueue_dead_child` / kDeadChildLock |
| **PAID 2026-08-22, on the day the condition it was waiting for came true.** The row read "when userland gains dynamic binaries" and userland gained them that afternoon, so the panic had to go the same day. `elf_resolve_dynamic_dependencies` now returns false and `task_create` unwinds; `shared_object_load_or_get` returns NULL for every failure it can have (missing file, malformed ELF, wrong e_type, unloadable `DT_NEEDED`, exhausted virtual window, out of memory), unwinding a partially-registered object — deps released, registry node removed, window slot reclaimed if it was the last bump. The FAULT path was paid too, and it was the bigger half: `shared_object_resolve_page` had four panics of its own (page alloc, read failure, relocation failure) that a corrupt `.so` could reach from ring 3 at any moment long after load; each now releases its `page_phys[]` slot back to 0 — never leaving it RESOLVING, or every core spinning on that page would spin forever — and returns 0, which the fault handler turns into a segfault-kill of the faulting task rather than a machine panic. Proven live: a binary whose `DT_NEEDED` names a library that does not exist answers `husk: cannot run nolib` and the shell keeps its prompt | Hole (FIXED) | — | done | `task.c` / `shared_object.c` / `simple_exceptions.c` |
| ~~Allocator silent-OOM → panic~~ PAID 2026-08-25 — see the Quick-wins row | Robustness (PAID) | — | done | allocator.c |
| `#DF` IST emergency stack | Robustness | S–M | none | ABI (deferred) |
| **A PLAIN SPINLOCK WAITED ON WITH INTERRUPTS OFF HANGS A ONE-CPU MACHINE** (booked 2026-10-20, found by the CPU-numbers slice's `nosmp` acceptance boots). `spinlock.h`'s plain variant leaves interrupts on, so its holder can be preempted, and its doctrine accepts that because "waiters spin through their quantum". A waiter with interrupts OFF has no quantum. `call_in_kernel_context` is irqsave (the stack-poisoner cure, task_exit_asm.S), so every lock taken inside it is waited on with IF=0, and so is everything the #PF handler's demand paging reaches. On SMP another CPU eventually runs the preempted holder, which is why no multi-CPU boot has ever shown it. On one CPU nothing can, and the machine spins forever. Caught twice on `nosmp` boots of the 3900X topology (`make run-sparse` + `nosmp`), in the post-boot tests, RIP in `spinlock_acquire`, RFLAGS 0x3002: once on the block cache's `sLock` (`#PF` → cikc → `elf_read_page` → `bc_read_shim`), once on `kOpenFileLock` (cikc → `open_do` → `vfs_openfile_register`). Roughly 2 boots in 3 on that topology with the CPU-numbers change, never on dense `nosmp`; the kernel before that change survived three sparse `nosmp` boots, so its timing (one log ring instead of 24, one LAPIC calibration instead of 24) may make the race likelier, but the code that races predates it. The plain locks today: block_cache.c `sLock`; vfs.c `kOpenFileLock`, `kMountTableLock`, `kNamespaceLock`; sysfs.c; icmp_conn.c; tcp.c. The block cache's is never held across I/O and takes irqsave cleanly, but converting it alone only moved the hang to `kOpenFileLock`. `kNamespaceLock` IS held across disk I/O by design (CLAUDE.md, the three locks), so irqsave is wrong for it and the answer there is a sleeping lock or keeping it out of interrupts-off paths. Slice: audit each plain lock for "can an IF=0 path wait on it", convert or restructure, and make the doctrine say which waiters are allowed | Hole (one-CPU boots) | M | before `nosmp`/`MAXCPUS=1` is relied on, and before any single-CPU machine | spinlock.h / block_cache.c / vfs.c; the 2026-10-20 sparse `nosmp` boots |

The second call also re-derives `kernel_rsp0` from the TSS after `ap_wakeup_entry` set it from the AP's own stack. Harmless today, found by reading. Cure: split the publish (GS, `cpu`, `apic`, `self`) from the one-time setup, or make the second call skip what the first did | Cleanup | XS | none | smp_core.c `init_core_local_storage` (found 2026-10-20) |

REMAINING: `/bin/shutdown` + husk wiring (Chris's, by his call); hard AP quiesce via IPI-halt (soft today: tickless parks idle APs, logd retired, shell blocked — quiescent in practice, not by construction); ATA FLUSH CACHE rides the AHCI-write row | Robustness | S each | /bin/shutdown: none; AP-halt: before shutdown runs on a busy multi-user-ish system | shutdown.c descent comment |
| **No native fsck / mount-time dirty check**: os64 mounts whatever the superblock claims, unverified — a torn disk mounts writable and compounds the damage. Today's auditor is host-side e2fsck from Ubuntu, which works but means the OS can't vouch for its own disks. v1 is NOT a repair tool: read-only verify (superblock sanity + the ext2 `s_state` clean flag honored at mount, warn-and-forced_ro on dirty) buys most of the safety for a fraction of the cost; a real repairing fsck.ext2 is its own arc | Robustness | v1 S, full L | after the shutdown verb (a clean-unmount flag is only meaningful once unmounting cleanly is possible) | ext2.c mount path / Chris 2026-08-08 |
| **IRQ0's EOI can go to the wrong controller, stranding a LAPIC ISR bit forever** (found by inspection 2026-08-10 while chasing the VBox freeze). `handler_irq0_asm` picks its EOI target by branching on `kIRQ0UsesLapic` — LAPIC if set, legacy PIC (`out 0x20`) if not. But `ioapic_adopt_isa_irq` (apic.c) sets that flag LAST: `ioapic_route_irq()` makes the redirection entry live, the PIC line is masked, and only THEN does `*uses_lapic_flag = true`. An IRQ0 delivered through the IOAPIC inside that window is acknowledged to the PIC while its LAPIC ISR bit stays set — and a set ISR bit is never cleared by anything else, so PPR pins at that vector's class for the rest of the boot, blocking every lower-priority interrupt permanently. Today the vector is 0x20 (class 2), so a stranded bit blocks almost nothing and the bug is invisible; that is exactly why it survived. It stops being invisible the moment anyone raises IRQ0's priority (see the wall-clock row) or lowers something important beneath it. Fix is one reorder — set the flag BEFORE the routing write, since a PIC EOI for an interrupt that never came is harmless while the reverse is fatal. NOTE the limit of the evidence: this does NOT by itself explain why 0x20 boots VBox cleanly and 0xE0 does not, so it is a real defect found on the way, not a proven cause of that freeze. Confirming a stranded bit needs one reading — LAPIC ISR (0x100..0x170) on a wedged guest | Hole (latent, lethal under any priority change) | XS | none — cheapest possible insurance against a whole symptom class | apic.c `ioapic_adopt_isa_irq` / handler_irq0_timer.S |

The address is non-page-aligned and `len=0x0`, which reads as a free-path lookup for something the allocator does not own — a double free or a freed-with-wrong-length during teardown is the shape to look for; the deferred-teardown residuals row above is the obvious neighborhood. TWO co-occurring oddities that may be one story or three: (1) hog EXITED AT ALL — with no argument its burn loop has no exit path (`endTick` stays 0), and it was NOT reproducible on the next run; (2) exit code 137 may have been a REPORTING artifact rather than a real kill: `task_terminate_sibling_threads` stamps SIGKILL on siblings of any exiting task, and a marked sibling's death then rewrote the task's retVal, so a normally-exiting multithreaded task reported 137. That artifact is PAID (`task_offer_exit_code`, task.c: the code is the first death's; fixture `/tests/exitcode`), so a 137 seen from now on is a real kill. Full evidence (serial capture with all three panic copies, tick timeline) was in this session; the panic DID reach the wire, so a repro is greppable. Not attributed to the same day's scheduler/IRQ0/settle changes — but not cleared of them either, because it has not recurred | Hole | M | none — first recurrence should be chased with the frozen-guest technique while it is still up | allocator.c:241 / task.c task_terminate_sibling_threads |
| **A disabled `printd` still evaluates its arguments — so a log line nobody prints can kill the machine.** `printd` is a real varargs FUNCTION (printd.h), not a macro, so the debug-level test happens INSIDE the callee: every call site pays the argument evaluation and the call regardless of level, and any argument that dereferences a pointer dereferences it even when the level is off. Known and accepted from the beginning (the cost was the point — one uniform function, no macro soup), booked 2026-08-10 when the scheduler re-entrancy hunt found the crash site at scheduler.c:1286 was a `printd` whose args walk `cls->currentThread->ownerTask` — a *silent* log line reaching through a corrupted pointer and taking the #GP that a corrupted pointer deserves, but at a line that looks inert. Cure: gate evaluation at the call site — `#define printd(lvl, ...) do { if (DEBUG_ENABLED(lvl)) printd_impl(lvl, __VA_ARGS__); } while (0)` — which also stops paying for logging the OS isn't doing. The reason it's a slice and not a one-liner: evaluation stops being guaranteed, so every call site must first be swept for arguments with side effects | Robustness | M | none — every disabled `printd` with a pointer deref in its args is a live tripwire pointed the wrong way | printd.h / serial_logging.h; scheduler.c:1281,1286 |

| **A RENDERER OPERATION IN FLIGHT CAN PAINT OVER A PANIC** (booked 2026-09-21, the general form of Codex #112 rd4, which was filed against the console-font install alone and fixed there with a handshake). A panic freezes the other cores with a maskable IPI (`mpFreezeOtherCores`, fire-and-forget) and then busts `kRendererLock`. Every renderer operation holds that lock with interrupts OFF, so a core that is mid-draw when the panic lands cannot take the freeze until it lets go — it finishes its glyph, its scroll (a ~3MB `memmove`, hundreds of microseconds) or its full blit concurrently with the panic's own `renderer_blit_full` and first lines, against a cursor position and a shadow the panic is also writing. `mpFreezeOtherCores`' comment argues a core that cannot take the IPI "was never going to overwrite anything anyway"; that is true of a WEDGED core and false of one that is merely inside the lock. The damage is cosmetic and bounded — one operation's worth of pixels, then the freeze lands — and the wire copy is unaffected, which is why this is a row and not a fix inside a fonts slice. The font install is the one drawer that could not be left to it, because what it writes is the FACE the panic draws with. Cure, when it is wanted: the install's handshake made general — a per-core "inside the renderer" mark that `renderer_bust_lock` waits out, bounded, before it takes the glass (or the freeze becomes an NMI, which interrupts-off cannot hold out) | Robustness (cosmetic) | S–M | the first panic photographed with another core's output across it | BasicRenderer.c renderer_bust_lock / smp_core.c mpFreezeOtherCores / Codex #112 rd4 |
| **Panics print raw stack RIPs, no symbolization** — os32 had real traces (`stack_trace.c`: EBP frame-chain walk + in-kernel `.symtab` scan → `module:function`, cascading process→libraries→parent→kernel); os64 regressed to a decoder-ring workflow (addr2line on the host). Design sketch, ratified in spirit 2026-07-26: (1) get the kernel's own ELF via **Limine's kernel-file request** and parse symbols **once at boot into a sorted RAM table** — the panic path may touch NO filesystem and NO kmalloc (the `/partition_info` corpse: that panic fired *because* the fs was unreadable; a trace that reads the disk deadlocks on its own cause); (2) walk the **RBP chain** (honest at `-O0`; the day we build `-O2`, `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` goes in or the chain lies), hopping the 5-QWORD interrupt frames so the trace crosses from fault back into the syscall; (3) `.symtab` name+offset is v1 — Chris leans toward **full debug info (DWARF file:line) loaded at boot** as the destination; the DWARF line program is a real interpreter, so it's a follow-on slice, not a blocker | Robustness | M | none — every future panic pays interest until this lands | panic.c / os32 `stack_trace.c` precedent |

| **PAID 2026-08-15 — the heap.** `map`/`unmap` landed 2026-07-19 and the libos64 `malloc` on top of them today (`libos64/heap.c`): boundary tags with Lea's in-use bit, one first-fit free list, pools for small + a dedicated region per allocation ≥128KB, a virgin frontier that makes `calloc` free on first touch, address-tied canaries, 0xA5 poison on free, and heap crimes that KILL (0xF12EEBAD / 0xCA9A12ED — Chris's ruling). An emptied region goes home to the kernel from the middle of the heap, which brk structurally cannot do. Proven three ways: `tools/test_heap_host.c` (124 host checks incl. a 20,000-round soak and the shrink-coalesce regression), `/tests/malloctest` on the roster (three entries — two of which PASS BY DYING), and `/proc/<pid>/heap`, whose audit identity (mapped == live+free+overhead+virgin) the kernel checks on every read. RESIDUE, booked below: the general userland mutex, the big-allocation address-space churn, and the fact that no APP allocates yet | Feature-gate | — | PAID | MALLOC.md / `libos64/heap.c` |
| ~~**Userland signal delivery.**~~ **PAID 2026-08-23/24 — PR #29, the signals arc (SIGNALS.md is the record; 21 Codex rounds).** Registration (syscall 49), three delivery paths (syscall exit, scheduler visit to a spinner, page-fault SIGSEGV), `sigreturn` (50), `OS64_INTERRUPTED` from every blocking call, SIGPIPE/SIGHUP/SIGTERM/SIGINT/SIGSEGV all catchable. The fifth customer, SIGWINCH, was paid 2026-08-25 (the resized-gterm row below; PTY.md § Resize). Original text kept below as the customer list. — `SIGPIPE`'s default action (terminate) was enforced *inside the kernel* (`raise_sigpipe_and_die`) because ring 3 cannot install a handler yet. Consequence: a program that wants to SURVIVE a vanishing reader can't — it always dies. Fix = deliver signals to ring 3 (handler + a way to return), after which `pipe_write` can hand `PIPE_ERR_CLOSED` back to a program that asked for it. **TWO MORE CUSTOMERS ARRIVED 2026-08-21**: SIGHUP and SIGTERM both default to death and neither can be caught, so a GUI app cannot say "wait — unsaved work" when its terminal hangs up or the machine goes down. Chris asked how to subscribe to SIGTERM the day it was built; the honest answer was "you can't yet, and this row is why". The shape when it lands: register a handler + a delivery trampoline (kernel pushes a frame on the user stack, redirects RIP, the handler returns through a stub that resumes the interrupted context) — os64's answer to sigaction, without POSIX's restart/mask warts. **DESIGNED 2026-08-23 — the full record is SIGNALS.md**, written before the building the way MALLOC.md and SIGINT.md were: identity becomes a NUMBER (killing signals.h's bit-value-indexing landmine), the handler moves to the TASK while the pending set stays per-thread (because the aim is already a broadcast, per-thread handlers would run one SIGTERM once per thread), the return stub is a second template in the existing `TASK_EXIT_TRAMPOLINE_VIRT` page (the stack trampoline every historic Unix used is outlawed here by NX), and an interrupted call returns INTERRUPTED rather than growing SA_RESTART and EINTR. A FIFTH customer arrived the same day: SIGWINCH has nowhere to be delivered, which is why a maximized gterm still reports 38x100 | Feature-gate | M | when a program needs to catch SIGPIPE, SIGHUP or SIGTERM | **SIGNALS.md** / `signals.h` / `syscall.c` |
| **Checkpoints conflate "will ever be caught" with "deliverable right now."** A masked-with-handler signal correctly counts as caught (so a second Ctrl+C during a handler no longer executes the program — the 2026-08-24 review fix), but the park loops and blocking-call bails use the SAME predicate, so a handler that itself BLOCKS while its own signal is pending again gets `INTERRUPTED` from every blocking call until it returns, in a hot loop. Accepted wart, named at birth (SIGNALS.md §7). **NARROWED 2026-08-25 (Codex #29 rd17), and the narrowing is the part worth knowing**: the reprieve now covers only the handler's OWN signal. `signal_has_handler_for_pending` walks every pending bit and answers "no reprieve" the moment it finds one whose default is death and which has no handler — so a stuck handler can no longer shield an unhandled SIGTERM (it could, indefinitely, until that round). What survives is exactly the hot-loop wart: a handler that blocks while ITS OWN signal is pending again. The cure is unchanged — two predicates: kill sites ask "will anything EVER catch this?", park/bail sites ask "can it deliver NOW?" — a held signal then parks normally and delivers after `sigreturn` unmasks | Cleanup | S | if a real handler ever blocks (none does today — sigdemo's is two stores) | SIGNALS.md §7 / `signal_has_handler_for_pending` |
| **Named pipes (FIFOs).** Same pipe object, same semantics — the ONLY difference is the rendezvous: a VFS name instead of inheritance across spawn. A FIFO holds no data (0 bytes on disk forever); it is just a name that resolves to a kernel pipe object, and `open()` blocks until both a reader and a writer arrive. `pipe.c` was written unaware of how it was created, precisely so this is additive. Also the first VFS node that isn't a file — the same door **procfs** walks through | Feature-gate | M | when two UNRELATED processes need to talk | `pipe.c` / VFS |

## Virtual terminals (the VT slice, 2026-08-08 — booked at birth)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A gterm's PAPER IS STORED AND IGNORED** (booked 2026-09-03, beside the row below). `ESC]11;` inside a window sets the pty's `glass_bg` and nothing looks different: gterm resolves a cell's default background against its own `GTERM_BG` constant, and the kernel never paints a pty on the glass, so the field is inert there. The room to fix it already exists — `os64_pty_header_t` carries a spare `uint32_t`, which is exactly one XRGB — so the kernel publishes the paper and gterm uses it instead of the constant. **And its sibling, which only becomes a bug the day that lands:** a pty copies the GLOBAL background at creation, so a window opened while VT1 is dark starts with VT1's paper rather than the default; a pty should be born with the default, since it is nobody's terminal but its own | Feature | S | when it annoys somebody (Chris's gate, and the honest one — the paper is a VT feature today and reads as one) | pty.h `_reserved` / gterm.c GTERM_BG / tty.c pty_create_slave |
| **A PERSON CANNOT SET THE TERMINAL'S COLOURS** (booked 2026-09-03, the day the terminal learned to obey escape sequences — Chris asked for a candy day). The kernel reads SGR and OSC 11, and the only senders are a program that writes the bytes: `$PROMPT` via husk's `\e`, or `/tests/ansiprobe`. Nothing typed at a shell can reach it, so the screen's own colour cannot be chosen and cannot survive a reboot. Three doors, and they are not alternatives so much as a stack: **(1)** a way to emit an escape from the command line — `echo -e`, or a `color`/`paper` verb (`color paper #101828` is the nicer thing to type); **(2)** a SETTING on the conf ladder so the machine simply IS that colour at boot, which is the half that stops it being a shell script; **(3)** husk's prompt vocabulary has no `\a`, so an OSC written there cannot be terminated — one line, and the reason the obvious `$PROMPT` recipe does not work. (1) and (2) are separable and (2) is the one worth having | Feature | S each | a candy day | tty.c ANSI_GLASS_BG / husk.c prompt_render / os64/ansi.h |
| **Widget plane has no backing store**: `print_at` paints raw glass, so a VT switch's repaint blows widgets away until their owner reprints (the clock self-heals in ≤1s, which is why v1 ships this way). The doctrine says widgets SURVIVE an F3; honest survival needs either a widget cell layer the repaint composes over, or a repaint-notification the owners subscribe to | Cosmetic | S–M | when a widget that repaints slower than the clock exists | syscall_numbers.h § widget doctrine / tty.c repaint |
| **No serial TTY backend**: serial is output-only (printd/logd) — no read path, no IRQ4, no tty_t bound to a UART. The tty_t object was shaped so a serial console is "just another sink + source"; building it is the day os64 gets a headless login | Feature | M | when the P5 (or the 3900X) wants headless use | tty.h layering note / serial.c |
| **No job control**: Ctrl+Z/SIGTSTP/`fg`/`bg` don't exist; per-tty fgTask made them POSSIBLE (the hard prerequisite is paid), but stop/continue semantics, husk verbs, and SIGCONT delivery are their own slice. **A SECOND CUSTOMER ARRIVED 2026-08-22**: the debugger. `/proc/<id>/mem` shipped read-only; its Plan 9 siblings `regs` (the saved frame — meaningful only while the task is STOPPED) and `ctl stop`/`start` are the next slice, and `stop`/`start` IS SIGSTOP/SIGCONT under its better name. Chris is sold; build them together | Feature | M | the debugger (regs + ctl stop/start), and Chris wanting to park an editor | signals.h SIGSTOP/SIGCONT stubs / procfs.c mem |
| **`/proc/<id>/mem` v1 reads only PRESENT pages** — a page the task owns (in a VMA) but has never touched has no PTE yet, so `maps` says it exists and `mem` says `??`. v2 populates on the reader's behalf (fault the page in through the task's VMA, then copy) — Chris: "may happen quickly depending on whether ?? is annoying". Also: the write path, gated on a debugger needing an int3, must document that an HHDM write ignores the task's PTE protections | Feature | S | the first annoyed `??`, or the first breakpoint | procfs.c proc_mem_read |
| ~~**GUI console window is not a tty sink**~~ **PAID 2026-08-19 by the VT8 chapter — and not the way this row imagined.** The row proposed teaching `kConsoleSink` about ttys: bind tty N's grid as a window surface so VT switching and window focus became one idea. What shipped deleted the sink outright — glass ownership IS VT focus, the compositor is VT8's seated shell, and the console window retired alongside the diversion (ruled: no ring-0-rendered windows at all). The unification the row wanted did happen; it arrived as "the GUI is a terminal like any other" rather than "a terminal is a window like any other", and the shell-in-a-window half became Phase E's `gterm`, in ring 3 where it always belonged. Row struck during the resize slice, having gone false the night before | Feature | — | PAID | GRAPHICS.md VT8 chapter / tty.c + BasicRenderer.c headstones |
| **The text-console mouse needs a GUI boot**: `vt_select.c` gives a text VT selection, copy and paste — but only when `GUI` is on the cmdline, and Chris met this the day it shipped (2026-08-21, and called it "not an issue"). TWO reasons, both in the plumbing: `mouse_init()` + IRQ12 adoption sit inside `if (kEnableGUI)` in kernel.c, so a text-mode boot never brings the AUX port up at all; and the input ring's only drainer is the compositor thread, which does not exist without the GUI. The fix is correspondingly two-part: bring the mouse up unconditionally (or behind its own flag), and give the ring a second drainer — `tty_flush_if_dirty`'s ~30Hz rider is the natural home, since it already runs on every boot for exactly this cadence | Feature | S–M | when someone wants a mouse on a GUI-less boot | kernel.c § input IRQ adoption / vt_select.h |
| **No ETXTBSY**: nothing stops a program opening a RUNNING executable for write and truncating it in place. os64 demand-pages text from the inode, so the running process would fault in a mix of old and new bytes and die confused — Unix added ETXTBSY in V7 for precisely this. Not currently reachable in practice: `os64get` publishes `/bin` files on ext2 through managed scratch + verify + atomic rename (a NEW inode; the running copy keeps the one it holds, via the ext2 orphan chain), which is the idiom every installer here uses. The debt is that the SAFETY lives in the tool's manners rather than in the kernel's refusal | Robustness | S–M | when a second tool learns to write into /bin | os64get.c § publishing / elf_loader.c |
| **os64get batch installation and replacement backups.** `-a` routes the manifest, skips matching files, and stages downloads in managed scratch directories on their destination filesystems. It verifies backups of replaced originals before publishing the batch. Preparation errors and Ctrl+C remove incoming scratch files without installing the batch; finalized original backups remain. Ctrl+C is deferred once publication starts. URL downloads share staging and cleanup but do not make replacement backups or consult the install configuration. | Feature (DONE) | — | done | os64get.c / install.c / OS64GET_ARCHIVE_PLAN.md |
| **A multi-file refresh is not atomic.** Publication uses per-file same-filesystem renames. Ctrl+C is deferred through this phase, but a crash, forced termination, or failed rename can leave a mixed installation. Verified original backups support manual recovery; they do not make a filesystem transaction. Closing this gap requires directory publication or a recovery journal. | Robustness | M | if torn publication needs automatic recovery | os64get.c `publish_run` / install.c `install_commit` |
| **os64get's HTTP half has no unchanged check.** The valet's `local_matches` declines a transfer before it starts by comparing the file on disk against the length and CRC the header names; a URL fetch re-downloads unconditionally, because HTTP offers no checksum and the parser keeps no `Last-Modified` or `ETag`. HTTP's own answer is conditional requests — `If-Modified-Since` / `If-None-Match` and a 304 — which is the same idea the valet's check already implements, so the shape is known and the asymmetry is the debt. Wants a consumer first: a person re-fetching one page pays nothing, a script polling one does. Booked 2026-09-02 with the URL slice | Feature | S | when anything fetches the same URL twice on purpose | os64get.c `fetch_url` / http.c `http_response_t` |
| **A length-less identity-coded HTTP response can fill the disk.** With no `Content-Length` the close is the length (RFC 1945's framing), so os64get reads an identity body until the peer hangs up with no cap on how much lands in managed scratch. The backstop is the write failing when the filesystem is full, which triggers temporary-file cleanup and leaves the previous file untouched — correct, but late. gzip no longer shares this debt: increment 3(d) caps a chunked or close-framed decoded body at 16 MiB, and a length-framed one at 100x wire size within a 1–16 MiB band. A general `--max-size` (curl's shape) or default identity ceiling would refuse the remaining case earlier and say why. Booked 2026-09-02 with the URL slice; narrowed when gzip arrived 2026-09-03 | Robustness | XS | when a length-less identity fetch actually costs a disk | os64get.c `fetch_url` body loop |
| **The URL parser refuses more of the grammar than a browser does**, each on purpose and each cheap to relax if a real address ever needs it: an IPv6 literal in brackets (the stack is v4), `user:pass@host` (os64 has no HTTP auth and a URL is a poor place to keep a password), and a space or control byte in the path (percent-encoding what a person typed is a guess about their intent, and a raw space in a request line is the request-splitting shape). Every one is refused BY NAME, so the diagnostic already says which. Booked 2026-09-02 so the refusals are on the record as decisions rather than omissions | Feature | XS | when a real URL is turned away | libos64/url.c `os64_url_parse` |
| **Boot does not clean temporary downloads.** os64get removes its run-owned scratch files on handled failures and Ctrl+C. Crashes can leave directories under `/tmp/os64get` or `<mount>/.os64get-tmp`; they require manual removal. Future boot cleanup must cover these locations on mounted filesystems and handle mounts that arrive later. Legacy scattered `.part` files lack ownership metadata and must not be swept by an unrestricted glob. | Cleanup | S | boot-time scratch cleanup | OS64GET_ARCHIVE_PLAN.md / install.c |
| **logd still carries its own copy of the `key = value` reader.** `os64_conf_read` (libos64/conf.c) was lifted from `logd_conf_value` on 2026-08-22 when os64get became the dialect's second consumer; logd was left on its private copy to keep that slice honest in size. Move it over (the callback shape was designed so logd's complaint channel survives: `key == NULL` hands it the bad line) and delete the duplicate — two parsers of one dialect is how the dialect forks | Cleanup | XS | next logd touch | logd.c logd_conf_value / os64/conf.h |
| **Pre-tty boot lines are pixels-only archaeology**: the ~6 lines printed before tty_init (memory map, paging, allocator, kernel stack) exist on glass but not in VT1's grid — the first switch away and back loses them. Deliberate (tty_init runs at the earliest possible moment, right after the shadow attaches; the lines are also all in the serial log). Recorded so nobody hunts a "missing lines" bug | Cosmetic (accepted) | — | none (fix would be scraping the shadow's pixels back into cells, which is absurd) | tty_init takeover comment |
| **The boot face is PSF1, so the lines before the root mounts are eight pixels wide on any screen** (booked 2026-09-22, the day `vtfont --startup` made every LATER line the person's choice). The Limine font module goes through `init_renderer(struct PSF1_FONT *)`, and PSF1 fixes the width at 8 by format — on a 1920x1080 panel the banner, the memory map and the mount lines are 240 columns of 8x16 that scroll past unread, and the panic face (the boot face, by design) is the same. The kernel already has a PSF2 loader (`psf2.c`) and a face-generic renderer (slice 1); what is missing is the module path using them: parse the module with `psf2_parse` (keeping PSF1 for the zap files that ship), build the boot face from it, and let `charset_verify` derive its table from whatever loaded. Then `module_path: boot():/boot/Uni2-Terminus32x16.psf` in limine-hd.conf is a readable boot from the first line, and a readable panic — which is the one screen no ring-3 program can ever fix | Feature | S–M | when the P5's early boot lines are wanted readable, or the first panic photographed at 8x16 on 1080p | BasicRenderer.c init_renderer / video.c / CONSOLE_FONTS.md § Persistence |

## Performance / fairness / cleanup

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Batch TLB shootdowns during populated-region release.** `syscall_unmap` frees demand-paged regions one 4 KiB allocation at a time; each HHDM removal broadcasts to the other cores while the frame lock is held, and userland `free` holds the process heap lock across unmap. Measured 2026-10-10: releasing 64 MiB took 5.86 s on eight-core QEMU versus 0.157 s on one core; six workers took 88.5 s versus 6.4 s. Seen again on the P5 2026-10-17: yonder's main thread spent ~20% of a core in `unmap`, ~300 calls a second at ~660 µs each, for paint buffers of 128 KB to ~500 KB (32 to ~125 pages each). yonder stopped freeing them (YONDER_PERF.md § perf-5), but any program that frees big blocks pays this. Coalesce notifications in bounded batches, preserving HHDM liveness and signal-frame protection and defining remote completion/frame-reuse ordering. This pattern predates the frame-table allocator; per-page descriptors need not change. | Performance | M | Measured gate met; next kernel memory-performance slice. Validate with `memphase`, SMP navigation/close stress, and P5 timings before closing acceptance. | [Profile, reproducer, and repair constraints](docs/webp-evidence/memory-profile.md#repair-direction-and-remaining-work) |
| **`wait()` SPINS, and ~39 callers read as if it sleeps.** `waitTicks` burns a core on `pause` until the global tick arrives — right before there is a scheduler, and free for years because everything using it ran when nothing else wanted the core. It stopped being free the day a caller ran beside userland: the LATE test phase held a core at 100% for its whole run (Chris, 2026-08-29, watching `top`). `nap()` (time.c) is the sleeping twin — same tick cadence, parks via SIGSLEEP, falls back to the spin where no scheduler exists — and the LATE-phase loops plus `tcp_conn_dial`'s handshake poll now use it. THE REST HAVE NOT BEEN AUDITED, and a sweep is exactly the wrong shape: `nap()` is unsafe while holding a spinlock and unsafe in interrupt context, so each call site needs reading, not replacing. Worth doing where a caller can wait a long time on a live machine (the device-init paths are the interesting ones — most run pre-scheduler and are already correct by the fallback). The tell that this is worth someone's afternoon: tcp's poll loop had a comment saying "short naps" above a call that spun, so the belief is already in the tree | Performance | M | when a spinning waiter is caught costing a core on a machine somebody is using | `time.c` wait/nap; `grep -n '\bwait('` across kernel/src |
| **PAID 2026-08-06 — the buffer cache (block_cache.c).** The old row: one NVMe command per fs block, 30MB ≈ 10s. The cache's 64KB line fills ARE the coalescing (one command per 64KB cold, zero warm — 46MB wc: warm pass ≈ 2s, memcpy-bound), and both filesystems plus the partition scanner ride through the interposed bops with zero call-site changes. RESIDUE, kept honest: `ext2_bmap` still kmallocs scratch and re-asks for indirect blocks per data block — the cache absorbs the DISK cost of that (indirect lines stay hot) but the CPU churn remains; a per-handle indirect-block cache in ext2_handle_t retires it if it ever shows in a profile | Perf | S (residue) | if ext2 CPU time ever matters | the grep autopsy → the caching arc, 8/6 |
| ~~**`kmalloc_dma` guarantees nothing about WHERE.**~~ **THE IDENTITY TOOTH IS PAID, 2026-08-19** — kmalloc_dma now returns the HHDM pointer and hands the physical address back separately (the fix this row prescribed, and the doctrine e1000 refused kmalloc_dma over — its ring code is now the acknowledged original of the style). The SAME conversion retires the row's third tooth, grown on the rtl8125 branch 2026-08-16 (the kworker burial-close #PF: the identity mapping lived at a lower-half VA in kKernelPML4 ONLY, so a DMA buffer was dereferenceable from exactly one address space, and a file close doing disk I/O from a task's CR3 faulted on `dmaWriteBuffer`) — the HHDM alias is valid under EVERY CR3, so that class is structurally gone; the convention it enforced ("every file-I/O path runs in kernel context") outlived it as doctrine until 2026-09-06, when the NVMe register window — the last lower-half dependency on the disk path — moved to its HHDM alias too, after a doorbell rung under a task's CR3 on a block-cache miss killed the P5 (CLAUDE.md's ext2 section carries what the wrappers still buy). What the conversion also killed, found during the payment: identity mappings were NEVER UNMAPPED on free, so every per-I/O NVMe PRP list left a stale writable PCD mapping over a recycled physical page — a standing hole through the lazy-HHDM use-after-free tripwire, ~1,100 new holes/sec under a logd-on-ext2 soak, plus the paging-pool churn that found the intermediate-table CAS race. All callers converted (NVMe queues/identify/bounce-buffers/PRP lists, AHCI read buffer); the PCD cache-disable was deliberately not recreated (x86 DMA is coherent; e1000 proved cacheable rings on QEMU/VBox/P5 since 8/06); `kmalloc_dma32_address` (zero callers) buried in the same grave. **THE REACHABILITY TOOTH SURVIVES, narrower**: there is still no address ceiling and no device-reachability contract — today's devices are 64-bit-DMA capable, and the first 32-bit-DMA device needs a designed bounded-phys allocator, not the buried fossil | Hole | S | before any 32-bit-DMA device | Chris's allocator experiment 2026-08-05; the HHDM conversion 8/19 |
| ~~**PCI config space trusts an MCFG base that may be ZERO.**~~ **THE REFUSAL IS PAID 2026-10-19** (the ACPI rewrite, acpi_tables.c): `init_PCI` panics by name when the MCFG described no segment-0 window, instead of walking VA 0x1000 into a triple fault (the i440FX autopsy is in VERIFICATION.md's headless section, 2026-08-18), and the bus scan covers exactly the buses the MCFG names. Of the two companion findings that rode this row, the page-0 unmap was paid by PR #70. **STILL OPEN:** the faulting core's GS base was 0 at CPL0 inside `readPCIRegister` after 15 clean timer ticks — if any kernel path runs with user GS live, every exception on it is a triple fault, and that wants an owner | Robustness | S | the GS question: needs Chris's eyes | the i440FX detour, 8/18 |
| **Growable log rings.** The per-core rings are a fixed 16MB each, allocated once at `logging_queueing_init`. Designed and then DELIBERATELY NOT BUILT (2026-08-18): growth was conceived when a full ring was fatal, and it no longer is — the ring-full path now overwrites oldest and counts it, and a serial-less machine retains instead of shredding, so the two hazards growth was meant to prevent are both closed. Evidence against building it yet: 3% of one ring after a boot with DEBUG_SCHEDULER|DETAILED, `lost` = 0. **The trigger is `/sys/log`** — build it when `used` runs high or `lost` goes non-zero under a real workload. WHEN it is built: grow in the CONSUMER, never in `log_store_entry` (which runs in interrupt context and inside fault handlers, and must never allocate); cap it from the cmdline; and RE-LINEARIZE rather than realloc — a ring's `head`/`tail` are indices whose meaning changes with `capacity`, so a byte-faithful `krealloc` would hand back a buffer that looks intact and is scrambled. Giving memory back later is a separate, trickier slice (shrink after sustained low water) | Perf | M | when /sys/log says so | the logging arc, 8/18; Chris's growth ask |
| **A `tail -f` that holds ONE handle.** `ext2_handle_t` snapshots the inode AND `size` at open, so a reader holding a handle can never see the file grow — `read()` stops at the size it captured and returns 0 forever. tail therefore has to RE-OPEN every poll to observe an appending log, which works (and is what makes tailing correct on ext2, where opens are not exclusive) but is not what `tail -f` actually is: one handle, streaming past EOF. The fix is for the ext2 read path to consult the LIVE `i_size` rather than the snapshot — cheap, and it would also let a reader notice truncation instead of reading into a hole. Booked 2026-08-18 after the evening this cost: on FAT the re-open was being REFUSED (`FF_FS_LOCK` makes a write-open exclusive), which starved tail, and when tail won instead it starved logd until logd gave up and released the log sink. Both symptoms were FAT-only — /home is ext2 now on the P5 AND in QEMU — but the snapshot-at-open behavior outlives them. **Chris's, for a quiet day.** | Feature-gate | S | when someone wants a real `tail -f` | ext2_internal.h `ext2_handle_t` / the tail-vs-logd evening, 8/18 |
| **FAT backward seek is O(position): `FF_USE_FASTSEEK=0`.** Every backward f_lseek rewinds to the file's FIRST cluster and walks the chain to the target — readline's seek-back gait went O(n²) on the 46MB log (4,320 lines/60s, decelerating; the grep autopsy 2026-08-06). os64_linereader cured the LINE-READING case userland-side (forward-only, zero seeks); this row is everyone else — any seek-heavy access to a big FAT file pays it still. Fix: FF_USE_FASTSEEK=1 + a per-FIL cluster link map (cltbl, allocated at open for files past a size threshold) | Perf | S–M | the next seek-heavy consumer of big FAT files (a streaming less would qualify) | ffconf.h line 37 / the grep autopsy |
| **NVMe PRP-LIST path: now LOAD-BEARING (2026-08-06)** — every block-cache line fill is a 128-sector (64KB = 16-page) command, so the once-never-run `prpCount > 2` branch executes hundreds of times per boot and the whole 24+34 suite rides on it (green first boot). Remaining diligence: a deliberate edge test (runs straddling the 2-page and MDTS boundaries, unaligned starts) has still never run ON PURPOSE — cheap insurance now that the branch carries the OS | Robustness | XS | next test-writing pass | the envelope autopsy 8/5; baptized by the cache 8/6 |
| **Caching arc phase 2** (each its own ruling, per the 8/6 phase split): memory-pressure eviction + the os64_memory_t `reclaimable` seat (drop-on-demand must be REAL before `available` counts cache bytes — the allocator discovers pressure holding its irqsave lock, so the eviction hook needs its own design conversation); write caching (deferred: no consumers; the 46MB-copy disk-full adventure = first wild consumer sighted); async readahead (wants completion infrastructure — the NVMe-interrupts era). Update-in-place on write was PROMOTED OUT of this row the night it was written: the first measured ext2 copy showed invalidate-and-refill reading 1.2GB to copy 1.5MB | Feature-gate | M–L | when a consumer demands it, ruling by ruling | block_cache.h phase-2 block |
| **NVMe queue depth: one command in flight, ever** (nvme_do_io submit→spin→reap under ioLock — the 64-deep submission queue stands empty). Evidence from the 8/6 iostat histogram: the ext2 copy's 29,760 writes were 2-block commands paying full serialized round trips each. Fix = pipelined multi-command submission WITHIN a request (submit K, reap all before returning — durability preserved, NOT write caching). Re-measure after cp (1MB buffer, Chris's) + update-in-place land: both may shrink the command count enough to demote this | Perf | M | re-measure first; then the next write-heavy sufferer | the iostat confession, 8/6 late |
| **`top` defeats parking: settle-on-read wakes EVERY core.** `mpAcctSettleAll` (the /proc CPU-time render) IPIs all cores so each settles its in-flight span on its own TSC — correct books, but a PARKED core gets woken just to report "nothing in flight," so `top` shows "0 parked" on an idle machine. Observer effect: the measurement destroys the state it reports (spotted by Chris on the 2-hour VBox e1000 soak, 2026-08-06, idle high-90s%). Fix sketch: settle the books AT PARK TIME — the pass that sends a core into `hlt` charges its in-flight span first (one TSC read, on the core itself, so the no-cross-core-TSC-math law holds); the reader then skips cores sitting in their idle thread and counts them parked. Bonus: parked count becomes an honest first-class stat instead of a self-defeating one. The fuller cure, which also ends top's torn 99%/101% readings, is one coherent snapshot read with no IPI at all: `docs/cpu_accounting_snapshot_plan.md` | Perf | S | when top's parked count should read true (it just started to matter) | smp_core.c settle-on-read / procfs.c / the tickless default |
| AP sibling starvation — no intra-pass timeslicing under a CPU-bound thread | Robustness | L | when it bites | SCHEDULER #2 / GRAPHICS #10 |
| Move scheduler printds OUTSIDE `kSchedulerSwitchTasksLock` (the convoy: DETAILED must be slow-but-honest, never a lockup) | Robustness | M | none — Chris's "bend to my will" requirement | SCHEDULER #4 / autopsy |
| **Wall-clock hardening — HALF paid 2026-08-10; the IRQ0 half was TRIED AND REVERTED THE SAME DAY.** The prologue reorder (guard before the `temp_rsp` store) shipped and stays. Promoting IRQ0 above scheduler priority (0x20 class 2 → 0xE0 class 14) shipped, froze VBox at boot, and was reverted hours later — full postmortem in the IRQ0_APIC_VECTOR headstone in smp_core.h. Short version: QEMU was perfect (24+19 green, INTx confirmed on GSI 20); VBox's e1000 probe reported EVERY candidate GSI silent and the OS wedged at scheduler start, and a DIRECTLOG boot showed `kTicksSinceStart` climbing to 560 and then all nine probe lines carrying the SAME timestamp, 597, across ~4.5 seconds of wall clock. The clock did not slow, it STOPPED, and every other interrupt with it — the signature of a stuck LAPIC ISR bit, which at class 14 pins PPR and blocks the NIC (class 4), the scheduler (class 7), and IRQ0 itself. The promotion almost certainly did not CREATE that bug; it made an old one lethal, because a stuck ISR bit at class 2 blocks almost nothing and hides indefinitely. DO NOT re-promote the vector: nothing high enough to outrank class 7 can avoid outranking devices at class 4, so the hazard is inherent to the approach, and cure (b) below retires the question entirely. **The tick loss is therefore UNPAID**, with a measured floor: ~6s lost over 290s of periodic-mode uptime under load (top + 4 hogs), and `top`'s clock visibly stepping 2 seconds at a time. Beyond the scheduler-pass losses, the remainder is to **IF=0 windows, where the LAPIC is not arbitrating at all** — dominated by SFMASK clearing IF for the ENTIRE duration of every syscall (smp_core.c ~461), plus the allocator's interrupts-disabled spinlock, plus host vCPU descheduling under WSL2 that no guest-side change can reach. Note the shape: a SHORT window only DELAYS a tick (the IRR holds it, it fires on the way out); only a window longer than one 10ms tick period destroys one, because the IRR holds exactly ONE pending instance per vector. So ~2% loss ≈ two >10ms IF=0 windows per second. TWO CURES, and the cheap one is a patch: (a) lost-tick compensation — `handler_irq0_asm` compares TSC against the last tick and credits the ticks it can prove went missing (Linux carried exactly this for years; S, honest clock, symptom-level); (b) the right answer — stop COUNTING interrupts and start READING a counter: derive wall-clock from the TSC (accounting already uses it) or the HPET, leaving IRQ0 to drive only scheduling cadence, at which point a delayed interrupt costs nothing (M–L, and the same instinct as tickless). The constant ~4s boot offset is a SEPARATE row below (RTC resync, declined: "steady is fine") | Robustness | (a) S / (b) M–L | none — and (b) is now the PREFERRED route, not merely the tidier one: (a) leaves the stuck-ISR hazard undiagnosed and the priority question open. `micros()` (syscall 59, 2026-09-27) is the counter's first ring-3 door — a stopwatch that reads the TSC and loses nothing — and the shape (b) grows from; `kTicksSinceStart` itself still counts interrupts | smp_core.h IRQ0_APIC_VECTOR headstone / SCHEDULER #7 / autopsy |
| `kSchedulerSwitchTasksLock`: irqsave + bounded-spin panic tripwire (send_ipi ICR pattern) — also the VBox-freeze hunt instrument | Robustness | S | none | SCHEDULER #8-9 |
| **logd file sink** — at root-mount, logd drains to a file (ramdisk = memcpy-speed, kills steady-state queue creep; NVMe boots get persistent logs). Serial stays for panics/BOOTMARK/early-boot + optional tee. PRECONDITION: FatFs write support is UNVERIFIED ("the FAT library has never written anything" — check FF_FS_READONLY, then a create/write/readback post-boot test BEFORE wiring the sink) | Robustness | M | after hello-userland (Chris's ordering) | log.c / never-drop rule / 2026-07-11 plan |
| ring-3 klogd (userland log daemon reading kernel log via syscall) | Feature-gate | M | post-shell — needs read-from-kernel-log plumbing | LIBOS64/ABI roadmap |
| Resync `kSystemCurrentTime` from the RTC at end of kernel_init — boot tick-starvation currently leaves the OS clock ~3-4s behind forever (Chris declined for now: "steady is fine"; one-liner when wanted) | Cleanup | XS | when the lag annoys | kernel.c / 2026-07-11 |
| `kCPUCyclesPerSecond` miscalibrated ~10× under QEMU/TCG — trust cycle counts, fix the calibration | Cleanup | S | before anything times itself with it | kernel.c tscGetCyclesPerSecond |
| Uncomment the divide-config write in `ap_configure_scheduler_timer` — arming currently depends on the divisor leftover from calibration | Cleanup | XS | none | smp_core.c |
