| Allocator first-fit linear scans (merge scans whole ledger; compaction every 10th free) | Cleanup | L | measure before caring | MEMORY #2 |
| IRQ-safe sub-tick wake primitive | Feature-gate | M | only if a real need appears (100Hz covers the GUI) | SCHEDULER #3 |
| Pipe waiter slots are SINGLE (one parked reader + one parked writer per pipe). A second waiter on the same end is still correct — it falls back on its ~1s SIGSLEEP backstop and re-checks — but it is slow. Fix = a real wait list per end | Cleanup | S | when >1 reader or >1 writer on one pipe becomes normal | `pipe.c` |
| Console scroll runs with **interrupts off**: the renderer spinlock is held across a ~3MB full-screen `memmove`, so a scroll can delay that core's tick by a few hundred µs. Correctness beats the jitter (the console is not a hot path), but if it ever matters the fix is a scroll that does NOT hold the lock — not a lock that does not cover the scroll | Cleanup | S | if the console ever gets hot | `BasicRenderer.c` kRendererLock |
| ~~`handle_alloc`/`handle_close` take no lock~~ **PAID 2026-08-25 (PR #29).** The gate this row named — "the day handles are shared between threads of one task" — had already passed when ring-3 threads landed, which is how the two-closers race (Codex #29 rd4) and the two-allocators race (Fable's rd-review) both got in. There is still no lock: the slot's TYPE is an atomic claim token. `handle_close` exchanges live→`HANDLE_CLOSING`, `handle_alloc` CASes `NONE`→`CLOSING` before publishing the real type, and neither touches a slot it did not win. A LOCK is the fallback if this table ever grows an operation that cannot be expressed as one atomic on the type | Cleanup (PAID) | — | done | `handle.c` |

## Threads (os64's first ring-3 threads, 2026-08-02)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A stack's guard pages are backed by physical frames nobody can touch.** `task_alloc_guarded_stack` allocates one run covering the usable stack AND the guard on each side, then maps only the middle — so the 4 guard pages at each end (8 frames, 32KB per thread, user and kernel stack alike) hold allocated RAM that is never mapped anywhere. A guard only needs to be unmapped VIRTUAL space; the frames could be left in the allocator. Cheap to fix, invisible until a machine runs thousands of threads. Noticed 2026-10-30 while giving a guard fault its own headline (`stack_guard_side`) | Cleanup | S | a quiet day | `task.c` task_alloc_guarded_stack |
| **No PER-THREAD teardown while the task lives.** *(Scope clarified 2026-08-15 — this row is NOT about task memory, which is fully reclaimed: `task_destroy` frees every thread's stacks and `thread_t` at burial, and the VMA reclaim closed the last residual the same week.)* What is still missing is reclaiming a thread that exits while its TASK KEEPS RUNNING: it becomes a ZOMBIE holding ~1MB of user stack + 64KB of kernel stack + its `thread_t` until the whole program exits. Blast radius today is ZERO — every threaded program starts N, joins them, and exits — and it only bites a program that creates threads in a LOOP, which nothing does yet. `thread_join.c:105` still carries the matching comment | Robustness | M | when any program creates threads repeatedly rather than once | `thread_join.c`, `syscall_thread_exit` |
| **No general locks or atomics for PROGRAMS** — deliberate v1 (Chris's ruling): a mutex designed before its first consumer is the wrong mutex. *(2026-08-15: the LIBRARY now has one — `libos64/heap.c` carries a test-and-set + `pause` + `os64_yield` lock, because threads share one address space and therefore one heap, making malloc the first genuine consumer of shared mutable state. It is deliberately private to the heap: a dozens-of-instructions critical section is the wrong customer to design a general mutex around, but it IS the first real datum about what one should cost.)* | Feature-gate | M | the first PROGRAM with genuinely shared mutable state | `os64/thread.h`, `libos64/heap.c` |
| `thread_join_create` leaks the `thread_t` if the stack-mapping check fails (the only failure path after createThread). Cannot currently happen — the stack was just mapped — and fixing it properly needs the thread teardown above | Cleanup | XS | with thread teardown | `thread_join.c` |
| No thread affinity control from ring 3: a program cannot say "put this one on its own core." The scheduler's own delegation handles the useful case today | Feature-gate | S | when a program has a reason to care | `syscall_thread` |
| ~~**User-thread stack size is fixed at 1 MiB.**~~ **PAID for a main thread 2026-10-31** (SCRIPT_STACK.md): a program declares its main thread's stack in PT_GNU_STACK (`ld -z stack-size`), the kernel gives it up to 16 MiB, and yonder takes 4 MiB, its engine 2 MiB (depth 3,040, at least 1,026,399 bytes of headroom under the deepest layout). Still open: a spawned thread's size (no caller yet), and demand-paged stacks (SCRIPT_STACK.md § 4 B). The original row: QuickJS must reserve headroom for host/native calls. J2's standalone 256 KiB profile retains about 702 KiB sampled headroom with a bounded 64 KiB native frame; the 768 KiB runner cap retains about 190 KiB in that case. D10 measures combined script/layout on the same stack with a 128 KiB browser engine cap: the tested near-bound block chain retains 190367 sampled bytes. Arbitrary native callbacks and future bindings still need their own measurements. A configurable thread-stack API is deferred, not authorized kernel work | Feature-gate | TBD after browser measurement | measured useful workloads cannot fit with safe native-call headroom | `kernel/include/thread.h`; [JavaScript runtime plan](docs/design/pending/JAVASCRIPT.md#resource-limits); [J2 evidence](userland/libjs/VALIDATION.md#j2-standalone-profile-and-scheduling-2026-10-02); [D10 evidence](docs/design/pending/DOM_D10.md#guest-stack-evidence) |

## Floating point (the FPU slice, 2026-08-27 — booked the day it was reviewed)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **No XSAVE, so no AVX.** The register file is switched as one 512-byte FXSAVE image; `CR4.OSXSAVE` stays clear, so any AVX/AVX2/AVX-512 instruction is `#UD` at ring 3 (which now kills the program, not the machine). Consistent and safe — the YMM upper halves cannot be live if nothing can execute an instruction that reaches them — but it means `-mavx` in any userland build is a crash, and the compiler must never be told the CPU has it. The cure is XSAVE with the variable-size image `CPUID.0xD` reports, a per-boot size instead of a fixed 512 in `thread_t` and both signal frames, and `xsaveopt`/init-tracking if the copy cost ever shows | Feature-gate | M | the first consumer that wants AVX (none in sight — `-msse2` is the baseline every app builds against) | `fpu.h`, `fpu.c` |
| **A CPU exception from ring 3 is a kill, not a catchable signal.** `#DE`, `#UD`, `#GP`, `#MF`, `#XM`, `#AC` end the task with exit `200 + vector` (`user_exception_kill`). Unix answers these with SIGFPE/SIGILL/SIGBUS, and a handler CAN legitimately want them (a calculator recovering from 1/0; a JIT probing instruction support). os64 has no such signals, and adding them to the table before a consumer asks is exactly the speculative growth the roadmap forbids. When one asks: the §9 path (`signal_deliver_segv`) already resumes a caught fault into its handler out of the exception frame, and these would ride it | Feature-gate | S–M | the first program that wants to survive its own FP or illegal-instruction fault | `simple_exceptions.c`, SIGNALS.md §9 |

## Heap (the userland malloc, 2026-08-15 — booked the day it was written)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Two threads faulting on the SAME page each resolve it, and the loser's page LEAKS.** The static demand-paging path is "resolve backing page → map it" with nothing serializing the pair, so concurrent faults on one address both `kmalloc_aligned` a frame, both fill it identically, and both map it — the second `paging_map_page` overwrites the first's PTE, and the orphaned frame is now reachable from no page table, which means the burial-time VMA reclaim (which walks the tables) can never free it. Harmless to correctness (the contents are identical), and *rare* — it needs two threads to fault the same page in the same instant — but it is a genuine leak in a kernel that just finished paying off every other one. Found 2026-08-15 while fixing the seek/read race next door; the shared-object path already dodges it with a CAS'd per-page slot (`shared_object_resolve_page`), which is the model for the fix | Cleanup (leak) | S | when a threaded program's first-touch storm shows up in the allocator's numbers | `simple_exceptions.c` handle_page_fault static branch / `shared_object.c` CAS precedent |
| **No APP allocates yet.** The heap ships with no consumer but its own fixture: every utility still carves fixed buffers or maps a whole region per object (`linereader` 64KB per open, `tail` 32KB chunks, `file_pager` the entire file). Converting them is not mechanical — each one gets to become a better program, which is why it is Chris's joy and not a sweep | Cleanup | S each | as each utility earns it | `libos64/heap.c` callers |
| **A big-allocation loop burns address space.** Every allocation ≥128KB takes its own region and gives it back on free, and region VAs are NEVER reused (the deliberate stale-pointer tripwire). A program looping malloc(128KB)/free would exhaust the 47-bit heap range after ~1 billion iterations. Not a today problem — glibc fights the same churn with a dynamic mmap threshold, and the same trick applies here (keep the last freed dedicated region cached for reuse) if a real program ever shows it | Cleanup | S | if a program's profile ever shows region churn | `heap.c` `HEAP_BIG_BYTES` |
| **First-fit is O(free blocks), and unresolved `largest_free` replacements still rescan.** Both are the price of the ruling that a single free list beats size-class bins for this OS (MALLOC.md rule 3). **THE GATE WAS REACHED 2026-10-28, measured on the P5:** yonder laying out the project's own pull-request page held 11,740 free blocks at 99% fragmentation — small leftovers first-fit keeps at the front, none able to serve a 4 KB request — and every allocation bigger than them walked all of them before carving. The layout's ~2,700 reallocs a second (a parse scratch per CSS rule, the row under § Browser layout, paid 2026-10-30) cost ~370 µs each in that walk alone: 8 of the page's 11.7 s "laid out in", pure user time on a full-speed core, and 7% of a core after the page was up (245 mallocs a second for the animation ticks, each walking the same list). The same page lays out in ~2 s on the host under glibc, which has had Lea's bins since 1987. Bins index the free blocks by size class so a request meets only the lists that can hold it; the give-back and canary invariants stay as they are. **2026-11-01 maximum-bookkeeping follow-up:** a maximum pointer and conservative bound certify replacement maxima within a transaction, removing most rescans in the Phoria point-light replay while preserving exact reporting. A 64 KiB maximum at the list head with a 60 KiB runner-up still scans on each 8 KiB allocation/free pair: the split remainder falls below the bound, then free restores the maximum. [HEAP_BINS.md Part B](docs/design/pending/HEAP_BINS.md#3-part-b--size-class-bins-in-the-heap-libos64-heapc-size-m) remains planned and limits that rescan to the highest occupied bin. A runner-up pointer with invalidation on removal is another possible optimization, deferred to avoid adding a further pointer-lifetime invariant here. See [maximum bookkeeping](docs/design/pending/HEAP_MAXIMUM_REVIEW.md) | Performance | M | reached: a page with tens of thousands of CSS rules on the P5 | `heap.c` `free_list_first_fit`, `heap_alloc`, `os64_realloc`'s move path |
| **`realloc` of a DEDICATED block always copies when it grows.** The region could be remapped in place if the kernel grew a `remap`, but it has no such verb and no consumer has asked. Shrinking a dedicated block is free (the region already covers it) | Cleanup | S | when a program reallocs big blocks in a loop | `heap.c` `os64_realloc` |
| **`HEAPCHECK=1` verifies every canary on every call — and nothing measures how slow that is.** It is the bug-hunt gear (MALLOC_CHECK done honestly), not a default; worth a number next to it so a future hunter knows what they are buying | Cleanup | XS | next time it gets used in anger | `heap.c` `gCheckAlways` |

## Kernel structure

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Thread kernel stacks belong in kernel space — retire the per-core scratch stack.** The stack poisoner (2026-08-12: two contexts colliding on one per-core "kernel_interrupt_stack") existed only because thread RSP0 stacks live at task-local lower-half VAs invisible to kKernelPML4, forcing every CR3-crossing path (task_exit's last breath, call_in_kernel_context's file/dir closes and shared-object page reads) onto a borrowed, bolted-to-the-core slab. Moving RSP0 stacks to kmalloc-backed upper-half memory (valid under EVERY CR3, use-after-free tripwired by the lazy HHDM like everything else) retires the slab, the cikc stack-switch, AND the whole "no sleeping on a borrowed stack" rule — a context would always stand on its own traveling stack. Until then: the teardown split (task.c) cures the exit path, and the borrowed-stack dispatch tripwire (scheduler.c) names any door that still swings. Also rename `kernel_interrupt_stack` — no interrupt has ever loaded it; it is the CR3-refugee scratch stack, and the name misled a two-day investigation | Robustness | L | a design ruling first (isolation doctrine vs kernel-resident thread stacks) | scheduler.c borrowed-stack tripwire / task.c teardown split / PAGING_ARENA.md's sibling doctrine |
| **EXCOLD and the old exception stubs retire together** (2026-08-11, the unification). The old per-vector stubs (handler_errors.S minus the NMI gate), the old reporters (`exception_panic`, `dump_fault_registers`, `dump_stack_trace`, the `gLastFault*` globals), and the deliberately-duplicated print/sanity helpers in exception_report.c all exist only to keep the EXCOLD fallback bootable. The duplication is the fallback's armor (a shared helper could break both paths at once) and dies with it. Retire after the unified path has survived a real soak AND a real fault on the P5 | Cleanup | S | unified path earning trust on bare metal | exception_report.c header note / kUseOldExceptions |
| **The demand pager still lives in simple_exceptions.c** — `handle_page_fault` plus its CoW/shared-object/user-kill halves belong in their own `page_fault.c`, with simple_exceptions.c shrinking to the EXCOLD museum piece. Deferred from the unification ON PURPOSE: a pure code move in the same change as a behavior change means two suspects when something breaks. Do the move alone, diff-only, after EXCOLD retires (one move instead of two) | Cleanup | S | EXCOLD retirement (the row above) | simple_exceptions.c |
| **`noseriallog` is a documented flag that does nothing.** The token rides in limine.conf's `/No Serial Logging` entry and was documented in CLAUDE.md as real, but it appears in no parser table and nothing outside kernel_commandline.c so much as reads the cmdline — the entry has always booted with serial logging fully on. Found 2026-08-16 by sweeping every limine.conf token against the parser table (the same sweep caught the lowercase NOAHCI/NONVME entries, which ARE now fixed). Chris's ruling 2026-08-16: book it and correct the docs rather than delete the entry — the entry is the feature request. **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-20**: the limine.conf walk deleted `/No Serial Logging` along with every other entry that carried a single bare token and no `ROOT=` (none of them mounted a root or reached husk). The feature request is not lost — it lives in THIS ROW now, which is the more honest place for it, since a boot entry that silently does nothing is a worse marker than a ledger line that says so. Real shape when built: a `kSerialLogging` bool the logd drain honors, DIVERTING rather than discarding (a file sink still gets every byte — "never drop a byte" is not negotiable, and a mute switch that eats log lines would be the exact thing the motto forbids) | Cleanup | S | when someone wants a quiet wire with a live file log | kernel_commandline.c cmdopts table (the limine.conf entry that used to carry it was retired 2026-08-20) |
| **`syscall.c` does too much work.** Chris's os32 principle (2026-08-02): the syscall file should DISPATCH — validate arguments, copy user memory, call the subsystem that owns the verb — with cases of five or ten lines, not implementations. os64 has drifted in layers: the newest syscalls already delegate (`net_dial`→`tcp_conn_dial`, `klog_read`→`klog_dequeue`, `thread`→`thread_join_create`), while the oldest carry their work inline (`read`/`write` own all the chunking and bounce-buffer discipline; `spawn` builds its own parameter block). The seam is obvious when it's worth cutting: boundary work stays at the door, verb work moves to the owning subsystem | Cleanup | L | explicitly LOW priority — "a working OS with more syscalls than its ancestor has earned a fat dispatcher" | `syscall.c` / os32 comparison |
| **synthfs renders every file into ONE contiguous buffer** — `synth_text_grow` doubles by `kmalloc(newcap)` + copy, and the allocator never returns NULL (exhaustion panics by name), so a synthetic file whose text runs to megabytes is a contiguous-allocation demand made on every `cat`. Every /sys and /proc file shares it; `/sys/net/tcp` is the first whose table can get there (16384 rows ≈ 1MB at the full ephemeral range) and where Codex flagged it (PR #46 rd7) after the row SNAPSHOT had already been chunked into pages for the same reason. Capping the table was rejected: it would blind netstat exactly under the load it exists to show. The fix is synthfs's, not one file's: page-chunked text (a `read` walks the chunk list by offset) or a streaming generator, so no synthetic file ever needs more than a page contiguous | Robustness | M | before any synthetic file routinely renders past a few hundred KB | synthfs.c `synth_text_grow`, sysfs.c `sys_gen_net_tcp` |

## Filesystems (the rename verb, 2026-08-16 — booked the day it was written)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`open` cannot say "not found".** SYSCALL_OPEN answers one value — SYSCALL_RESULT_INVALID — for a missing file, a malformed path, a full handle table and "nothing mounted yet" alike, so no ring-3 reader can distinguish ABSENT from UNOPENABLE. First consumer: `os64_slurp` (Codex #30 rd10), whose NO_FILE had promised "nothing is there, a fresh write is safe" — a promise it could not keep, now withdrawn in slurp.h. The shape when built: a distinct not-found result (an OS64_ERR_* the way timeout got one, since these cross the ABI), plumbed from vfs lookup through syscall_open; then slurp's NO_FILE splits into NO_FILE (absent, write-safe) and OPEN_FAILED, and image.c's status names can say "no such file" again | Correctness | S | the first caller that decides whether to WRITE on the strength of NO_FILE (conf writers already write to /home unconditionally, which is why nothing is wrong today) | slurp.h NO_FILE paragraph; syscall.c syscall_open return sites |
| **`mount.h` PUTS ITS OWN MEANING ON A NUMBER THE SYSCALL BOUNDARY ALREADY OWNS** — the row above's problem seen from the other end. `SYSCALL_RESULT_INVALID` is -1 and `SYSCALL_RESULT_BAD_USER_DATA` is -2 (syscall.c), and any family publishing its own negative codes in an abi header shares that number space without either side knowing. **`os64/net.h` is the house pattern and says so out loud** — "The first two are the syscall boundary's house-wide verdicts, named here so a dialer can read them; the rest are dial's own" — reserving -1 and -2 and starting dial's own codes at -3. `mount.h` did not: `OS64_MOUNT_BAD_ARGS` is -1 (harmless, it happens to coincide) but `OS64_MOUNT_NOT_FOUND` is -2, so a courier returning BAD_USER_DATA made `mount(1)` print "no partition wears that name or GUID (see /sys/block)" for an argument that was merely unreadable or over-long — accusing a disk of missing a partition and sending the reader to the wrong file to check. Found 2026-09-01 reviewing the round's own fixes. The PRACTICAL hole is closed (8c08109: the two couriers answer argument errors in mount.h's vocabulary, so no -2 leaves those paths, and the collision is written down where they return), but the DIVERGENCE stands — any future mount-family path that returns BAD_USER_DATA reads as NOT_FOUND again, silently and only in the error message. Fix = renumber so -2 carries the boundary's meaning and the mount family's own codes start at -3; in-tree that is mount.h, the kernel's returns, and `mount(1)`/`unmount(1)`'s tables, which are the only readers | Robustness | S | none — but it is an ABI renumber, so do it when nothing is mid-review against the old numbers | `abi/include/os64/mount.h` vs `abi/include/os64/net.h` |
| **FAT's flags-zero replacement rename is NOT atomic.** ext2 swings an existing directory entry to the source inode with one block write, but FatFs's `f_rename` refuses an existing destination, so legacy `fat_rename` does remove-then-rename and there IS a window where neither name exists. `OS64_RENAME_REQUIRE_ATOMIC_REPLACE` closes the safety hole for callers that opt in by refusing an existing FAT destination without touching either file; it does not make legacy replacement atomic, because FatFs exposes no repoint-in-place operation and FAT has no file identity separate from its directory entry. Existing callers deliberately retain flags-zero behavior | Feature-gate | L | only if FAT must perform replacement rather than safely refuse it | `fat_glue.c` fat_rename / `os64_rename_with_flags` |
| **A crash mid-rename leaves ONE SPARE LINK for e2fsck.** Deliberate: `ext2_rename` raises `i_links_count` before publishing the new name and lowers it after removing the old, so every crash window has count ≥ names, never fewer. Over-counting is the safe direction (fsck corrects it; an under-count lets the allocator hand out a live inode), but it does mean an unclean shutdown during a rename leaves a filesystem e2fsck will *fix* rather than *pass*. The real cure is a journal, which os64 does not have and is not pretending to | Robustness | XL | if os64 ever wants crash-consistency without fsck (i.e. ext3's whole reason for existing) | `ext2_write.c` ext2_rename step ordering |
| **The namespace lock is a plain spinlock held across a whole mount.** `kNamespaceLock` (vfs.c) makes mount and unmount exclusive, and its critical section contains the driver's `initialize`/`uninitialize` — real disk I/O, milliseconds, not microseconds. spinlock.h's own contract says the plain variant is for sections that are SHORT or callers that are RARE, and this leans entirely on the second: a contending core spins for the length of somebody's mount. That is affordable while these verbs are human-paced (typed at a shell, or a handful of `/etc/mounts.conf` lines at boot) and stops being affordable the moment anything automated mounts in a loop — a hotplug responder, or an auto-mounter. The upgrade is the one spinlock.h already names: a sleeping mutex, which is also what the exclusive half of the path gate would want. Not built now because os64 has no sleeping mutex at all, and inventing one under a bug fix is how a fix becomes an arc | Performance | M | the first caller that mounts without a human waiting for it | `vfs.c` kNamespaceLock / spinlock.h plain-variant note |
| **A renamed directory strands any task whose cwd is inside it.** `task->cwd` is a STRING, so renaming `/a` to `/b` leaves a task sitting in `/a/x` holding a path that no longer resolves — every relative open from that task then fails. Unix does not have this problem because a cwd there is a reference to the directory itself, not to its name. Fix = hold something identity-shaped (inode number + filesystem) rather than text, which is a real change to how cwd works everywhere | Robustness | M | when someone actually renames a directory out from under a running shell (husk, most likely, and it will be confusing) | `task.h` cwd / `ext2_rename` |
| **Cross-filesystem rename is refused, and nothing does the copy instead.** `syscall_rename` correctly declines when the two paths land on different mounts (that's a copy, not a rename) — but until `mv` exists in userland to do copy-then-unlink, moving a file from `/home` to `/` simply cannot be expressed. Not a kernel debt so much as a note that the userland half is what completes the verb | Feature-gate | S | when `mv` gets written (Chris's, per the labor division) | `syscall.c` syscall_rename cross-mount refusal |
| **~~A failing `close()` cannot tell anyone~~ — KERNEL HALF PAID 2026-08-24; what remains is one ABI RULING.** The silent-loss half is fixed: `handle_file_object_close()` returns the filesystem's status, `file_close_in_kernel()` carries it back through a params block, `fat_close` reports it (and no longer leaks its two allocations on the failure path), and a failed flush is logged LOUDLY at the source under `DEBUG_EXCEPTIONS` — always-on, deliberately, since a tripwire tagged with a flag that is off by default is a check that cannot fire. Every closer now learns, including the burial closer in task.c that has nowhere to return an error to. **PAID IN FULL 2026-09-22 — the ruling came ("not 0") and the answer is `OS64_CLOSE_NOT_COMMITTED` (-3, `abi/include/os64/file.h`); the paragraph that follows is the reasoning as it stood, kept because the answer it argues for is the one that landed:** what should `close()` return to ring 3 when the flush failed? POSIX says the descriptor is gone regardless and the error is informational, which os64 should probably match — the handle must be released either way or a program leaks handles by failing — but "released, and by the way your data may not be on disk" needs a distinct answer that is not `SYSCALL_RESULT_INVALID` (that already means "no such handle", and conflating them tells a caller to go looking in the wrong place). `os64_conf_write` does not need it — it commits through `os64_sync`, which does propagate — so nothing was blocked on this | Feature (PAID) | — | done | `kernel/src/handle.c` handle_close_rc / `syscall_close` / `abi/include/os64/file.h` |
| **"Read a whole file up to a cap, and say whether it was bigger" EXISTS THREE TIMES, and every fix to it has had to be applied three times.** The copies: `kernel/src/conf.c` (`conf_io_kernel`), and `os64_conf_read` + `os64_conf_write` in `userland/libos64/conf.c` — the last two in the SAME FILE, twenty lines apart. This is not a suspicion, it is the review record: PR #29 rd1 fixed truncation-reported-as-success in two of the three; **rd3 was the third copy, missed by rd1**; rd12 fixed read-error-treated-as-EOF in all three at once. Three separate rounds spent on one bug in n places. That is what the config subsystem has actually been "screaming for" across thirteen review rounds — not more care, less arithmetic. THE CURE HAS PRECEDENT IN THIS TREE: `klog_format.h` holds the ONLY copy of the log layout logic precisely because it used to be spelled four times across the ring boundary; same disease, same shape of fix. Minimum viable payment is to collapse the two USERLAND copies into one `conf_slurp()` (same file, no ABI surface, pure subtraction); the kernel's stays its own because it is a different ring with different I/O, but then there are two that look alike instead of three that drift. Booked at Chris's call 2026-08-24 rather than done inside the signals PR — it is a refactor, and refactors do not belong in a review gauntlet. **THE HELPER NOW EXISTS: `os64_slurp()` (userland/libos64/slurp.h + .c, born 2026-08-25 with libimage), carrying all four distinctions and the receipts for each. The remaining payment is ADOPTION** — the two conf.c copies calling it instead of their own loops. Deliberately not done in libimage's PR: rewriting the most-reviewed code in the tree does not belong in a slice about pictures. **AND THE COUNT WAS WRONG — IT WAS FOUR COPIES, NOT THREE.** `kernel/src/gui/desktop.c`'s `read_whole_file` was the fourth, written before this row's "before a fourth reader" gate and never counted because it was named differently. It carries the rd12 bug UNFIXED to this day (`if (n <= 0) break;` — an error read as an ending); the consequence is mild (a truncated wallpaper fails to parse, nothing is written) which is exactly why nobody noticed. **It leaves with the desktop shell** in the ring-3 move, which is why it is not being fixed in place — but if that move stalls, fix it there | Robustness | M | ADOPTION next time conf.c is opened; desktop.c's copy dies with the ring-3 shell | Codex #29 rd1/rd3/rd12; `kernel/src/conf.c` + `userland/libos64/conf.c` + `gui/desktop.c` |
| ~~Exclusive file creation~~ **PAID 2026-09-03.** `open(path, "x")` reserves the caller's handle capacity first, then atomically creates a new regular file for writing or refuses if the name already exists. ext2 pins the exact new inode before publishing its dirent; FAT uses FatFs `FA_CREATE_NEW`. The older prepare-then-claim door remains `OS64_RENAME_NOREPLACE`, for callers that want to build a temporary and publish it only after verification | Feature (PAID) | — | done | `os64_open` mode "x" / `OS64_RENAME_NOREPLACE` |
| **Two concurrent saves of ONE config file lose one of them (a lost UPDATE).** `os64_conf_write` is read-modify-rename and the middle is unserialised: two savers read the same old text, each merges its own setting, and whichever renames LAST silently discards the other's. The corruption half is already fixed — per-saver temporaries (Codex #29 rd7/rd8) mean nobody publishes an inode another writer is still filling — so what remains is purely the lost update. `OS64_RENAME_NOREPLACE` now provides the real name claim this fix was gated on; a future slice can claim a guard around the whole read-modify-publish operation and retry after contention. Chris's 2026-08-24 priority ruling still stands: with os64's current codebase and userbase the odds are "approximately 0.00000001%" — no consumer saves the same file from two processes, and the ones that save at all (gclock, the desktop) each own their file | Robustness | M | when concurrent config writers become real | Codex #29 rd9 / `userland/libos64/conf.c` os64_conf_write / OS64_RENAME_NOREPLACE |
| **/dev block nodes — the day raw disk I/O becomes ordinary file I/O.** The NAMES are already committed (`block_assign_dev_names`: `nvme0`/`sata0`/`ram0`, printed by /sys/block — /dev nodes MUST reuse them verbatim, that commitment is the whole reason they were settled early). Reads are cheap; WRITES need a designed answer to `block_verify_write_allowed` (a raw write to a mounted partition is exactly what the stray-write tripwire exists to stop, and also exactly what dd is for — "unmount it first" is the obvious v1 policy) plus block-cache coherence. First customers: hexedit on a partition, dd, the native fsck above, reflashing the lifeboat from inside os64 | Feature | M | after mount/unmount ships; hexedit-on-a-partition is the pull | the 2026-08-30 mount design conversation |
| **USB mass storage + ISO9660 — thumb drives mount like disks.** `usb0` is the fourth bus prefix when xHCI grows bulk transport. ISO9660 enters the way every filesystem enters os64 now — something recognized on a block device in the sweep — NOT welded to ATAPI/CDs (there will be no CDs, Chris 2026-08-30). Wrinkle worth remembering: a stick imaged from an ISO has no GPT — whole-disk filesystem recognition is part of the slice | Feature | L | when a thumb drive matters | Chris, the 2026-08-30 mount conversation |
| **FatFs volume numbers leak on non-LIFO unmount.** `kFatDiskNumber` only reuses the TOP number (unmount handles LIFO); mount A, mount B, unmount A leaks A's number until reboot, bounded by FF_VOLUMES (10). Loud when it finally bites (f_mount refuses); a free-number bitmap is the cure if mount/unmount churn ever becomes a workload | Cleanup | S | if FAT mount cycles become routine | vfs_unmount |

## Userland utilities

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Five fixtures FAIL on a hand-run testrun where they should SKIP or not care.** Seen on the P5 2026-10-30 (`testrun` typed into husk: 62 passed, 5 failed, every failure a fact about the launch). `renametest`, `openxtest` and `gziptest` bail at their first step when neither `/fat` nor `/ext2` is a secondary mount — the P5's mounts.conf is `mount = home` alone — which is the synctest rule's case exactly (stat_test's step 6 already answers SKIP for a boot with no secondary disk: a fact about the BOOT, not the code under test). `cwd_test` step 1 asserts the start cwd is `/` and `stat_test` step 5 stats a relative `bin` against `/`: true from the TESTRUN boot entry, where the kernel spawns testrun at the root, and false from any shell not sitting there — the fixtures should `chdir("/")` themselves before asserting. Until paid, a P5 testrun is read against this row | Cleanup | S | a quiet day | `userland/tests/{renametest,openxtest,gziptest}`, `kernel/test/elf/{cwd_test,stat_test}.c` |
| **tar's recursive safety relies on a namespace with no links.** Its self-exclusion can identify the output archive by canonical path text because one file cannot have a second name, and extraction's lexical absolute/`..` gate is sufficient because no earlier member can install a symlink that redirects a later one. Link support changes both facts: hard links require file identity for self-exclusion, while archive creation must not follow symlinks and extraction must either refuse link members or resolve every destination beneath the extraction root without following installed links. This is a feature gate, not a defect in today's namespace; the link slice must update tar in the same change | Feature-gate | M | before either hard or symbolic links enter the namespace | `tar.c` canonical_path / `tar_format.c` tar_extract_path / `dirent.h` symbolic-link warning |
| **libpng refuses a zlib stream that expands beyond IHDR's exact raster.** The extra decoded bytes make the datastream malformed, but established libpng treats this encoder defect as a benign warning and displays the declared raster. The strict refusal reports the integrity violation as MALFORMED at the cost of rejecting that existing file population. If browser interoperability makes the cost visible, recovery must discard every byte beyond the raster without weakening the separate pixel cap or Adler-32 check | Compatibility | S | before the browser decodes arbitrary web PNGs | `png.c` filtered-size inflate limit / `map_inflate_status` |
| **libpng does not decode Adam7-interlaced PNGs.** Legal files with IHDR interlace method 1 return `OS64_PNG_UNSUPPORTED`; libimage propagates the unsupported-variant result and Yonder displays a placeholder. Confirmed with Debian's `https://www.debian.org/Pics/openlogo-50.png` (50×61, 8-bit RGBA, Adam7): the existing PNG host harness reproduces the rejection before decoder allocation. Supporting it requires decoding and scattering the seven passes, including empty passes, packed samples and per-pass filter history, while retaining size limits and integrity checks. This is a PNG compatibility gap, separate from SVG rendering | Compatibility | M | next PNG web-compatibility slice | `userland/libpng/png.c` parse_ihdr; `userland/libpng/include/png/png.h`; `tools/test_png_host.sh` interlaced refusal case |
| **A program cannot ask what its output handle IS.** `ls \| grep x` receives ls's terminal-width columns, several names to a line, because ls has no way to learn that handle 1 is a pipe rather than a terminal — Unix ls answers the same question with `isatty(1)` and prints one name per line into a pipe, which is what makes `ls` a well-behaved pipeline stage. The fact already exists: `/proc/self/handles` names each handle's kind (`console-out`, `pipe-write`, `file`), so the seam is a libos64 call that answers for one handle — reading that report, or a syscall if parsing text per launch proves too heavy — shaped as "what kind is this handle", not a bare yes/no, because a pager and a progress meter want the same answer. ls is the first consumer: one name per line when handle 1 is not a terminal, and `-C` to ask for columns anyway | Feature | S | the next pipeline that trips over column output, or the next program that wants to behave differently on a terminal | `ls.c` print_short_listing / `procfs.c` proc_gen_handles |
| **whois follows no referrals.** IANA answers a domain query with the TLD's registry in a `refer:` line, the registry answers with the registrar, and a full whois chases that chain until a server has no `refer:` left (GNU whois carries a static table of servers per TLD instead — a maintenance burden this house does not want). v1 prints IANA's answer raw and `-h SERVER` walks the chain by hand. Booked at birth (BROWSER.md rung 2 said "book it, don't build it"): the chase is a loop over the same dial-send-read the program already does, plus parsing one `refer:` line and a hop limit so two servers referring to each other cannot spin | Feature | S | when a person tires of typing `-h` | `whois.c` main |
| **POSIX ustar cannot record a file of 8 GiB or larger.** Its 12-byte size field carries 11 octal digits, so tar now refuses above 8 GiB minus one byte with a format-specific diagnostic instead of the codec's generic numeric-field error. GNU's base-256 size encoding is the compatible extension when a real file reaches that ceiling; it belongs in both encoder and decoder, with host interop at the boundary | Feature | S | the first archive containing a file of 8 GiB or more | `tar.c` write_header / `tar_format.c` put_octal and get_octal |
| **A fixture's SKIP code cannot tell "this boot can't" from "this code is broken".** `synctest` reports `0x05CC0001` and `conftest` reports `0x0C0F0001` when a write to `/home` fails, and testrun treats both as SKIP — correctly, because a boot with no writable `/home` is a fact about the boot and a suite that cries wolf gets ignored. But the SAME code is what a genuinely broken write path would produce, so the day `os64_conf_write` or `sync_all` actually breaks, the suite says SKIP and goes green. The cure is for the fixture to establish WHY before deciding: probe `/home` for writability first and report "no writable /home" as its own distinct code, leaving the original code to mean what it says. Cheap, and it wants doing once for both fixtures rather than twice. Booked at Chris's request 2026-08-23 after `conftest` failed a run that had no data disk attached; low priority precisely because a boot without `/home` is not the normal case, which is also what makes it the kind of masking nobody notices until it matters | Bug (test-only) | S | before trusting a green suite on an unusual boot — or the next time either fixture is opened | `testrun.c` skipcode column / `conftest.c` / `synctest.c` |
| `tail`: a SHORT READ inside the backward block scan silently loses lines. If the fill loop ends with `filled < blockSize`, only the bytes that arrived are scanned but `blockEnd` still moves a whole block, so newlines in the unread tail are never counted — fewer lines printed than asked, no error. Needs an I/O error or a mid-read shrink to trigger. Fix = treat a short fill as an error, or rescan from `blockStart + filled` | Cleanup | XS | when a real short read is ever observed | `tail.c` find_tail_start |
| `tail` reads no STDIN — `something \| tail` exits 2 instead of following the pipe. A pipe can't seek, so `-n` would need a keep-the-last-N ring instead of a backward scan; that is the actual work | Feature-gate | S | when a pipeline wants it | `tail.c` main |
| `tail` computes "the last N lines" from the `stat` size taken BEFORE the open, so a file that grows in between is measured slightly stale. Self-corrects instantly under `-f`; harmless otherwise | Cleanup | XS | never, probably | `tail.c` main |
| **`js` with no script prints usage instead of an interactive prompt.** Reading a script from standard input works (`js -`); typing one line at a time and seeing each result is the prompt node and python give a bare invocation, and JAVASCRIPT_TASKS.md defers interactive input. The prompt needs a line editor and a way to evaluate one entry and show its value, which R0's run operations deliberately do not return (CONTRACT.md § Source and job turns: completion values are discarded) | Feature | M | when someone wants to try JavaScript a line at a time on os64 | `apps/js/js.c` parse_plan / USAGE.md § Not supported |

## husk scripting (the control-flow slice, 2026-08-28 — booked the day it was written)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~**No `for`, because husk has no variables.**~~ **PAID 2026-08-28, the same evening, by the ruling it was waiting for**: a variable IS an environment variable (DIVERGENCES § The shell). `NAME=value` is a statement, `for NAME in WORDS` assigns each word the same way, `for NAME` walks a script's arguments, and `export` survives as the older spelling of the same act | Feature (PAID) | — | done | husk run_assignment / exec_for |
| **No `break` / `continue` / `case`.** `break` and `continue` are an unwinding verdict the evaluator does not carry yet (it carries `exit` and abort); `case` is a whole second matcher. Bourne had all three in 1977; nobody here has wanted one yet | Feature | S / M | a script that needs to leave a loop early | husk exec_while |
| **A `while` loop that spawns nothing cannot be stopped from its own keyboard in a script.** Interactive husk polls the console between iterations for a Ctrl+C typed while husk itself was foreground (a child gets it as SIGINT and dies 130, which aborts the block). A SCRIPT does not poll — its stdin may be data, and polling would eat it — so a script's loop is interruptible only through a child. `while true; do :; done` with no child in it is a runaway; kill it from another VT. Gate: a way to ask "is a Ctrl+C pending?" that does not consume stdin (the tty owner could answer it) | Robustness | S | the first runaway script loop | husk loop_interrupted |
| **Tab completion reads the line raw**: no quotes, so a name with a space in it is not completable, and the word is cut at the last blank. Also lists everything at once rather than on a second Tab, and offers no completion for `$NAME` | Polish | S | the first file with a space in its name that someone wants to complete | husk complete_at_caret |
| **husk's screen model trusts its inputs** (booked 2026-09-15, the day wrapped-line editing arrived; (2)–(5) were named to Chris before the first line — "the rest of the limits seem fine, for now" — and (1) turned up while building it). The editor moves the caret by row and column arithmetic from three facts — the width, the column the line starts in, and where the cursor is — and each can be wrong. (1) **The prompt is assumed to start at column 0**: output that ended without a newline puts it further right, and edits across a wrap land that many cells off. Cure: ask the terminal where the cursor is (`ESC[6n`, which os64's tty does not answer) or zsh's PROMPT_SP trick, which needs care because os64's tty and a VT100 disagree about the last column. (2) **A line taller than the screen**: a row scrolled off the top cannot be climbed back to. Hard to reach at 256 bytes; an 80×24 ssh window meets it at 4096, so it returns with the longer line. (3) **Other output while you type** (a background job's notice, a crash line) moves the cursor behind the editor's back. (4) **A multi-byte character, in `$PROMPT` or typed into the line**, counts as several cells, which is wrong on a UTF-8 terminal over ssh (the editor is byte-oriented throughout: Backspace takes one byte of it, too). (5) **A terminal that reflows its own text on a resize** (Windows Terminal) has moved the line before the SIGWINCH arrives, so the redraw starts a row or two off — readline's compromise too. Every one is a wrong PICTURE only: the buffer never depends on the screen, so Enter submits exactly what was typed, and the next prompt starts clean | Polish | S each | (2) the 4096-byte line; the rest, the first time one bites | husk § the line on the screen |
| **A builtin's output inside `$(...)` is not captured.** Builtins write to husk's own stdout, not through the capture pipe; `$(dirs)` prints to the screen and substitutes nothing. Redirecting husk's own handle 1 for the duration would fix it and is a small change; nobody has wanted `$(dirs)` yet | Polish | XS | a script that wants a builtin's answer as a word | husk run_substitution |
| **`time ! cmd` times but does not negate.** Both are prefixes handled in run_segment, `time` first, and `time` hands the rest to run_expanded which knows no prefixes. `! time cmd` works. Bourne treats `!` as part of the pipeline grammar so either order works there | Polish | XS | someone typing it the other way round | husk run_segment |

## GUI (all GRAPHICS.md "future work")

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Pulse top-consumer details — deferred 2026-11-10.** Add a small consumer name/value line to CPU, memory, download, and upload details when workload attribution warrants it. Motivating case: an animated Yonder page can make the compositor (`guicomp`) the largest CPU consumer; include kernel tasks/workers in the ranking and distinguish measured CPU ownership from which application caused the work. `htop` is sufficient for investigating this today, and preserving Pulse's low overhead takes priority. Preferred direction: shared, cached kernel summaries, provisionally `/sys/cpu/top` and `/sys/net/top`, refreshed at a modest cadence so Pulse avoids per-process `/proc` scans. Kernel ranking still has a cost: benchmark total system overhead, including accounting and refresh work, rather than judging Pulse's own CPU percentage alone. Network rankings need reliable process attribution with defined TCP/UDP, shared-connection, payload/retransmission, and sampling-interval semantics. Memory ranking must distinguish heap footprint from total resident memory. Summary paths and refresh policy require design; no new syscall is presumed. | Feature / observability | M–L | revisit when occasional `htop` inspection no longer suffices; explicitly deferred, not a Pulse merge gate | Pulse Instruments follow-up; `userland/apps/pulse/pulse_data.c`; `libos64/monitor.c`; `kernel/src/driver/filesystem/proc/procfs.c` |
| **Remembered placement by view or mode.** The Pulse graphical proposal needs one shared position and independent preferred sizes for normal/compact presentations. Keep its first routing adapter local; extend the shared placement helper with an explicit role/mode key when a second application needs the same behavior. Preserve larger saved preferences when fitting smaller screens and merge only the active role's fields. This does not resolve the separate cross-process publication race below. | Feature | M | a second application needs independent sizes per view/mode | [Pulse graphical arrangement](docs/design/pending/PULSE_GRAPHICAL.md#remembering-the-users-arrangement) |
| **E5D remembered placement uses last complete publication, without cross-process serialization** (accepted E5D limitation). Two instances of one window role can read the same record and publish different whole-record merges; the later publication can lose an earlier edit even when the instances changed different components. Independent roles use different files, and closing an instance writes nothing. Add conflict-preserving updates before same-role instances are required to share live geometry state | Feature-gate | M | a requirement for same-role windows to share live state without lost edits | [E5D stored records and restoration](docs/design/pending/explorer/E05D_WINDOW_PLACEMENT.md#stored-records-and-restoration) |
| **The title-bar X never escalates, and six raw-API apps ignore the close request** (found 2026-10-18, glogo and the Mandelbrot demo would not close from their X). Closing is a REQUEST on the owner's queue (input.h `INPUT_EVENT_WINDOW_CLOSE`); libui answers it for every toolkit app (`ui.c`: `on_close`, else quit), but glogo, fpu_demo, fpu_orbit, gbounce, gclock and roam read their own queues with `os64_gui_event_poll` and drop it. Alt+F4 covers that with its escalation (a second press within `GUI_CLOSE_ESCALATE_TICKS` is SIGTERM to the owner); the X, added with the decorations (9/23), goes through `wm_decoration_action`, which only delivers the request, so a click is ignored however often it comes. Not a regression: neither app ever answered the request, and the X never escalated. **Cure, two halves:** one close-request path for the key and the button, so the X gets the same escalation; and the raw apps handle `OS64_GUI_EVENT_WINDOW_CLOSE` (three lines each). glogo matters most, because it is the start-here exemplar and teaches every new app to skip the request. A fixture: a window that ignores the request, closed by a second click. Until then, Alt+F4 twice closes any of them (Chris: "I like my double-F4") | Polish | S | when someone reaches for the X on a raw app again, or the next new GUI app copies glogo | compositor.c Alt+F4 escalation / window.c `wm_decoration_action` / libos64 ui.c close dispatch |
| **gclock does not visibly follow Interface font Apply in Chris's setup** (reported 2026-10-11). Increasing the Interface font substantially and clicking Apply leaves the clock text unchanged; window resizing works again. Chris reports that font behavior predates E1K and explicitly deferred it. The controlled QEMU test with inherited FontSize and a 16-to-32px change succeeded, so it does not establish that the Workshop path works in the reported setup. Investigate effective `gclock.conf` FontSize, session delivery and adoption/refusal limits before choosing a fix; cause and exact reproduction settings remain unverified. Acceptance: an inheriting clock follows supported Interface changes through Workshop, and a refused change is understandable. | Bug / investigation | TBD | next gclock / appearance maintenance; deferred by Chris, not an E1K blocker | `userland/apps/gclock/gclock.c` `refresh_clock_font` / `resize_for_font`; [E1K evidence](docs/design/pending/explorer/evidence/e01k/README.md) |
| Tickless + GUI coexistence — add the damage-wake nudge IPI in `gui_damage_add`, lift `gui_start()`'s pin refusal. Grew teeth 2026-08-05 when tickless became the DEFAULT; **the acute half HEALED 2026-08-13** (the backstop arc's preemption lease — GUI entries dropped `SCHED=periodic` and boot the default scheduler with `BACKSTOP=10`, verified same day). The chronic half remains: a publish's damage reaches the compositor only when an input IRQ or the backstop cadence happens to end its hlt — no wake of its own — and the compositor still declines its core-1 pin under tickless | Feature-gate | S | when GUI latency on a QUIET system matters (the nudge); when pinning proves worth having back | GRAPHICS #1 / SCHEDULER #6 |
| **`ls` assumes a 100-column terminal and no terminal can tell it otherwise.** `%-20.19s` five to a row is exactly 100 columns, and gterm's grid is exactly `WANT_COLS 100` — so every `ls` row fills the grid, the EAGER wrap in `tty.c` moves to the next line, and `ls`'s own `\n` leaves a blank one. Three separable fixes, smallest first: (a) DEFERRED WRAP — a `pending_wrap` flag in `tty_t`, set instead of wrapping at the last column and honored by the next PRINTABLE character, which is what every terminal since the VT100 does and why `printf("%80s\n")` does not double-space on an 80-column xterm; this alone fixes the symptom at any width, on gterm AND the text console. (b) a way for an app to ASK its terminal's size, so `ls` computes columns instead of assuming five. (c) gterm resizing its pty grid on WINDOW_RESIZE plus a notification — booked in **PTY.md's Resize row** (this used to point at GRAPHICS.md for a "SIGWINCH-shaped seam", which was never written there; GRAPHICS.md § Event delivery now carries the signal-vs-event RULE, and PTY.md carries the slice). Note (b) and (c) are separable and (b) is the one `ls` needs: a signal saying "it changed" is useless to a program with no way to read what it changed TO. **(b) and (c) PAID 2026-08-25**: `/proc/self/tty` already printed `rows`/`cols` (the VT arc) — it reported 38x100 on a maximized gterm only because nothing ever told the pty, and `pty_resize` + SIGWINCH now do (PTY.md § Resize; `winchtest` reads the size back through that file). **(a) remains open** — it is the one producing the blank lines, and it is the smallest | Bug (a) | (a) S | (a) now — it is the visible one | `tty.c` wrap block / `ls.c` print_entry |
| Legacy text console (`BasicRenderer.c`) has no SMP locking | Robustness | S | if legacy console is used under SMP (GUI path doesn't) | GRAPHICS #9 |
| ~~**The Alt+Tab SWITCHER — a visible strip of titles, nothing moves until Alt is released.**~~ **PAID 2026-08-23, same day, by Opus — and VERTICAL, not a strip of cells.** Chris's question on reading the design ("what's in each box? the name of the app?") exposed the layout error: a horizontal row of cells works for ICONS, and os64 has none — sixteen titles sharing 1024px get five characters each. A vertical list of titles is both the arithmetic answer and the older Unix one (twm's window menu 1987, fvwm's `WindowList` 1993). Built as designed otherwise: compositor-owned scene layer, snapshot carrying titles + dim flags, nothing moves until commit, Escape cancels, Up/Down walk it too, a VT switch away abandons the hold. `wm_touch_focus` deleted with its reason. **Found on the way, before it shipped: `ascii == 0x1B` DOES NOT MEAN ESCAPE** — every arrow key arrives as a three-event VT100 burst whose first event carries 0x1B, so Escape-cancels would have fired on the first third of every Up press; `keyboard_is_escape_key` / `keyboard_arrow_updown` join `keyboard_fkey_number` as the dialect-aware helpers. Verified by QMP screendump across seven cases, zero panics. The old row read: Today's Alt+Tab raises every window it steps through (the raise IS the feedback) and, worse, RESTORES every minimized window it passes — Chris minimized everything and could not get one back without bringing them all back (2026-08-23, first afternoon with minimize; Fable's "stays restored" rule was the mistake). Design is complete in GRAPHICS.md § "The Alt+Tab switcher": a compositor-owned strip (the rubber band's precedent — not a window), titles as the icons, minimized entries dimmed, Escape cancels, commit on release touches exactly one window. **Deferred to Opus the same day** (the morning's review fan-out cost 7% of the week's Fable quota) | Feature | — | PAID | GRAPHICS.md switcher chapter |
| ~~**The config SEARCH PATH — one `conf = dir:dir` line in `/etc/os64.conf`, one walker, every reader obeys, `/sys/conf` reports which file won.**~~ **PAID 2026-08-23, same day as the design.** All six private ladders gone (logd, husk.rc, os64get, hosts, net.conf, desktop); `kernel/src/conf.c` is the one walker; `/sys/conf` publishes the ladder, its source, and which file each reader took; every resolve prints one DEBUG_BOOT line naming the misses. TWO DEPARTURES from the design, both argued in the doc: ring 3 asks the KERNEL to walk (`SYSCALL_CONF_RESOLVE`, 47) rather than parsing `/sys/conf` itself — the design's route was two parsers of one ladder plus a separate reporting channel, and the syscall makes the per-reader diagnostic free; and the walk is RESUMABLE (`from` position, returns index+1) because `hosts` is MERGED by Chris's 2026-08-22 ruling and first-hit-wins would have silently overturned it — a settings file is not a database. Proven by flipping `conf = /etc:/home` and watching husk move from `/home/husk.rc` to `/etc/husk.rc` | Cleanup / feature | — | PAID | docs/conf_path.md |

| ~~Damage rect LIST instead of single union~~ **PAID 2026-08-18** (compositor.c: 8-rect list, merge-when-cheap heuristic with a 128px slack, overflow falls back to the v1 union so the worst case is exactly the old behavior). THE LIST CARRIED A PREREQUISITE nobody had written down: `composite_one` painted each window's WHOLE frame, harmless under one damage rect and corrupting under several (paint outside rect i lands in rect j, already composited and not yet flushed). Chris's P5 found it within the hour — console background bleeding into gbounce on raise, and trailing the ball only while the mouse moved over the console. Fixed by compositing through a `surface_view()` of the damage rect, so surface.c's existing per-primitive clipping makes "writes stay inside the rect" structural instead of remembered; reproduced and confirmed with a deliberately re-broken control build (2562 bleed pixels vs 0). It is also a second win in its own right: a 25x25 ball no longer repaints the 768x480 console beneath it. Measured in QEMU, same layout and workload both sides: guicomp **18% → 7%** with one animating ball and `top` running. NOT region algebra, deliberately — an exact non-overlapping cover costs more to maintain than the overdraw it saves at this population size; revisit only if rect counts ever get interesting | Cleanup | — | PAID | GRAPHICS #4 / Chris's P5 GUI shakedown |
| PAT write-combining for the framebuffer flush (replaces PCD/UC mapping) — **"measure first" is satisfied**: the P5 pays retail for UC writes and guicomp pegs a core under one animating window (2026-08-17). **Re-ranked 2026-08-18**, deliberately NOT taken with its two neighbours: this is the only one of the three that leaves the compositor — per-core `IA32_PAT` MSR setup in `ap_initialization_handler` plus PTE PAT-bit plumbing in paging.c, kernel-wide blast radius — and the other two just cut the number of bytes it would be a multiplier ON (flushes/sec 33 → 1 with the covered window; the surviving flushes are small rects). Take it when the P5 says the remaining flush volume still hurts, not before | Cleanup | M | after a P5 re-measure with the damage list + occlusion in | GRAPHICS #3 / Chris's P5 GUI shakedown |
| ~~**Occlusion**~~ **— PAID.** ~~(1) compositor-side~~ **PAID 2026-08-18**, and its known limit **PAID 2026-10-18** (OCCLUSION.md): `gui_window_publish` damages only the part of its rect no window above covers (`wm_visible_damage`), and the compositor paints each damaged pixel once, by the one window or the desktop that can be seen there (`gui/visible.c`'s claim walk), where it had painted every layer bottom-up. QEMU, yonder animating danlegt: 2.89 paints per damaged pixel became exactly 1.00, and with a gterm over most of yonder the damage fell from 89M to 13M pixels per 20 s. What the 2026-08-18 version did, for the record: drop the damage when the rect was entirely behind ONE window above it, so no damage → no composite → no flush. Chris's acceptance test ran green in QEMU: dragging the console over gbounce took guicomp **7% → 3%** and flushes **33/sec → 1/sec** (the survivor is `top`'s once-a-second console repaint), and uncovering it brought the ball back live and correct — the snapshot still happens under occlusion precisely so a reveal never shows a stale frame. Its known limit then, chosen at the time: single-window containment, so two windows that JOINTLY cover a third still composited it. ~~**(2) toolkit-side**~~ **PAID 2026-08-27 — by the OTHER shape** (GRAPHICS.md § Covered windows): the kernel keeps a `COVERED` flag per window (`wm_cover_sweep_locked`, every compositor frame) and nudges with COVERED/UNCOVERED events; `os64_frame_clock_bind` makes the frame clock nap while covered, so every clock-paced app went quiet with one line and gterm stops painting on the event. That is X11's VisibilityNotify with the cooperation moved INTO the library, not the Wayland frame-callback this row sketched — a publish-ack is still the more general design (idle-by-default for apps with their own loops), and it stays available if a consumer outgrows the flag. Proven in QEMU: fpu_demo behind gterm reads 0% and its TIME stops; the eleven-gbounce P5 run is Chris's to repeat. What the measurement had found, for the record: Chris's P5, 2026-08-18: eleven gbounce windows lined up to vanish behind the console. guicomp went to a flat **ZERO** and the machine read 100% idle — the compositor half has nothing left to give — while each hidden client kept accruing **1-2ms** of CPU. That residue is exactly two things, both outside the compositor's reach: the client still computing and painting frames nobody can see, and publish's canvas→content snapshot, which deliberately still runs so a reveal repaints from a CURRENT frame (deferring it to reveal-time would tear, which is why snapshot-on-publish exists at all). The fix is a publish acknowledgment a client can wait on — libui paces app frames on it, Wayland's frame-callback insight (X11's VisibilityNotify asked apps to cooperate and they never did; callbacks make idle the DEFAULT). ACCEPTANCE TEST, better than the original: eleven hidden gbounces, and the per-client ms must stop climbing | Perf | rides libui (2) | (2) Phase C/D's frame-loop design | Chris's occlusion question 2026-08-17; his eleven-window P5 measurement 2026-08-18 |
| **The cover sweep still asks "behind ONE window?"** `GUI_WINDOW_COVERED` (`wm_cover_sweep_locked`) is set only when a single window above covers the whole on-screen frame, so a window hidden by two that jointly cover it is still told it can be seen, and its frame clock keeps running. Publish and the compositor stopped using that test on 2026-10-18 (OCCLUSION.md); the sweep kept it because "covered" tells a CLIENT to stop, which is a different promise from "don't composite this". The exact answer is now cheap: `wm_visible_damage(w, on_screen, ...)` returning 0 is "nobody can see any of you" | Perf | S | a consumer whose window is hidden by a pair of windows in practice | OCCLUSION.md § What it does not do |
| ~~**The compositor holds kGuiLock longest, with interrupts off**~~ **PAID 2026-10-19** (booked 2026-10-18). On the P5, danlegt's middle in a 1700x900 window on a 2560x1440 desktop, one minute: the composite site held the lock 66 ms a second, 149 µs a pass, with 1,582 holds between 1 and 4 ms and the worst at 5 ms. Anything that runs on the BSP while it waits, where knet lives and the RTL8125 interrupts, waits with it. The work in that hold is compositing into the RAM backbuffer (the flush to VRAM runs after the lock is released), and the copy in it, `surface_blit`, moved one pixel a loop at -O0. It is one `memcpy` a row now, as is the flush. P5, danlegt's middle maximized at 1920x1080, one minute: 577 µs a million pixels composited where it was 1,946, 51 µs a pass, 26 ms a second, the worst hold 1.6 ms and none over 2 ms; knet woke later than 1 ms in none of 64,248 wakes. **A publish buffer swap is SHELVED on the same readings.** It would take publish's copy out from under the lock: two canvases, publish flipping which one the compositor reads, and the client bringing its stale one forward by last frame's damage outside the lock (EGL's buffer age; two are enough because the compositor reads content only under the lock). The price is what `os64_draw_ctx` promises every app, one canvas pointer for life. Once the kernel's `memcpy` became libos64's, publish held about 330 µs at under 50 a second, about 1.6% of one core, so the swap would buy little. Reopen it if a publish hold grows back past a millisecond, or knet's late wakes start following yonder | Perf (PAID; the swap SHELVED) | M (the swap, ABI through libos64) | the swap: a publish hold past a millisecond, read as two reads of `/sys/gui` a minute apart, or knet's wake latency (`/sys/net/knet`) following yonder | YONDER_PERF.md / GUI_LOCK.md / Fable's note and the P5 readings of 2026-10-18 and 2026-10-19 |

| **Canvas capacity is committed eagerly** — a window reserves and maps a SCREENFUL of canvas (and a screenful of kernel-side content), not the size it actually is. That is exactly what buys the resize contract: no allocation, no pixel motion, and never an unmap of a live user page. **MEASURED 2026-08-19** (Chris asked whether the estimate was real, having found 0x301000 of canvas in gterm's `/proc/<pid>/status` and reasonably concluded the cost was 3MB): a windowed task costs **7,616,144 bytes**, a windowless one **1,306,808**, so the window itself is **6,309,336** — exactly two capacity surfaces (2 × 3,145,728 at 1024x768) plus ~17KB of page tables for the task mapping and the `window_t`. The same gbounce under the old content-sized allocation carried ~474KB of surfaces, so its total footprint went 1.76MB → 7.62MB, a 4.3x increase. HALF OF IT IS INVISIBLE FROM `/proc`: only the task-side canvas appears in the heap range: the kernel-side `content` surface is `kmalloc`'d and shows up nowhere a process can see, which is its own small observability debt. The lazy version maps pages as the window grows — the mapped region is always a PREFIX of the reservation, so growth stays append-only and still needs no shootdown — but mapping into a foreign address space from the compositor's thread needs a task reference or a deferred-work channel, since the owner can exit between the decision and the mapping. Booked BEFORE coding per the known-debt rule; take it when window counts or window sizes make the standing cost real | Perf | M | when a desktop holds enough windows for 6MB apiece to matter | `window.h` canvas_cap_w comment / `gui_client.c` pivot |
| ~~**A resized gterm lies about its size**~~ **PAID 2026-08-25 — the SIGWINCH slice (PTY.md § Resize): syscall 51 `pty_resize`, signal 28 admitted with `pty_resize` as its producer, gterm's arm calls it, `/tests/winchtest` in the suite with its broken-kernel control run first. The build found what the design missed: every park loop ended only for terminating signals, so a caught WINCH would have arrived at the next keystroke — `signal_park_must_end` now asks "terminate, or anything a handler will catch" at all twelve sites. Original row kept as the record:** — the window resizes, the pty grid does not: a grown terminal letterboxes, a shrunk one clips its right and bottom edges away. The other half is grid realloc plus a notification to the program inside, which is SIGWINCH's entire job description and the reason `stty size` exists. PTY.md booked it before the terminal existed ("Resize — grid realloc + child notification; gate: window resize"); that gate is now open. **THIS ROW USED TO SAY THE NOTIFICATION "WANTS THE CLIENT-NOTIFICATION SEAM". IT DOES NOT, and the sentence was written 2026-08-19, before the event queue carried `WINDOW_RESIZE` and before ring 3 could catch a signal at all** (2026-08-25 correction). It is TWO hops, and only one of them is a notification problem: **WM → gterm already arrives** on gterm's own event queue and is ignored at the resize arm, no new mechanism needed; **gterm → the program inside the pty is SIGWINCH**, whose number 28 is already reserved. The seam is about when a GUI client WAKES and paints (publish-ack, theme reload, retiring the snapshot poll); a signal is about a fact a process must hear, and a program inside a pty has no window and no queue — which is exactly why 4.3BSD invented SIGWINCH. **The two are independent: neither blocks the other, and the seam changes when the event loop wakes, not what it reads, so nothing gets written twice.** Third piece, separable and smallest: a way for a program to ASK its terminal's size (`ls` row item b, our `stty size`), because a signal that says "it changed" is useless to a program with no way to read what it changed TO | Feature-gate | M | now — its gates are open and it needs nothing unbuilt | PTY.md Resize row / `gterm.c` resize arm |

| **The environment lives in husk.rc, and programs no shell started never see it.** Found the morning the root menu could seat `top` in a gterm with no husk in the chain (2026-08-26): environment inherits from the SPAWNER, so a menu-launched program gets the desktop's, which got the kernel's seed (`PATH=/bin`) — and the day husk.rc says `export EDITOR=/bin/scribe`, everything launched from a menu, a dock, or `gterm <program>` is a stranger to it. Costs nothing today; it is the seam that has not been chosen yet. Unix chose it twice, badly — `.profile` (per-shell, misses GUI launches) versus `/etc/environment`/PAM (system-wide, runs no script) — then every desktop grew a third layer (`~/.xsessionrc`, `~/.pam_environment`, `environment.d`) because shell config had been allowed to become where the environment lived. **The os64 shape, proposed and not yet ruled:** the environment is the SYSTEM's and the shell is one consumer of it — an `env.conf` on the config ladder (`/home` over `/etc`, `key = value`), read once by whoever is init and seeded into every task before anything is spawned; the kernel's `PATH=/bin` seed becomes that file's default instead of a constant. husk.rc keeps what is genuinely a shell SESSION's (aliases, the prompt, `cd`). Then a program launched from the menu, from a gterm, from VT2's husk or at a text boot on the P5 sees one world. Shape it with Chris before coding — it touches init (kernel today, husk-as-init tomorrow) and the env seed in task_create | Design | M | before husk.rc grows its first `export` that a GUI program needs | `task.c` env seed / `husk.rc` / docs/conf_path.md |

| **USB has no hotplug: a device plugged in after boot, or plugged back in, is never attached** (booked 2026-09-23, after Chris unplugged the P5's keyboard and mouse to test #123's lost-device release, and neither came back). xHCI enumerates once, at boot; a port-status change now releases what a vanished device held (`xhci_hid_lost`) but nothing enumerates what arrives. The cure is boot's own path, run from the event: on a port that reports a connection, reset it and repeat the slot/address/configure/arm-the-endpoint sequence, with a fresh `hid_keyboard_t` or pointer source, and free the dead device's slot and rings when it leaves. | **xHCI has no interrupt yet: `kusb` drains it when the tick rings** — the event rings are drained by a daemon pinned to the BSP (`kusb.c`, XHCI_INTERRUPT.md § The design), so an event waits a tick, and USB input is as live as the BSP is. What that costs, measured in QEMU on 2026-11-12 with `/sys/usb`: the worst gap between passes is 20 ms where the poll's was 10, so about one event in a hundred waits a second tick. The longest single event handled inside a pass is a terminal switch's repaint, 37 ms under QEMU's emulation, which is how far Bluetooth's clockwork can be held past the pass's one-tick deadline. On the P5 (2026-11-12): 29 of 3,300 event-finding drains on the Bluetooth adapter's controller waited between 20 and 200 ms and none longer, and the Bluetooth step ran at most 20.8 ms after a wake began with terminal switches among the events. The interrupt (tracker X.3) removes the tick from the common path. | **An LE device takes 5 to 10 s to reconnect, and the poll is not why** — measured on the P5 2026-11-12 with `/sys/usb`'s timeline (XHCI_INTERRUPT.md § What was measured): the USB poll comes round every tick there, so an event waits 10 ms at most. A reconnection spends 2.03 s in the scan round that finds the device, up to 30 s before that round if the previous one just missed it (`manager_retry`'s backoff), 0.3 s connecting and encrypting, and 1.4 to 3.2 s discovering the HID service over the air again, although the device is bonded and its handles were read the first time. Keeping a bonded device's HID handles with its bond would remove the largest fixed piece; a shorter first retry, or scanning continuously while a bonded device is absent, the largest variable one. A reconnection that fails to resolve the address costs a second scan round, about 4 s | Performance | M | when the reconnect time is the complaint again | `bt_manager.c` (`manager_retry`), `bt_le.c` service discovery; `/sys/usb` |
| **The AX210 sometimes does not answer its first reset after a reboot, and Bluetooth is dead for that boot** — seen on the P5 on 2026-11-12 after a warm reboot with both LE devices connected (`/sys/bluetooth/scan`: `failed (reboot required)`, `HCI command timed out (opcode 0c03)`; `/sys/usb`'s timeline: the reset sent 6.7 s after boot, 7 events from the adapter's controller, failed 4 s later with the poll running normally), and by Chris's account once before, weeks earlier, about three boots in a row with a pause of a few seconds at a device initialization step. Seen a third time the same day (a warm reboot with both devices connected, on the X.2 build), with NO pause on the boot screen: the reset is sent a few seconds after boot and times out in the background, so a pause at device detection is not its symptom. A later warm reboot on the same kernel worked, so it is intermittent. A cold boot cleared it every time, which points at state the adapter keeps across a warm reboot and loses with its power. UNDIAGNOSED. A warm boot that finds the firmware already running skips the firmware load (BLUETOOTH.md), so the adapter may come up holding the previous boot's connections; whether the reset was never delivered, never answered, or answered on an endpoint nobody was listening to is what the next occurrence has to say. Read `/sys/usb` and `/sys/bluetooth/scan` BEFORE rebooting, and note whether the reboot was warm and whether devices were connected. One failed reset should not cost the boot: retrying it, or loading the firmware again, is the likely cure once the cause is known | Robustness | ? | the next occurrence, with the two files read first | `bt_scan.c` (the init round), `xhci.c` AX210 bring-up; BLUETOOTH.md § warm boots |

| **A kernel-half pointer to a GUI syscall answers -2, which ring 3 reads as NO_RESOURCES.** The rows that put a pointer in the dispatcher's mask (create's title, get_surface's, event_poll's and get_state's outputs) are refused by the dispatcher with `SYSCALL_RESULT_BAD_USER_DATA`, -2, and -2 is `OS64_GUI_ERR_NO_RESOURCES` in the GUI vocabulary, so a bad pointer reads as "out of memory". `gui_window_set_title` keeps its pointer out of the mask and lets `copy_user_string` refuse it, so it answers BAD_ARGS. | **The terminal app needs a pty-shaped seam** — a userland program owning another program's stdio (husk's stdin/stdout wired to a GUI window instead of a VT). No such kernel object exists: tty binding is task↔VT only. Unix hit this wall in the '70s and invented the pty (a pipe that lies about being a terminal — why `script`, Emacs shell-mode, and every terminal emulator since work); os64's shape will be its own, likely a handle pair with one tty-shaped end. Same machinery the pipes-into-pagers `/dev/tty` problem wants (Chris's "not now", 2026-08-13) — one design serves both. Booked at GUI-toolkit arc start, BEFORE coding, per the known-debt rule | Feature-gate | M | GUI toolkit Phase D (design) / Phase E (the terminal is the consumer) | GRAPHICS.md phases 2026-08-17; tickless-backstop candy list |
| **A full damage list becomes one rectangle around everything.** `os64_damage_add` (shared/damage.c, both rings) keeps the list its caller sized — libui's eight rectangles, the compositor's sixteen — and, when one more merges cheaply with none, unions them all: a window with nine small changes far apart repaints the box around them. Merging the pair whose union wastes least would keep the list full and the waste near the marks. Found when a page's 35 form controls marked every frame repainted 2506x1267 of the view (yonder) | Performance | S (shared with the compositor: it reviews as kernel code) | a window or desktop with more than eight small, scattered changes a frame | `shared/damage.c` (`os64_damage_add`) |

## Build system

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A host test that links libos64's string file has no sanitizer watching its long copies** (booked 2026-11-10, H2.3's mutants). `userland/libos64/str.c` compiles in `shared/copy_fill.c`, which defines `memcpy`, `memmove` and `memset` under the compiler's own names, as a freestanding build needs, and moves a long run with the CPU's string instructions in inline assembly. On the host that definition takes the place of the sanitizer's checked one, and the sanitizer does not instrument assembly, so a long copy or fill that runs past a buffer inside such a harness is not a report: a mutant that copied 5,000 bytes into a 4,096-byte scratch passed `tools/test_h2_host.sh` under ASan until the test put guard bytes after the scratch. Every host harness that links str.c has the same blind spot. A host build of str.c that leaves the three names to the C library (the `os64_` names calling them) would give the checks back. | M | S | A harness trusted to catch an overrun in copied bytes | `userland/libos64/str.c`, `shared/copy_fill.c`, `tools/test_h2_host.c` |
| **The mutant runner deals out fixed shares, so one slow share is the whole tail** (booked 2026-10-18, the first full run through `tools/mutants_parallel.py`). Each harness's mutants are split into contiguous balanced shares, and a harness lists related mutants together, so similar slow ones land on one worker: motion-5 drew a run of tick mutants at about 2 minutes each plus one that ran to the 1800 s case timeout, and was still running at 19:00, while the other motion workers had finished between 17:48 and 18:44. **Cure:** a shared queue per harness (or one for the whole run), each worker taking the next mutant when it is free, keeping everything the runner guarantees now: the per-worker copy and baseline, verdicts only for mutants that worker ran, and one line per mutant in the summary. A queue leaves no worker idle while another holds a backlog | Perf | S | the next full run that waits on one worker | tools/mutants_parallel.py `plan` / `work` |
| **`tools/test_dom_mutants.py` has not built a mutant since 2026-10-21** (booked 2026-10-27). That day `dom.h` began including `way/origin.h` (fetch and XHR), and the harness's include list never gained `libway/include`, so every mutant reports COMPILE ERROR and none is counted. Adding the include is not the whole cure: a trial build compiled and then ran past the harness's own case timeout, against a DOM host that has grown since. Fifteen of its anchors have also gone stale under the arc's later slices (an anchor checker over every harness lists them). `mutants_parallel.py` cannot take it as it stands, since its list is written `mutants=[`. **Cure:** the include, a timeout sized to today's suite, the stale anchors re-cut, and a full run | Test | S–M | the next libdom slice that wants its contract mutants | tools/test_dom_mutants.py |
| **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-22 — do not build this.** The row used to say the "real fix" for GDB's address ambiguity was PIE with a loader-assigned per-TASK load bias. It is not a fix and cannot be one: **shared text requires ONE virtual address per image across every task that maps it**, which is exactly what makes a page shareable, so the moment libos64 became a `.so` a per-task bias became incompatible with the feature it would have been serving. `upper \| upper` will always share a base. Two of the three stated limits were paid a different way the same day: the 16MB image cap and the 104-slot count were both artifacts of the slot size, and shared libraries shrank app images by 90% (13MB of `bin/` → 1.8MB), so the slot went 16MB → 4MB and the count 104 → **443**.
