The hard ceiling underneath is `-mcmodel=small` (non-PIE apps must live in the low 2GB — ~128 apps at 16MB, ~500 at 4MB) and Chris named it from memory before anyone counted. The third limit — two instances of one program being indistinguishable — is now permanent, and its real successor is the row below | Superseded | — | never | `app_bases.py` header comment |
| **An in-OS debug server (GDB remote protocol), and the retirement of the whole unique-address scheme.** THE LINUX ANSWER, transplanted: Linux never makes addresses unique — every non-PIE binary loads at `0x400000` and PIE gets a *random* bias — because its debugger is PROCESS-AWARE (ptrace attaches to one process; gdb "inferiors" each carry their own symbol table and address space; `/proc/<pid>/maps` says where things landed; the linker's `r_debug` rendezvous tells gdb when a `.so` appears and at what bias). os64 pays a **no-MMU tax on a machine that has an MMU** — unique link addresses are a technique from DOS TSRs and ROM overlays, needed here only because host GDB stares at QEMU's flat linear space with no idea what a task is. A gdbserver speaking the remote protocol out of the running OS ends the entire problem class: `app_bases.py` retires, the low-2GB ceiling stops mattering, and `upper \| upper` becomes two inferiors. **T-SHIRT SIZE: L**, and it decomposes — (1) the wire protocol and a transport, S–M, the protocol is simple text packets and os64 has both serial and TCP; (2) stop/continue/step of a chosen task plus register read/write, M, needs the scheduler to hold a task stopped and hand over `thread->regs` (STOPPED state already exists); (3) memory read/write in another task's address space, S, `paging_walk_paging_table` + HHDM is already the house technique; (4) breakpoints, M, int3 patching means writing to shared, CoW, possibly read-only text pages — the one genuinely new mechanism, and it interacts directly with the page cache built today (a breakpoint must privatize, or every process running that binary gets it); (5) the symbol/bias handshake, S, since `/proc/<pid>/maps` already names every region including `/lib/libos64.so` and its base. Item (4) is where the surprises live | Feature | L | when debugging two instances of one program stops being a curiosity and starts being a Tuesday | `app_bases.py` / SUCCESSION § harness / PROC.md |
| **NOTHING IN os64 CAN SAY WHICH BUILD IT IS** (Chris, 2026-09-03: "We really need to institute versioning"). The day it cost an hour: the P5 was refreshed from the wrong tree and answered a chunked fetch with the PREVIOUS os64get's refusal, and the only way to tell which binary was running was `ls -l /bin/os64get` against two byte counts. No program has a `--version`, `/sys/shlib` shows an inode and a size, the kernel banner names no commit. THE SHAPE THAT FITS: one string, stamped at build time (`git describe --always --dirty` plus the UTC build time), compiled into libos64 — every program loads it, so `--version` on any of them and a row in `/sys/shlib` fall out of the same string; the kernel gets its own copy in the banner and `/sys/version`. The valet's `LIST` verb should carry the server's stamp too, so a refresh can say "installing 3(b)-abc123 over 52-def456" before it does. Not a semantic version — nobody is promising compatibility between two builds of a hobby OS a day apart; a build IDENTITY, so two people looking at two machines can tell whether they are looking at the same code | Cleanup | S | the next time two machines disagree about what os64get does | `userland/GNUmakefile` (the stamp), libos64, `kernel.c` banner |
| **A SERVED BUILD DIRECTORY CAN OFFER A PROGRAM THAT NO LONGER EXISTS.** `tools/os64serve.py` serves `userland/bin`, `userland/bin/tests` and `kernel/bin` WHOLE, and the rules that fill them copy what their lists name and never remove what a list stopped naming — so an app deleted or renamed (or moved between `apps/` and `tests/`, which is how `/tests` was born) leaves its old binary to be listed and installed by `os64get -a` until a clean build. Found auditing the invariant behind a libgarb review finding (a staging directory holds exactly what its list names), which `userland/bin/pages` and `userland/bin/fonts` now keep with `stage-pages` and `stage-fonts`; these three predate that slice and are filled by the userland and kernel makefiles' own lists. The fix is the same shape per directory: after staging, remove what the list does not name — with care in `userland/bin`, which also holds the `.so`s and the staged subdirectories | Hygiene | Low | none | `GNUmakefile` stage-pages / `userland/GNUmakefile` / `kernel/GNUmakefile` |
| `--defsym` **must precede `-T`** on the ld command line or it is silently ignored and every app links at the `0x400000` fallback — builds fine, runs fine, and fails only in the debugger. Guarded by a comment at the flag; a build-time assert (entry == assigned base) would make it loud instead of documented | Robustness | XS | none | `userland/GNUmakefile` APP_RULE |
| **THE NX ARC NEVER REACHED THE `kernel/test/elf` FIXTURES — the ones with a `.bss` run RWX.** That script places `.text`/`.rodata`/`.data`/`.bss` with no page alignment between them, so ld folds them into ONE LOAD segment carrying the union of their permissions: `readelf -lW kernel/bin/stat_test` answers `RWE`, where an app answers three segments (`R E`, `R`, `RW`). The apps were fixed on 2026-08-16 and app.ld's "PAGE-ALIGN EACH PERMISSION CHANGE" comment carries the whole argument — including the part that matters here, that **the kernel derives page protections from segment flags**. So these fixtures genuinely run with writable text and executable data. Found 2026-09-01 when `stat_test`'s buffer grew and the fixture re-linked, surfacing a warning ld had been printing all along — a fixture only re-links when its OWN source changes, which is why it went unseen for a fortnight; confirmed pre-existing by rebuilding the same fixture at its old buffer size (identical `RWE`). A fixture with no `.bss` escapes by accident, not by design. Severity is low because fixtures are trusted code that ships to no one, but the proof harness runs under weaker memory protection than the system it is proving. NOT the `userland/tests` fixtures — those link as apps and are already split (`nx_test`, of all things, is one of them). Fix = the `ALIGN(CONSTANT(MAXPAGESIZE))` boundaries app.ld already has | Robustness | S | none | `kernel/test/elf/linker.ld` vs `userland/link/app.ld` |
| os64 FAT driver can't read a sub-FAT32-minimum volume: `mformat -F` forces FAT32, but <65525 clusters (~34MB) yields a malformed hybrid the driver misclassifies as FAT16 → every open fails. Currently worked around by the 64MB DISK_SIZE_MB floor. Fix = teach the driver FAT16 (or drop `-F` and let mformat pick), so the ramdisk can shrink below 64MB | Robustness | M | if the ramdisk ever needs to be <64MB | GNUmakefile disk target / fat driver |
| ~~**`os64 refresh` no longer carries the fixtures**~~ **PAID 2026-08-29, same day.** Booked when `/tests` split out of `/bin`: the valet routed by FILE NAME, the fixtures share no suffix to key on, and `os64serve.py` skips subdirectories — so an over-the-wire refresh kept `/bin` current and left `/tests` at whatever the last ISO burn delivered. The fix is LOTS: a served directory may carry a label (`os64serve.py .../userland/bin/tests=tests`), every LIST line gained a fourth field naming it, and `os64get.conf` grew an `@lot` rule class. The lot names the SOURCE, never the destination, and ranks BELOW exact and suffix rules — which is what lets one served directory hold files bound for several places (`kernel/bin` carries fifteen fixtures for `/tests`, plus `os64_kernel` for `/fat/boot` and `libtest.so` for `/lib`). The field is APPENDED so an older os64get reads the first three and stops; all it loses is the catalogue CRC (its hex parser wanted exactly eight characters to end of line), so that one transitional refresh re-checks each file against its own per-file header — slower, never wrong, and healed by the newer os64get it just fetched. A single-file fetch asks for a LIST it would not otherwise need, but ONLY when the conf has `@lot` rules and no DEST was given. Verified over slirp: `-a` reported 103 files 102 unchanged (a wrong route would have installed 47 fixtures into /bin instead), and a bare `os64get HOST fputest` answered `/tests/fputest` | Feature-gate (PAID) | — | done | `os64get.conf` / `tools/os64serve.py` / `os64get` |
| **`/QEMU GUI Boot` roots on the FAT lifeboat, not ext2.** Its `ROOT=2f4fd02e-…` is the FAT partition's GUID (`sgdisk -i 1 disk/os64.img`); `/QEMU Boot (ext2 root)` and `/RAMDisk Boot (GUI)` carry the ext2 one, `1ec5f5ab-…`. The entry was written from the pre-ext2 template and never followed the 2026-07-19 move, and its own comment ("QEMU Boot plus the optional GUI subsystem") claims the same root as the entry above it. What it costs: the lifeboat carries no `/etc/certs`, so every https page on that entry answers "TLS trust could not open (bundle: /etc/certs/roots.pem)", and /home mounts but the root is a 64MB FAT with its own husk.rc. Found 2026-11-04 hunting a GitHub page death in QEMU; it had hidden since the GUI entry was born because a GUI boot in QEMU rarely visited the wire. Fix = the ext2 GUID, and say so in the entry's comment. Low priority by Chris's call (it took this long to come up); `/RAMDisk Boot (GUI)` is the working GUI entry meanwhile | Robustness | XS | none | `limine.conf` `/QEMU GUI Boot` |

## Shared libraries (booked 2026-08-22, the day libos64 became a `.so`)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A refresh that replaces open files leaves orphaned inodes on disk until the next boot — expected, and now permanent for libos64.so.** Confirmed 2026-08-22 while testing `os64get -a`: after a run that replaced 83 files, `e2fsck -fn` on the image reports "Inodes that were part of a corrupted orphan linked list" plus bitmap differences, and ONE more boot clears it completely (122 → 114 inodes, spotless). That is the ext2 orphan chain doing exactly its job — a file unlinked while open has its name removed at once and its storage freed at LAST CLOSE, with the on-disk chain as the crash record. What shared libraries changed is the duration: husk and logd release their old inode when they exit, but **the loader holds `/lib/libos64.so` open forever (no unload)**, so a replaced library's old inode stays orphaned until the machine reboots. Nothing is lost and nothing is corrupt; the cost is that fsck cannot be green between a refresh and a reboot. Two things follow — don't read a post-refresh fsck as damage, and note that `make fsck-ext2` normally runs on a freshly BUILT image and so never sees this at all. **PAID 2026-08-28 by unload-at-refcount-zero**, exactly as this row's gate said it would be: an object is unloaded the moment nothing holds it, which closes the handle, which is what makes ext2 reap the orphan. Verified end to end — a session that replaced two RUNNING binaries by rename, then let the old copies exit, left `make fsck-ext2` green on both images with no orphan complaint. The duration is now the honest one: a replaced binary's old inode lives exactly as long as the programs still running it | Cleanup (PAID) | — | done | ext2 orphan chain / `shared_object_unload_locked` |
| **A replaced `.so` on disk does not take effect until reboot, and the old one keeps working even after it is deleted.** The registry is keyed by PATH STRING and never unloads, so once `/lib/libos64.so` is resident, `shared_object_load_or_get("/lib/libos64.so")` is a cache hit that never touches the directory again. Demonstrated live: renaming the library out from under a running system, then starting programs that had never run before — they all started fine. Half of that is *correct and Unix-like* (a mapped image outliving its directory entry is exactly what an inode reference buys, and ext2's orphan chain already makes it true for the file). The half that is a real gap is the other direction: `os64 refresh` replacing libos64.so leaves every subsequent spawn on the OLD code with nothing saying so. The fix is retention policy — notice the inode changed, or unload at refcount 0 — and it is the same slice already deferred for unload, which is the same slice deferred for the block read cache. **PAID 2026-08-28, and it needed BOTH halves of that sentence, not either.** Unload-at-zero alone would have missed precisely the files worth refreshing (husk, logd and libos64.so, which a refresh finds RUNNING and so holding a reference — they CAN reach zero, by exiting every shell and killing logd, but a refresh does not wait for that), and the identity check alone would have left every idle program resident forever. So: every load opens the path and compares the file's identity (`vfs.h` f_ident — an ext2 inode number), a changed identity RETIRES the resident copy (transitively, so its dependents rebuild against the new library too) while its current holders keep running it, and an object with no holders is unloaded. The old direction is unchanged and still correct: a program that is running keeps the image it started with | Cleanup (PAID) | — | done | `shared_object.c` retire/reload; `test_shared_object_reload` |
| **Symbol lookup is a LINEAR SCAN of the whole dynamic symbol table, per relocation.** `shared_object_find_symbol` walks every `.dynsym` entry doing `strcmp`. libos64 exports 158 symbols and a page carries a handful of relocations, so this is free today; it is O(exports × relocations) and would stop being free at a few thousand exports. The irony is that `DT_HASH` is ALREADY PARSED — the loader reads its `nchain` word to learn the symbol count, and then ignores the hash buckets sitting right next to it. Using them properly is maybe thirty lines | Cleanup | S | when a library exports thousands of symbols, or first-touch latency shows up in a profile | `shared_object.c` `shared_object_find_symbol` |
| **A same-page fault collision still SPINS.** A core that faults a page another core is mid-resolution on busy-waits (with interrupts deliberately re-enabled, see the comment) for the duration of a disk read. Correct, and the wrong shape: the faulting thread should USLEEP on the I/O completion and be woken on publish. Carried over from the original design, unchanged, and now reachable far more often since every program's own image goes through this path | Cleanup | M | when a fleet of tasks starting at once makes it measurable | `shared_object.c` `shared_object_resolve_page` |
| **PAID 2026-08-22 (and the first fix was wrong, which is the instructive part).** `make -C userland` built with the HOST toolchain while `make` from the repo root built with the cross one — same tree, same makefile, different compiler depending on which directory you typed in. `userland/GNUmakefile` says `CC ?= x86_64-elf-gcc` / `LD ?= x86_64-elf-ld`, and that is a NO-OP: `CC`/`LD` are make BUILT-IN variables (`cc`, `ld`), and `?=` only fills in an *undefined* variable, not one with a built-in default. From the root the value is inherited from the parent make and the cross tools win, which is why nothing has ever visibly broken; build inside `userland/` and you silently get host gcc and host ld. Cause: `CC ?= x86_64-elf-gcc` is a no-op when `CC` is defined, and `CC`/`LD` are make BUILT-IN variables (`cc`, `ld`). What saved the root build all along is `MAKEFLAGS += -rR` on line 2 of the root GNUmakefile — `-R` is `--no-builtin-variables`, which propagates into the sub-make and leaves CC/LD genuinely UNDEFINED, so `?=` fired there. Build straight from `userland/`, with no `-rR` in play, and the built-ins are back and `?=` goes quiet. **The obvious fix — `ifeq ($(origin CC),default)` — is exactly wrong**: under `-rR` the origin is `undefined`, not `default`, so it fixed the direct build and BROKE the root build (every link ran with an empty `$(LD)`, and make strips the resulting leading `--` as its own ignore-errors prefix, so the error read `defsym: No such file or directory (ignored)` — a genuinely baffling message for a missing linker). Shipped fix assigns when the origin is `default` OR `undefined`, and never when a human meant something (command line, environment, parent make). All four paths verified. It mattered because the two toolchains have DIFFERENT DEFAULTS — host ld emits `--hash-style=gnu`, bare-metal binutils sysv, and the kernel's loader needs sysv (it takes the dynamic symbol count from DT_HASH's nchain); that one is now pinned explicitly on both link lines | Cleanup (FIXED) | — | done | `userland/GNUmakefile` CC/LD |
| **Every dynamically-linked EXECUTABLE ever run is retained at refcount 0, forever.** The warm-cache retention policy was written for one library and silently widened to every app the day userland went dynamic: `cat /sys/shlib` after boot shows `/tests/hog` at `refs: 0` with its page frames, `page_phys[]`, parsed tables, and an OPEN ext2 handle — and each of the ~64 apps joins it on first run. Two consequences beyond memory: `shared_object_find` is a linear `strcmp` walk that grows by one per distinct program ever run, and every replaced binary's old inode rides the orphan chain until reboot (the row two above, multiplied by the whole of /bin). Found by the 2026-08-23 review of the shlib commit. The fix IS the unload-at-zero slice already booked — this row exists so that slice knows its real population is executables, not libraries. **PAID 2026-08-28.** `/sys/shlib` on a running system now lists only what is in use — every entry at `refs: 1` or more — and a fourth consequence turned up while paying it that nobody had booked: ext2's open-inode table is 64 slots, so a boot that ran 64 distinct programs would have filled it and started refusing every open of a file it did not already hold | Cleanup (PAID) | — | done | `shared_object.c` retention / `/sys/shlib` |
| **`app.ld` and `lib.ld` carry byte-identical section blocks** — the four PLT sections, the eight read-only dynamic-metadata sections (`.hash` … `.eh_frame`), and the `.dynamic`/`.got`/`.got.plt` trio, with the same "canonical names or they evaporate" commentary twice. ld's `INCLUDE` would make those blocks one shared linker-script fragment. The second library paid the two adjacent debts from the original row: `--lib-slot-size` now feeds `lib.ld`'s slot ASSERT, and `app_bases.gdb` uses bare `add-symbol-file` for the prelinked libraries instead of rediscovering their absolute `.text` addresses by regex. What remains is extracting that shared fragment, whose value arrives when either script next needs the duplicated block changed | Cleanup | XS | the next dynamic section either script needs | `userland/link/*.ld` |
| **A spawn re-reads and re-parses the image of any program with no live instance** (booked 2026-08-28, the day unload-at-zero landed — the price of the retention policy, stated rather than discovered later). Every `shared_object_load_or_get` opens the path (it must, to compare identity), and a miss then runs `elf_parse_image` — section headers, dynamic tables, and `.symtab`/`.strtab` when stack traces are on — plus one disk read and one relocation pass per page the program touches. Everything after the first time comes out of the block cache, so it is CPU and kmalloc churn rather than I/O, and the object that would actually hurt to reload (libos64.so) stays resident while any dynamically-linked program runs — which is every moment a shell or logd is up; exit every husk and kill logd and it unloads like anything else, and the next "press any key" shell pays this reparse once. NOT MEASURED: the honest statement is that no slowdown was visible driving the shell by hand, and nobody has A/B'd it against a build that retains | Cleanup | S | if a spawn-heavy workload (`watch -n 1 "ps -ef"` is the house's worst case) ever feels slower — measure before optimizing, and the answer is probably a parse cache keyed by identity, not a return to retention | `shared_object_load_or_get_locked` |
| **Objects in a DT_NEEDED cycle can never be unloaded**, because their references are each other's. A depends on B and B on A: each holds an edge on the other, so neither ever reaches zero even after every task using them is buried, and both stay resident (and registered, and holding their files open) until reboot. This is refcounting's oldest known limitation and the cure is a mark-and-sweep over the registry, which is a great deal of machinery for a condition os64 has not produced: its three libraries form an acyclic graph (`libpng.so` needs `libgzip.so` and `libos64.so`; `libgzip.so` needs `libos64.so`; `libos64.so` needs no library), and the loader's cycle handling exists because DT_NEEDED can express a cycle | Cleanup | M | the day a real dependency cycle exists — which wants a reason first, since a cycle between two libraries is usually a design mistake | `shared_object.c` deps[] |
| **An in-place rewrite is invisible to the identity check.** Identity is the ext2 inode number, so replacing a file by RENAME is noticed (the new file has a new inode) and truncating one in place is not. That is sound today by construction rather than by luck: while an object is loaded its file is open, and ext2 REFUSES to truncate an open file — so `cp`'s "w" mode cannot rewrite it behind the registry's back. The gap is the `"u"` mode (open for update without truncation), which is not refused and which no shipped program uses. A future byte-patching editor pointed at a running binary would have its edits ignored by every later spawn until the last holder exits. Cheap cure if it ever matters: fold size and mtime into the recorded identity | Robustness | S | when something in userland opens a file `"u"` | `shared_object_t.ident` / `ext2_truncate_locked` |
| **The shared-library window's bump allocator is reclaimed only when the unloading object was the last one placed.** An exact reclaim in the common case (load one PIE image, unload it, load it again) and an abandoned span otherwise, exactly like the failed-load unwind it copies. Unloading turns bump slots into something a running system consumes repeatedly rather than once per distinct image, so the march is now unbounded in principle — at 512GB of window against images measured in kilobytes it needs millions of interleaved loads to matter, and only images that arrive WITHOUT an address of their own are in the bump region at all (a prelinked library and a non-PIE executable both take load_bias 0 and consume nothing) | Cleanup | S | if the window ever runs out — which prints its own diagnosis | `shared_object_unload_locked` / `kSharedObjectNextVirt` |
| **The relocation set is three types wide** (`RELATIVE`, `GLOB_DAT`/`JUMP_SLOT`, `64`). No TLS, no IFUNC, no `COPY` — and `COPY`'s absence is a RULING, not a gap (libos64 exports functions, not variables; see DIVERGENCES). No `.init_array`/`.fini_array` execution either, which is the one that will bite first: LIBOS64.md promises ELF constructors and destructors for at-exit handle cleanup, and the loader does not run them | Feature-gate | M | when libos64 wants a constructor, or C++ ever happens | `elf_relocate.c` / LIBOS64 § startup |
| **Port-local C library function bodies repeat across libraries** (booked 2026-10-01 at the JavaScript design review). A rename header maps upstream C spellings onto libos64 verbs without adding function bodies. FreeType carries fourteen hidden stand-ins, including `ftport_memchr`, `ftport_strcmp`, `ftport_strrchr`, `ftport_strstr`, `ftport_qsort`, and a `memcpy` family; libtls carries a `memcmp`. A body fixed in one port can stay broken in another. Chris ruled that shared semantics belong in libos64 as `os64_` verbs, with port-specific C spelling shims kept separate. The QuickJS prerequisite change supplies `os64_memchr`, `os64_strchr`, `os64_strrchr`, and `os64_strcmp`; the QuickJS port must use a rename header. FreeType and libtls consolidation remains deferred, and must check semantic differences when mapping existing bodies onto shared verbs | Cleanup | S | the next change to either port's runtime, or the first bug found in one of the copies | `userland/libfreetype/port/runtime.c` / `userland/libtls/port/runtime.c` / JAVASCRIPT_REVIEW.md finding 8 |

## Networking (the arc's booked honesty — NETWORK.md carries the arguments)

| Debt | Sev | Cost | Gate | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **TLS 1.3 engine boundaries.** Full certificate-authenticated TLS 1.3 and a guarded TLS 1.2 fallback are implemented. 0-RTT, external PSKs, TLS 1.2 session resumption, client certificates, post-handshake authentication, send-side record padding, OCSP stapling, SCTs, certificate compression, ECH and post-quantum groups remain outside the engine profile. A CertificateRequest receives an empty Certificate; mandatory client authentication fails without weakening policy. | Scope | L | a required site or measured workload needs one of these features; each needs its own design and interoperability evidence | `userland/libtls/`, [TLS13.md](docs/design/completed/TLS13.md) |
| **The TLS hello is os64's own, and a fingerprint tells it from any browser's.** A server can hash the first message of a handshake (its suites, extensions, groups and signature types: the JA3 and JA4 families do), and yonder's is short and unlike Chrome's or Firefox's whatever the User-Agent says. Chris's ask, 2026-11-10: make it look like the preset's browser. **The rule any cure keeps: never advertise what we cannot honour or safely ignore**, because every item in a hello is a promise the server may take up. That sorts a browser's hello into three piles: free to match (suite and signature order, GREASE values, padding, extensions whose answers can be ignored); needs real code (certificate compression wants a Brotli decoder, and Chrome's application-settings extension an answer); needs real cryptography (Chrome's post-quantum key share, over a kilobyte of the hello). The values come from captures of the browsers' own hellos, never from memory (WIRE.md § 1's rule), and the design reverses DIVERGENCES § The browser's identity, which records the handshake as os64's own. It is one signal among several: the row "A CDN's managed bot challenge never completes" fails in the challenge's script, after the handshake has succeeded | Compatibility | L | a site that refuses the handshake itself, or serves a challenge to yonder that it does not serve a browser with the same headers | `userland/libtls/port/tls13_handshake.c` `hello`; HTTP2.md § 2.9 |
| **Kept TLS sessions are the process's, not a site's.** The session cache is keyed by hostname, port and trust (TLS13.md § Resumption), so a host embedded by two different sites is offered, on the second, a ticket it issued on the first, and can tie the two visits together. Chrome and Firefox key their TLS session caches by the top-level site as well, for exactly this. A ticket is still offered once and never past its lifetime, and the same host can already do the same with a cookie wherever yonder sends it one | Privacy | S | the day yonder partitions cookies or storage by top-level site: the session cache's key gains the same part then, or the partition leaks through it | `userland/libtls/port/session_cache.c`; `userland/libfetch/fetch.c` `dial` |
| **A TLS 1.2 server that packs its first flight into one record is refused instead of falling back** (booked 2026-11-01 from the P5; the engine harness reproduces it). `tls13_handshake.c`'s `handshake` fails with `BR_ERR_UNEXPECTED` when bytes follow a ServerHello in its record — right for TLS 1.3, where everything after the hello is encrypted in records of its own — before `complete_message` can rule the hello a TLS 1.2 one (`TLS13_ERR_PEER_TLS12`). SChannel and a buffered OpenSSL send ServerHello, Certificate, ServerKeyExchange and ServerHelloDone in ONE record, so against them the engine reports `TLS protocol error (policy 0, engine 10)` and libfetch, which retries with the Bear TLS 1.2 client only on `OS64_TLS_PEER_CHOSE_TLS12`, never retries. The existing fallback case passes because its fixture (`scripted_hello`) sends the hello alone; the packed shape, fed through the same harness, answers status 10 where the lone hello answers 15. **Cure:** judge the hello first — a ServerHello that `complete_message` rules legacy may be followed by anything (the connection is discarded and a fresh one made), and only a TLS 1.3 hello with trailing bytes is the violation; add the packed flight to `fallback_downgrade`'s cases | Bug | XS | any TLS 1.2-only site (a browser-capabilities test page, 2026-11-01) | `userland/libtls/port/tls13_handshake.c` (`handshake`, the boundary check before `complete_message`), `tools/test_tls13_engine_host.c` |
| **A certificate chain with an extraneous or repeated certificate is refused** (booked 2026-11-01 from the P5). The validator (`certificate_policy.c` over Bear's streaming `x509_minimal`) reads the chain by POSITION: the first certificate is the leaf, every one after it must be a CA that issued the one before. A server that sends its leaf twice — a chain file with the leaf pasted in again, which a learning site did on 2026-11-01 (positions 0 and 1 the same serial and SHA-256) — fails the second copy's CA role (`policy 8`, "does not satisfy its CA or leaf role") and Bear's DN match (`engine 55`), and the site that worked a day earlier is a TLS error. RFC 8446 § 4.4.2: only the end-entity's place is fixed, and implementations SHOULD be prepared for extraneous certificates and arbitrary orderings; browsers build the path by issuer and ignore the rest. **Cure, in two sizes:** skip a certificate whose DER repeats one already seen (S, covers this case and the common "intermediate sent twice"); or build the path by issuer name from the whole list before validating, which is the standard's shape and needs the chain held whole rather than streamed (M). The cheap half first | Fidelity | S | a site whose chain carries a repeated or out-of-order certificate | `userland/libtls/port/certificate_policy.c` (`start_cert`/`end_cert`), `certificate_der.c` (`basic`, the role check) |
| **Loopback is TCP to 127/8 and nothing more (2026-09-23, REMOTE.md § 1).** Three things a fuller loopback would do are refused or absent, each on purpose: (1) UDP and ICMP to 127/8 are refused as `BAD_DEST` at dial — so `ping localhost` says no rather than working; their conn layers pick `kNetDevices[0]` and speak from the machine's address, and teaching them the per-connection local address TCP now has is the whole cost; (2) a dial to the machine's OWN LAN address still goes to the wire and is not looped back; (3) a port has one listener whatever its address — `tcp!127.0.0.1!N` and `tcp!*!N` together is `PORT_TAKEN`, where BSD would let both exist and give the specific one the call. None has a consumer: the consumer that built loopback (sshd forwarding to vncd) is TCP to 127.0.0.1 | Scope | S each | a program that wants one of the three | `loopback.h`, `syscall_net_dial`, `tcp_listener_announce` |
| **The P5's RX path went silent mid-transfer (2026-08-22)** — seen ONCE: `os64get` fetching its own replacement over the RTL8125 froze partway, the hub's lights kept blinking (the valet was still sending), a ping to the P5 got no answer, the log said nothing, and a reboot made the same transfer complete first try. The fingerprint says RECEIVE, not wire: frames arriving, nothing consumed — a stalled RX ring (descriptor ownership never handed back, or an interrupt the driver stopped hearing), not a dropped link. Nothing lost thanks to the `.part` discipline. UNDIAGNOSED; the next occurrence wants `/sys/bus/pci` and the driver's ring counters read BEFORE the reboot — an observability row as much as a bug row. Candidates worth a look when it recurs: the r8125 ring's refill under back-to-back full-MTU bursts, and the processSignals poll rider's relationship to a lost INTx (see the rows below). **SEEN A SECOND TIME, 2026-10-11 10:14:09** (read out of the P5's log on 10-18): the watchdog's deaf line fired with the transmit ring empty, 22 s after the boot's first RDU overrun; nothing arrived for 54 s, the VNC viewer's ssh session died of it, the recovery line printed only after the viewer left, and Chris rebooted. The watchdog now prints the last interrupt status and the age of the last arrival in that line; nothing else was in hand. **A THIRD SHAPE, probably different:** `os64get -a` pauses 10-30 s mid-transfer (Chris, 10-18; the log has a 13:57:23 run on 10-17 that went 32 s with no network line at all, then ten RDU overruns in 0.7 s as the stream resumed — a burst after a pause, which is what a reopened receive window or an RTO-recovered sender does, not what a dead ring does). During such a pause, `cat /sys/net/tcp` (is our window closed — `buf` full — or is the peer silent?), `/sys/net/r81250` and `/sys/net/knet` would decide it | Robustness | ? | the next pause with the three /sys/net files read during it | r8125.c / r8125_ring.c / os64get |
| ~~**`net_tcp_refused` fails "randomly" on the P5**~~ **PAID 2026-08-27, same day** (one post-boot failure in six boots, Chris): the dial that TIMES OUT with no RST counted is now a SKIP that names the reason — the gateway answered port 9 with silence, which is a fact about the router (a firewall, or a home router rate-limiting its own RSTs), not the stack. A dial that DID get an answer is still held to all three assertions. Whether the P5's gateway is silent or slow is still unread; if the SKIP shows up there often, the line says so | Flake | S | kernel/test/test_main.c test_net_tcp_refused |
| **`reset` means two things, and the morgue's `rst` flag can only say one.** `tcp_conn_t.reset` is set by an arriving RST *and* by tcp_poll when the retransmit retries exhaust — so a `/sys/net/tcp` row whose peer merely went silent reads `rst`, the word the eyes slice reserved for "the machine answered and said no". Harmless while nothing detached could exhaust (a bare FIN was never retransmitted before 2026-09-02, so a detached close never reached the exhaustion branch); now a close into a dead link ends there ~31s later and wears the wrong flag. Shape when paid: a second bit (`abandoned`) set by exhaustion, `reset` reserved for a RST that arrived, the row and dial's `why` reading both. Booked rather than done because every `c->reset \|\|` wake and return test would grow a sibling, which is the kind of sweep that earns its own review | Correctness | S | the first time someone reads `rst` on a row and goes looking for a peer that refused | tcp.c `tcp_poll` exhaustion branch; sysfs.c `r->rst = c->reset` |
| **A TCP dial cannot be interrupted.** `tcp_connect`'s handshake wait polls the state and `nap`s to its own deadline without ever asking `signal_park_must_end`, which is the question every other blocking wait in this kernel asks (CLAUDE.md § Signals: "a new blocking loop must ask it too"). So a caught signal does not reach a task that is dialing — Ctrl+C at a client showing `Trying host...` is not seen until the handshake finishes or the ten-second timeout expires, and a shutdown's SIGTERM waits the same. Found 2026-09-09 making telnet survive a resize: the TCP read and write paths answer `OS64_INTERRUPTED` correctly and only the connect does not. Paying it needs a value before it needs code, because **the dial vocabulary and the signal sentinel collide at -4** — `OS64_NET_ERR_BAD_ADDRESS` is the library parser's "that is not a dotted quad" and `OS64_INTERRUPTED` is the system-wide "your wait was cut short", so a dialer could not tell them apart. `os64/gui.h` met the same collision and moved its own -4 (NOT_RUNNING) out of the sentinel's way; the net table would do the same | Correctness | S | when a slow or silent dial has to be abandonable, or a shutdown has to be prompt with a dial in flight | `tcp.c` `tcp_connect`; `abi/include/os64/net.h` |
| **No DNS cache anywhere** (Chris, 2026-09-03: "I suspect not"). `resolve.c` is ring 3 and per process: the hosts files up the conf ladder, then one question to the server, the answer used once and forgotten — the TTL the server sent is not even parsed. Today it costs nothing, because os64get exits after every fetch and nothing long-lived resolves twice. The day a browser is one process making fifty lookups a page, every one of them is a round trip to the resolver, and on the P5 that is the internet's latency per link. Two honest shapes and they answer different questions: a per-process cache inside the resolver (keyed by name, expiring at the TTL) covers the browser and costs no new machinery; a machine-wide one needs a daemon (Solaris's nscd of 1990 is the ancestor; systemd-resolved the modern one) or a file, and os64 has no IPC for the former yet. Start with the per-process one; measure before the daemon | Performance | S | when the browser is up and the delays are noticed (Chris's own gate) | `userland/libos64/resolve.c` |
| **The entropy pool has no virtio-rng driver.** Every machine this OS runs on has RDSEED (RNG_PROBE.md); the bare QEMU model is the one machine without, and the boot jitter loop seeds it in milliseconds, so the device that hands a guest host entropy has no customer. It is a small driver (one virtqueue, one request) the day a VM without CPU randomness matters | Feature-gate | S | a VM target whose CPU model hides RDRAND/RDSEED and whose host entropy must be trusted over the jitter loop | RANDOM.md; random.c |
| **The entropy pool ESTIMATES nothing.** "Seeded" is a threshold — 256 hardware bits, 256 jitter deltas past the stuck test, or 1024 folded timing events past the same test — and `/sys/random` names which door it came through; no per-event credit arithmetic exists. Linux's estimator was a decade of argument that ended at roughly this shape. Booked so nobody reads the threshold as a measurement | Cleanup | M | never, unless a source's honest worth needs stating in bits | RANDOM.md; random.c fold_fast_pools_locked |
| **No `getrandom` syscall; the file is the door.** BearSSL reads a fresh seed from `/dev/random` per connection, and an open per DNS query is what the resolver pays. If a consumer ever proves the open costs it something, a syscall arrives then | Feature-gate | S | a measured cost | RANDOM.md; devfs.c DEV_NODE_RANDOM |
| **No seed is saved across boots.** The P5 has RDSEED; a saved seed is for machines that do not, and none of ours is one. Linux writes one at shutdown and reads it at boot for exactly that class of machine | Feature-gate | S | a target with no hardware source and a boot too short for the jitter loop | RANDOM.md |
| **Disk-completion timing does not feed the pool.** The NIC interrupts, the tick and knet's drain are the timing sources; NVMe completion joins when it has an interrupt to hang `random_add_timing` on (it is polled today) | Feature-gate | S | the async NVMe slice | RANDOM.md; random.h random_add_timing |
| **THE TERMINAL DOES NOT SPEAK UTF-8, so an em-dash in a message prints as three glyphs** — `cannot reach host:70 \xe2\x80\x94 no such host` on the glass. The font is not the problem and adding a glyph would not help: nothing DECODES the sequence, so `—` is three bytes and three lookups. Measured 2026-09-04: **1008 em-dashes live inside string literals** across the tree, so sweeping them to ASCII is a 1008-line diff that also would not cover what gets written tomorrow — the decoder fixes all of them without touching one. **THE CHEAP MOVE IS GONE, 2026-09-09**: this row used to say the cell need not grow because `_pad` was free — the CP437 slice spent that byte on the cell's character set, so `ch` widening to `uint16_t` now takes `os64_pty_cell_t` from 8 bytes to 12, and both the ABI assert in `syscall.c` and gterm's ring-3 painter are in the change. The alternative is packing `attrs`/`bg`/`charset` back into two bytes, which is its own argument. Wants: a byte-at-a-time decoder in tty.c with `ansi.c`'s shape and `ansi.c`'s host harness, BECAUSE A MULTI-BYTE SEQUENCE STRADDLES TWO WRITES the same way an escape does; a codepoint below 256 keeps indexing today's Latin-1 font (0xE9 really is `é`, which is why a 1994 gopher menu renders); an invalid sequence falls back to Latin-1 so a raw high byte still draws; and a small extras glyph table above 255 for the punctuation os64 actually writes (— – ' ' " " …). Chris's call 2026-09-04: he would have said "just use a hyphen", and is owed the character instead | Cosmetic | M | when somebody tires of the mojibake, or the browser meets a page that is not ASCII — whichever comes first | `kernel/src/tty.c`, `abi/include/os64/pty.h` `os64_pty_cell_t`, `userland/libos64/include/os64/font_psf1.h` |
| **A DOS DOOR WANTS A BARE CR, AND TELNET CANNOT SEND ONE.** Measured through a wiretap on 2026-09-09 at the "PRESS [ENTER] TO CONTINUE" screen of a 1993 poker door on the target board: `CR NUL` (this client's default, RFC 854's "the Return key was pressed") and `CR LF` (its `crlf on`) both drew ZERO bytes in reply, while a bare `0D` and a space each advanced it. The board's OWN prompts take `CR NUL` everywhere — the ANSI question, both name fields, `< ENTER >`, the bulletin menu — so this is the door path, where a telnet frontend is supposed to hand a DOS program the bare CR its FOSSIL expects and does not. Same defect that makes a LORD line-prompt double-prompt. **A bare CR is only LEGAL inside BINARY** (RFC 854 requires CR to be followed by LF or NUL otherwise), which is exactly why BSD telnet sends one only under `toggle binary` and why SyncTerm and NetRunner negotiate BINARY on a board. Two shapes: the small one is a third value on the existing EOL setting — `crlf on\|off` becoming `eol crlf\|nul\|cr`, default unchanged, about fifteen lines, effective but a deliberate protocol deviation; the honest one is negotiating BINARY both ways and sending bare CR under it, which also settles the 8-bit path this client already passes through by luck. Chris's call 2026-09-09, having reproduced it himself on the P5: booked, not built — "I'll survive without playing 90s poker a little bit longer" | Interop | S | when a door matters more than the doc does, or when ssh wants BINARY anyway | `userland/apps/telnet/telnet_protocol.c` `telnet_set_eol_crlf`; TELNET.md § Protocol scope |
| **Gopher+ is not spoken** (the 1993 extension: attribute blocks, `+INFO`, alternate views, forms). A Gopher+ server answers plain gopher too — the `+` it appends to an item line is read as a fifth field and ignored — so nothing in gopherspace is unreachable for want of it. It would buy item sizes and MIME types shown before you fetch, which is the only thing anyone would notice | Feature | M | when a server is met that answers ONLY Gopher+, or somebody wants sizes in a menu | `apps/gopher/wire.c` `gopher_item_parse` |
| **Item types `2`, `8` and `T` are shown and not followed**: CSO phone books (type 2) indexed campus directories that no longer exist, and `8`/`T` are telnet and tn3270 SESSIONS, which want BROWSER.md rung 5's client rather than a fetch. Each is drawn with its name, so a person can see what it is and go dial it by hand; following one blindly would be guessing what a type means, which is how you download a thing that is not what it said | Feature-gate | S | when rung 5 (telnet) lands — `8` becomes a spawn, the way `h` is a spawn of os64get today | `apps/gopher/wire.c` `gopher_type_followable` |
| **/bin/gopher keeps no cache and no bookmarks**: LEFT walks a history stack of addresses and REFETCHES each one, so going back costs a round trip and a menu you were reading a second ago is asked for again. Honest at a LAN's latency and visible at the internet's. Bookmarks are the other half of the same want — a place to put the addresses worth returning to, which today live in your shell history | Feature | M | when Chris notices the wait, or gopherspace gets browsed for long enough that retyping an address annoys | `apps/gopher/gopher.c` `browse` |
| **A NUL byte inside a menu line ends that line early**: `gopher_stream_line` hands back a C string, so a NUL arriving mid-line truncates it and whatever followed on that line is discarded. The outcome is SAFE — a truncated item line fails `gopher_item_parse` and is drawn as unreadable rather than followed — but the reason shown says "unreadable" where "a control character" would be truer. Refusing the line outright would need the reader to carry a length beside every string it returns | Cosmetic | S | if a length-carrying line reader is wanted for another reason | `apps/gopher/wire.c` `gopher_stream_line` |
| **/bin/gopher's text viewer CLIPS a long line rather than wrapping it**, so a line wider than the screen is only ever readable to the right margin, and one longer than `GOPHER_LINE_MAX` (4096) is not even held whole — `os64_linereader_line` truncates it and swallows the rest by contract, and the page is marked truncated on the evidence of a full buffer. Both halves are the same missing feature: a viewer that soft-wraps at the window width, which would also give the marking an exact answer instead of a full-buffer inference. It bites minified HTML through the `h` handoff, where the whole document can be one line — 80 columns of it, honestly labelled. Wrapping changes what a "line" means to the scroller, the page count and Page Up/Down, which is why it is a feature and not a fix | Feature | M | when a handed-off page is one somebody actually wants to read, or a gopher text file is met that is written in long paragraphs | `apps/gopher/gopher.c` `view_local_text` / `draw_clipped` |
| ~~**STOP-AND-WAIT SENDING: one unacknowledged segment at a time.**~~ **PAID 2026-09-07 by the send window (PR #65): a 64KB send ring, RFC 5681 congestion control with NewReno and limited transmit, 4.4BSD go-back-N on timeout, a persist timer for a shut window, and a submission contract that treats a local drop as loss (`TCP_SENDER.md`). The rig's delay+loss upload leg went 19.9s → 4.4s; the 13.8s this row priced was the whole of it. VERIFICATION.md carries the upload table. Original row kept as the record:** Honest for what os64 sends today (a request is one segment), and priced on 2026-09-04 by the rig's upload leg: 100KB at a 200ms round trip takes 19.9s with the measured RTO, of which 13.8s is 69 segments each waiting for its own ack — the number a send window (RFC 793's SND.WND honoured, several segments in flight, a retransmit queue instead of one slot) would take back. Nothing in the tree uploads yet; the day something does over a real link (the P5 sending to the internet, FTP's STOR) is the day this is paid. `/tests/netsend` + `tools/tcpsink.py` are the instrument | Feature-gate | L | when an upload over a real round trip is measured slow, or FTP STOR arrives | tcp.h V1 SCOPE, tcp.c tcp_conn_write / the retransmit slot |
| **No Nagle (RFC 896).** Every write goes out as soon as the windows allow, however small: a program writing one byte at a time to a connection with data already in flight puts one-byte segments on the wire, forty bytes of header each, which is the 1984 Ford Aerospace problem RFC 896 was written about — and was written about TELNET, the program that most wants the answer to be "send it now". A send buffer makes coalescing possible, and the first bulk writer that trickles is the consumer that earns the knob (with a per-connection off switch, since the terminal program wants it off). Nothing in the tree writes that way today: os64get writes one request, netsend writes 8KB at a time | Performance | S | when `/sys/net/tcp` shows `segments_out` far past `tx_bytes / mss` on an upload | tcp.c tcp_output (the SWS rule is where it would live) |
| ~~**No window scaling (RFC 1323).**~~ **PAID 2026-09-10 (fable/tcp-wscale): both windows go through RFC 7323's shift count — ours rides the SYN beside the MSS (`tcp_options.h`, a pure module with a host test, because QEMU's user networking cannot exercise a scaling peer), the peer's is read from its SYN-ACK and applied to every window field after it, and both rings grew from 64KB to 1MB, the bandwidth-delay product the P5 was measured against with headroom (200 Mb/s at 28ms is 700KB; 1MB at 28ms is the P5's own NIC ceiling). A peer that sends no shift gets the unscaled field, which is the old 64KB and not a regression. What is NOT paid with it: the passive side (no listener exists to put our shift in a SYN-ACK; the parser is written for it) and delayed ACKs (an ACK per segment is ~4% of the return path at any window, a separate slice if it ever shows in a measurement). Original row kept as the record:** Both windows are 16 bits, so neither side can have more than 64KB in flight, and a single connection is therefore capped at window ÷ round trip whatever the link can carry. **THE TRIGGER FIRED ON 2026-09-09 AND THE ROW IS NOW PRICED.** This row used to say the P5 on a home connection was nowhere near that ceiling; it is sitting on it. Chris fetched a 38,428,617-byte file from ftp.gnu.org with `/bin/ftp`, which reported **1996.1 KB/s (16.4 Mb/s)**; the P5's own `ping` put that host at **28ms** (5 replies, 3/2/4/2/3 ticks at the 10ms resolution `ping` prints), and 64KB ÷ 28ms is **2340 KB/s — so the transfer flattened at 87% of the theoretical maximum**, which is what a bulk transfer against a fixed window looks like. THREE MEASUREMENTS MAKE IT A MODEL RATHER THAN A COINCIDENCE, because the same arithmetic predicts all of them: **os64get fetching over HTTP from the same host took the same time**, so the cap is beneath both clients and both protocols and belongs to the stack; the same P5 to a **LAN** server ran at 179 Mb/s, which is 64KB ÷ 2.9ms and again the ceiling, so throughput tracked 1/RTT across a 10x change with nothing else different; and the CONTROL that clears the link — a Windows browser across the SAME wifi/ICS path to the same mirror — pulled ~200 Mb/s, because every modern stack scales its windows and is not living in a 64KB box. What paying it buys is exactly that gap: at a 28ms round trip, 16 Mb/s becomes whatever the path actually gives. The shape is unchanged — the option rides the SYN (RFC 1323 §2.2, each direction negotiated on its own), the receive ring grows past 64KB, and the send side applies the peer's shift to `snd_wnd`. Fable's tier by BROWSER.md's rule (any change to tcp.c's protocol behavior) | Performance | M | paid | tcp_options.h; tcp.h TCP_RCV_WSCALE, TCP_RCV_BUF |
| **THE GAP TO THE LINK.** With the window scaled, the P5 sustains ~154-158 Mb/s from the internet (VERIFICATION § window scaling) on a connection that regularly shows 300 Mb/s to a Windows browser over the same ICS path. Half the link is a lot to leave on the table, and nothing has yet said where it goes — candidates, none measured: the 1MB ring at the path's real round trip (1MB at 50ms is 160 Mb/s, suspiciously close), an ACK per segment on the return path, the copy path from ring to file at write-through ext2 speed, the receiver's tick-granular wakes. The instrument is the same fetch with `ping` beside it and `/sys/net/tcp`'s `win` and `buf` columns read mid-transfer; if `buf` sits near the ring the ring is the answer and it is one constant | Performance | S to price, unknown to pay | when Chris books the day for it (his words, 2026-09-10: "sometime later in the future") | tcp.h TCP_RCV_BUF; VERIFICATION § window scaling |
| **A TICK-DRIVEN NIC'S RING IS SMALLER THAN A SCALED WINDOW.** A NIC drained once per tick (virtio, and anything under `NETPOLL`) can take at most its receive ring per pass, and with the window at 1MB the ring is the smaller number: virtio's 64 slots are ~93KB a tick, ~9MB/s, and a scaling peer that sends more than that in one tick has the excess dropped at the ring and recovered as loss — cwnd halves, the peer learns the ring's pace the hard way. Nothing in the tree hits it today: QEMU's user networking never scales (libslirp answers MSS-only), and the P5's RTL8125 rings its own doorbell and has 1024 slots. It becomes real the day a tap netdev puts the Linux host behind the cable as a scaling peer. Two shapes: grow virtio's rings (`VQ_SIZE_CAP`, a slab budget) or give virtio the doorbell the e1000 and RTL8125 have. Booked here on 2026-09-10 rather than fixed, because the rig that would measure it is the same tap the row above says was not set up | Performance (harness) | S | when a scaling peer sits behind a tick-driven NIC | virtio_net.c VQ_SIZE_CAP; tcp.h buffer comment |
| **No SACK (RFC 2018).** The receiver holds ahead-of-sequence data in the ring since 2026-09-04, but the sender is told only where we are (`rcv_nxt`), never what we already hold, so a hole is refilled by the sender's own rules: fast retransmit on our duplicate acks (one resend, then everything held behind it delivers — the rig's 34s → 1s), or its RTO. With several holes in one window a SACK-less sender resends more than it needs to; the rig's `loss` knob beside `reorder` is the measurement. `TCP_HELD_MAX` (16 ranges) is the same slice's other bound: `out_of_order_dropped` rising beside a large `out_of_order_held` is that number being too small for the weather | Feature-gate | M | when `/sys/net/tcp` shows `retransmits` climbing under reordering, or `dropped` beside `held` | tcp.h TCP_HELD_MAX, tcp.c tcp_hold |
| ~~**A PEER THAT ADVERTISES A ZERO WINDOW STALLS THE SENDER FOREVER.**~~ **PAID 2026-09-09 (PR #65 and PR #85):** PR #65 added send-ring queueing, zero-window persist probes and bounded detached lifetime. `os64_write_for` now supplies finite patience through write's fourth argument, returning queued progress or `OS64_ERR_TIMEOUT` when no room arrives. Owned connections keep TCP's persist policy; the caller decides when to give up and close. `os64_write` explicitly requests infinite patience. The original defect coupled writes directly to the peer window, so a zero window could trap the caller before its application deadline was checked | Robustness | S | paid | tcp.c `tcp_conn_write`; syscall 3 arg3 |
| **Closing a connection with unread receive data sends FIN, not RST.** Seen on the rig 2026-09-04: os64get closed a handle with 38KB still unread, the buffer filled, we advertised a zero window, and the peer's sender sat stalled on a connection nobody would ever read until its own timeout — FIN_WAIT_2, `zwin`, `det` in `/sys/net/tcp`. 4.4BSD answers a close-with-unread-data with RST (RFC 2525 §2.17 names the opposite as a known problem), which tells the peer at once that the bytes went nowhere. The sender now bounds detached FIN_WAIT_2 to 30 seconds without ACK progress, but still does not immediately notify the peer with RST. The row tracks that remaining behavior | Robustness | S | when a peer is seen stalled on a detached connection | tcp.c tcp_conn_close |
| **DHCP lease renewal** (T1/T2 timers) + RELEASE/DECLINE courtesies — v1 leases once and never renews; slirp/VBox leases are functionally eternal, a real LAN's are not | Feature-gate | M | before long uptimes on a real LAN (the P5) | dhcp.h policy note |
| IPv4 fragment REASSEMBLY — arrivals with MF/offset drop loudly (counted + logged). Modern DF-world paths rarely fragment; "rarely" is not "never" | Feature-gate | M | when something real fragments at us | ipv4.c fragment stance |
| ICMP port-unreachable when UDP has no binding (how `traceroute` ends and `nc -u` probes learn) — currently counted-and-silent | Feature-gate | S | when a consumer needs the courtesy | udp.c rx_no_binding |
| **virtio-net has no interrupt wired** — NETWORK.md Phase 1 planned MSI-X from the start; polling shipped first because it made packets move the same day. Since 2026-09-05 its drain runs in knet, rung by the tick once a tick (DOORBELL.md) — the cadence it always had, out from under the scheduler lock — so frames still reach it at the tick's cadence (`ping` to the slirp gateway reads 7–11 ms on virtio against 0.6 ms on the e1000, 2026-09-27 — the drain, not the wake) — the reader's wake-on-arrival and the ring-full reap in transmit are what moved its QEMU number, not the bell; the e1000 and the RTL8125 ring the bell on arrival and it does not. Its conversion is per-queue MSI-X machinery, a different slice; the PCI capability walker (`pci_find_capability`) and the doorbell are now there for it. Mind the house rule (AP-routed vectors ≥ 0x40) | Feature-gate | M | when the QEMU rig's number matters, or when MSI-X wants a first customer | virtio_net.c / NETWORK.md Phase 1 |
| ~~processSignals runs the device polls AND the whole inline protocol stack UNDER kSchedulerSwitchTasksLock~~ **PAID 2026-09-05 with the bottom half (DOORBELL.md):** the NIC drains, the protocol stack they feed and the TCP/DHCP timers run in knet, a kernel thread, on nobody's lock but their own; `processSignals` keeps the sleeper sweep, one ring of knet's bell per tick, and the wake sweeps. Chris's 2026-08-01 ruling said the cleanup lands WITH the bottom half, and it did. What the lock still covers is measurable in `/sys/net/knet` only indirectly (the drain's cycles are the thread's now, `wake_cycles_max`); a hold-time stamp on the pass itself is a diagnostic nobody has needed since | Cleanup (PAID) | — | done | signals.c processSignals / DOORBELL.md |
| ~~**e1000 INTx Stage 2 — the bottom half**~~ **PAID 2026-09-05 (DOORBELL.md):** the wake-from-ISR primitive the scheduler lacked is the doorbell — an interrupt handler rings it lock-free and provokes a pass with a self-IPI in the scheduler's own priority class; the pass answers it at two service points under the queue lock it already holds. The e1000's ISR rings knet's bell instead of raising `kE1000RxWork`; the drain runs on arrival. QEMU e1000 rig: 4MB upload 29.8s → 1.8s (one segment per tick before). The row's own warning, "wake sweeps live in processSignals precisely because of the 9badced lock saga", is why the ring takes no lock and the answer runs inside the pass | Feature-gate (PAID) | — | done | e1000.c e1000_isr / doorbell.c |
| **knet wakes once per frame** (measured 2026-09-05 on the QEMU e1000 rig: ~2,900 frames, 2,509 wakes; `/sys/net/knet`). A top half with no coalescing does exactly that — one interrupt, one wake, one drain per arrival, bursts folding into a wake only when they land inside a drain. Under TCG a wake is an emulated scheduler pass, which is why the QEMU download barely moved (1.5s → 1.0s) while the upload moved 17×; on the P5 a wake is microseconds and the count may not matter at gigabit. Two levers if it does, neither built: the RTL8125's hardware interrupt mitigation (`IntrMitigate`, 0xE2 — the vendor sets it), and a NAPI-style linger in knet (spin a bounded moment after a productive drain before parking, so back-to-back frames coalesce). MEASURE ON THE P5 FIRST: `wakes` against `rx_frames` across a big `os64get`, then decide | Performance | S–M | when the P5's `/sys/net/knet` says wakes track frames AND the download number is below the wire | knet.c / DOORBELL.md |
| **`expedite` is a one-pass boost, not a priority.** The doorbell marks its woken thread so the pick that follows takes it over aging (aging resets to zero on every entry to RUNNABLE, so a freshly woken drainer would tie with the thread the interrupt displaced and lose on queue order). The dispatch clears it, so at the next tick knet competes on aging like everyone else — deliberately, so a flood cannot starve husk on the BSP. The day something wants a standing priority (a real-time thread, a compositor that must never wait), this flag is the seed of that slice and must not be widened into it by accident | Cleanup | M | when a second customer wants "always first" rather than "first this once" | scheduler.c find_thread_to_run / thread.h expedite |
| **One knet, pinned to the BSP** (Chris, 2026-09-05: "keep the complexity down since this will be a very complex change"). The NIC interrupts are routed at the BSP, so a ring is a self-IPI and the tick that preempts a busy drainer is the BSP's own. The next shape is a network core: knet on a tickless AP with the MSI addressed there, so line-rate traffic never shares a core with the shell — and per-NIC drainers the day a machine has two NICs that matter. **The P5 has shown the inverse of the feared shape (2026-10-18):** not the drain saturating the BSP, but the BSP's other tenants starving the drain — 75% of the RTL8125's rings landed with knet runnable and off the CPU (`bell_rung_runnable`), and the receive ring overran 57 times in three seconds under a page load while yonder, vncd and sshd ran beside it. The 2026-10-18 slice grew the ring to cover the wait and added `/sys/net/knet`'s `wake_latency_*`; `late_10ms` climbing with a NIC's `rx_overruns` is this row's trigger met | Feature-gate | M | a second NIC that matters, or `/sys/net/knet` `wake_latency_late_10ms` climbing beside a NIC's `rx_overruns` on the P5 | kernel.c knet creation / DOORBELL.md |
| ~~**UDP conns still wake their reader at the tick.**~~ **PAID 2026-09-27 (ARRIVAL_WAKE.md), AND THE ROW'S PREMISE WAS WRONG: `udp_conn_rx` and `icmp_conn_deliver` both wake a parked reader on arrival now, tcp_input's shape, the sweeps kept as the backstop for a reader registered but not yet parked — but measured with the wakes disabled, the sweep already woke the reader 15–128 µs after the enqueue, because knet's own park after a drain is a scheduler pass and the sweep runs in it. "At the tick" is true only under a loaded drain, where knet never parks between frames; that is the case the code is kept for, and it is not measured. What the slice visibly bought is the instrument: `micros()` (syscall 59) and a `ping` that prints milliseconds — 0.6 ms to the slirp gateway on the e1000, where the old `ping` printed "0 ticks". virtio-net's DRAIN is still rung by the tick (its own row): `ping` reads 7–11 ms there, and no wake can shorten a read that has not happened.** Original row: tcp_input wakes a parked reader on arrival now (the pipe idiom — knet is a thread and may take the queue lock), but udp_conn's enqueue was left on the sweep alone: nothing has asked for a faster datagram. One `scheduler_wake_isleep_thread` after the enqueue, condition and release order copied from tcp_input's tail | Performance | XS | paid | udp_conn.c context map / icmp_conn.c / test_net_icmp_conn's wake figure |
| **`micros()` cannot run backward, but on a machine whose cores' TSCs disagree it can STALL.** Syscall 59 reads `rdtsc` on whichever core the caller is on and subtracts an anchor read on the BSP — a cross-core comparison, which nvme.c's house rule says QEMU and WSL2 have both been seen to punish with per-core offsets. The value is folded through a global high-water mark (Codex #145 rd1), so a lagging core hands out the last figure anybody was given instead of a smaller one, and a core behind the anchor reads 0 instead of 2^64 − offset; a desynchronized pair therefore costs an interval its accuracy by at most the offset, never its sign. What is not built is the check that would make the interval RIGHT: Linux's boot-time sync test (exchange counters across an IPI round trip per AP, record each core's offset, subtract it on read) | Robustness | S | an interval that reads visibly short on real hardware, or a multi-socket machine | syscall.c `syscall_micros` |
| **The sweep-backed waiters could take the owed wake and retire their sweeps.** Pipes, the console and ptys, UDP, TCP, ICMP and thread joins still wake with `scheduler_wake_isleep_thread`, and a wake that lands while their waiter is mid-park is caught by a per-pass sweep in `processSignals` — one pass late, and every pass pays for the walk. The owed wake (WAKE_OWED.md) closes that window instead; moving them to it is a deliberate non-goal of that slice, because retiring a sweep changes what each of those subsystems relies on and earns its own review (Fable's, of WAKE_OWED.md) | Performance | M | open | WAKE_OWED.md § What does not change |
| **A ring that lands after a pass's service point waits for the next tick.** `doorbell_ring` sends its self-IPI only when the target core is not already mid-pass (send_ipi's rule); a ring during a pass that has already answered its bells is caught by the next tick, ≤10ms later, or by the one-second backstop. Rare (the pass is microseconds) and bounded, but a pass that re-checked the bells at its exit and re-provoked itself would close it. Not done because the tick already bounds it at today's latency and the exit path is the scheduler's most delicate code (SCHEDULER_REENTRANCY.md Fix 2) | Cleanup | S | when a latency measurement on the P5 shows a 10ms tail that the counters attribute to it | doorbell.c doorbell_ring / DOORBELL.md |
| **No shared-INTx framework**: e1000's ISR practices shared-line etiquette (ICR==0 → count a stranger, decline politely) but nothing CHAINS handlers on one GSI. Also note: e1000_enable_intx sets PCI Interrupt Disable (command bit 10) on every OTHER device — os64's drivers all poll, so this makes the de facto official; the day a second driver wants a doorbell it must clear its own bit AND this framework gets built. intx_strangers climbing = a neighbor moved onto GSI without telling us | Feature-gate | S | the day a second PCI device adopts INTx | e1000.c enable_intx probe-safety comment |
| **Loadable driver modules** (booked 2026-08-21, Chris's pick; ratified as a FEATURE, not a sneakernet cure — HD boot solves the logistics alone, since the kernel is one file). v1 shape agreed in the sizing talk: LOAD-ONLY at boot (unload is where it becomes XL — quiescing IRQs, refcounts on every registration, in-flight I/O), resolve against the kernel's own symtab (symbols.c already carries 1554 names — the export table exists), relocate with the machinery elf_relocate.c already proves every boot, load into a module VA arena within ±2GB of kernel text (mcmodel=kernel reach — the same constraint that gave Linux its module area). The chicken-and-egg blesses the split: whatever mounts root (NVMe/AHCI, ext2, console) stays built-in; NICs, USB, GUI are the candidates — or modules ride Limine module_path lines and load pre-root. Prerequisite that pays double: a poll/IRQ registration seam — processSignals calls xhci_poll BY NAME today (the NICs moved behind the seam's `drain` verb and knet on 2026-09-05 — DOORBELL.md), and the xHCI half of that tidy-up waits for a third driver; a module loader IS that third driver. Kernel W^X (the NX arc's booked half) is the natural companion for module text. Lineage: Linux shipped its first four years all-built-in too — insmod arrived with 1.2 in 1995; SunOS 4 beat them to it | Feature | L | after HD boot; wants the poll-registration seam + module VA arena designed first (known-debt-beforehand: design talk before code) | 2026-08-21 sizing conversation; symbols.c / elf_relocate.c as the down payment |
| **The r8125 PHY runs on its power-on state, not the vendor's `hw_phy_config`** (booked 2026-09-05, the PHY-advertisement slice). Realtek's driver and Linux's r8169 both load a per-revision PHY firmware blob (`rtl8125b-2.fw`) and apply thousands of register writes before the first negotiation; os64 writes the standard MII advertisement and restarts autoneg, nothing else. RTL8125.md's "two landmines" named this the day the driver was designed; it is booked now because the driver finally touches the PHY and the difference became a decision. THE GATE: the `DEBUG_BOOT` negotiation dump showing both sides offering 1000 and the link still landing at 100 — that is a training failure standard registers cannot reach, and it is this row's turn. Shape when it comes: the firmware is redistributable (linux-firmware), the register table lives in GPL source whose facts would be re-expressed here and whose code would not be copied (the MIT tree's rule, applied to the host test 2026-09-08), and neither can be rehearsed anywhere but the P5 | Feature-gate | L | the day the negotiation dump says the advertisement was not the problem | RTL8125.md AMENDMENT 2 / r8125_phy_configure |
| **2.5GBASE-T is advertised** (`R8125_ADVERTISE_2500`, 2026-09-05) — the PHY's own default, left alone. The slice's first draft turned it OFF on the theory that older gigabit switch silicon fumbles the 802.3bz next pages and falls back to 100M; the P5's "as found" dump refuted it the same afternoon (2.5G advertised, partner offering 10/100/1000, link 1000/full, before the driver touched anything). ON means a firmware-default PHY matches the plan and a healthy boot never renegotiates. The remaining debt is what a REAL 2.5G partner would do to a receive path drained once per scheduler pass: the 256-descriptor ring already overruns on gigabit bursts, so the day a 2.5G switch arrives the bottom-half row above is what pays this one | Feature-gate | XS | with the bottom-half row, or the day a 2.5G switch arrives | r8125.h R8125_ADVERTISE_2500 |
| **The r8125 leaves the PHY's pause advertisement as found — and as found it is ON.** The P5's 2026-09-05 dump read ANAR 0x1de1 (pause + asymmetric pause advertised by the PHY's default) and PHYstatus 0xf08000f3 (RxFlowCtrl and TxFlowCtrl both resolved), so flow control is negotiated on that link today. What is NOT known is whether the MAC acts on it: the vendor driver configures MAC-side flow control separately (`tp->fcpause`), and this driver has never touched that. If it does act, the NIC is already asking the switch to hold when the FIFO fills instead of dropping, and `isr_overrun_events` across an `os64get -a` is the instrument that says so; if it does not, enabling it is the cheapest throughput win available to a driver drained once per 10ms pass | Performance | S | a quiet P5 afternoon with the overrun counter in hand | r8125_phy_plan_advertisement's policy comment; vendor r8125_n.c fcpause |

## libfetch (the fetch seam, 2026-09-11 — booked the day it was cut; LIBFETCH.md carries the arguments)

| Debt | Class | Size | Trigger | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`gopher://` as a libfetch scheme.** The gopher client's own wire code works and its menu parser is the UI's; moving the bytes buys nothing until a second program wants gopher bytes. The scheme table is the seam it joins at | Feature | S | the browser's first `gopher://` link | LIBFETCH.md § Booked |
| ~~**A kept connection saves a handshake only while it is kept.**~~ **PAID 2026-11-10 (HTTP2.md H2.1): TLS 1.3 sessions are kept and resumed (TLS13.md § Resumption); a reconnect to a host visited within the ticket's life skips the certificate chain. What stays unpaid is the TLS 1.2 fallback's handshake, named in the row "TLS 1.3 engine boundaries".** Was: connections are kept between fetches (WIRE.md § 2), but the first connection to a host pays a whole TLS handshake every time, and so does every one after the server closes an idle connection: session resumption was not spoken (tickets were consumed and discarded). It was the next cut of a page's connection cost worth paying | Perf | M | a page load whose connections are few but whose handshakes still dominate its time | `userland/libfetch/pool.c`; `userland/libtls/` |
| ~~**Fetches to one host are not capped at once.**~~ **PAID 2026-11-10 (HTTP2.md H2.2): a key has at most six connections of every kind, a seventh fetch waits for one on its bell, and an idle connection never makes a fetch wait (pool.h).** Was: the pool keeps at most six idle connections to a key (`FETCH_POOL_PER_KEY`), but a seventh fetch to a host whose six connections are all busy dials a seventh connection rather than waiting for one, as Chrome's six-per-host limit makes it wait: a page that asks for a dozen sheets at once opens a dozen connections and handshakes, which then carry the rest of the page between them (WIRE.md § 2 as built). A cap is a queue in front of the dial, and a wait a fetch's cancellation must be able to end | Perf | M | a page whose burst of same-host requests still pays a handshake apiece before its connections start being reused | `userland/libfetch/pool.c`; `userland/libfetch/fetch.c` `exchange` |
| ~~**HTTP/2 and pipelining are not spoken.**~~ **PAID 2026-11-11 (HTTP2.md H2.5): a direct https connection is offered HTTP/2 and one connection carries every request to its host at once;** `http2 = off` in net.conf turns it off. Pipelining is spoken by nobody and stays unbuilt | Perf | L | — | `userland/libfetch/pool.h`; HTTP2.md |
| **HTTP/2 is spoken on a direct TLS 1.3 connection and nowhere else.** A hop through a proxy, the TLS 1.2 fallback and a plain `http://` hop stay HTTP/1.1; there is no `h2c`, no `Upgrade`, no prior knowledge (no browser speaks them) | Perf (narrow) | M | a proxy in daily use whose pages wait in line for six connections (it needs CONNECT tunnelling of https, a larger change than HTTP/2 over it); a server that matters and speaks h2 only over 1.2 | HTTP2.md § 2.1, § 2.10 |
| **What HTTP/2 offers and libfetch declines**: server push (`ENABLE_PUSH` 0, and a PUSH_PROMISE is a connection error); CONNECT and extended CONNECT, so no WebSockets over it; PRIORITY and PRIORITY_UPDATE frames (a request's priority is said once, on its HEADERS, as the preset's browser says it); keepalive PINGs, and the PING Chrome sends ahead of a request on a quiet connection | Completeness | S each | a server that needs one of them to serve a page; WebSockets being built at all | HTTP2.md § 2.10; `userland/libfetch/h2_conn.c` |
| **HTTP/3 is never learned of.** Alt-Svc and HTTPS DNS records are not read, so a server's offer of h3 goes unheard, and a host's HTTP/2 support is remembered for the life of one process only (the first visit after a start dials once per fetch that finds no connection, up to six) | Perf | L (h3 is QUIC: a transport of its own) | measurements showing the first visit's extra dials, or h3-only servers | HTTP2.md § 2.10; `userland/libfetch/pool.c` `remember` |
| **A condemned HTTP/2 connection's socket closes up to a tenth of a second late, and its reader wakes ten times a second to keep it that short.** Its reader thread is the socket's only closer and looks every `H2_READER_SLICE_MS`; the pool counts a closing socket against its total until then, so a fetch that needs that slot waits out the slice. A page of more hosts than the pool has places pays it on each new host (measured on the P5: HTTP2.md § H2.5, as built) | Perf | S (kernel) | a kernel verb that hangs a connection up without closing its handle, wanted for any other reason | HTTP2.md § 2.3, Q3 |
| **A browser preset's HTTP/2 priority is captured for five kinds of request.** A document, a sheet, a script, a picture and a `fetch`; a font, a frame, a worker and anything else asks with the `fetch` row's weight and Priority line, which is a guess at what that browser sends for them. Safari's preset has no capture at all and greets as libfetch's own does | Fidelity | S per capture | a site that scores the priority of a font or frame request; a Safari to capture from | `userland/apps/yonder/agent.c`; captures in the kit's `h2cap/` |
| **A kept HTTP/1.1 connection is shared whatever ALPN its dial offered.** A fetch that offers `h2, http/1.1` may ride a connection a costume (or a fetch under `http2 = off`) dialled offering `http/1.1` alone, and the other way round, when that connection was in use as the identity changed. ALPN is part of what a server can score a TLS handshake by, and os64's handshake is not a browser's either way | Fidelity (narrow) | S | the handshake itself being matched to a preset (DIVERGENCES, "The browser's identity") | `userland/libfetch/pool.c` `fetch_pool_take` |
| **A request of more than 256 fields is refused over HTTP/2** (`FETCH_H2_FIELDS`, a Cookie line counted as its pairs: about 245 cookies), where HTTP/1.1 would send it as text | Completeness (narrow) | S | a site that sets that many cookies | `userland/libfetch/fetch.c` |
| **The system trust store is read once per program.** A fetch whose caller gives no store verifies against the system's, which libfetch reads on the first https hop and shares for the rest of the process (`fetch_pool_trust`), and every kept connection is keyed by the store it was verified under. So an edit to `/etc/certs` or `tls.conf` reaches the programs started after it, and not a running browser: until 2026-10-24 every fetch read the store again (and parsed every root of it), so an edit reached the next fetch | Security (narrow) | S | a store edit that must reach a running program: a reload verb (re-read, publish the new store, and let the pool keep connections keyed by the old one until they idle out or are drained) | `userland/libfetch/pool.c` `fetch_pool_trust` |
| **`Range:` and resume** (BROWSER.md 3e). `extra_headers` can carry the header today; 206 semantics and the resume discipline want a consumer | Feature | S | a download worth resuming | LIBFETCH.md § Booked |
| **Cookie jar.** libfetch carries per-hop Cookie/Referer and individual Set-Cookie values; storage and matching belong to the navigator | Feature | M | the first site that will not show a page without one | LIBFETCH.md § Booked |
| **A `data:` picture cannot be saved.** libfetch never sees one: yonder's picture worker decodes a `data:` picture with the processor its Fetch uses (`yonder_fetch_data_url`), from markup and from sheets alike, at any length the page's catalog of addresses allows (libpage's `p_resolve_picture` keeps a picture's past `OS64_URL_REF_MAX`). Save Picture As takes `http:` and `https:` alone, so it is not offered for one | Feature | S | a person who wants to save one | yonder.c `downloadable` |
| **Progressive `open`** (head available before the body on a non-blocking loop). Every consumer today runs a fetch on a thread | Feature | M | a single-threaded UI that must not block | LIBFETCH.md § Booked |

## libpage (what a page MEANS, 2026-09-12 — booked the day it was cut; LIBPAGE.md carries the arguments)

| Debt | Class | Size | Trigger | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Range arithmetic differs from Firefox at extreme precision boundaries.** The bounded decimal-grid contract passes the independent Decimal oracle, but eight of 186 retained Firefox observations differ. Keep those cases visible when expanding browser compatibility; full browser equivalence is not established | Compatibility | S | Broader independent range conformance evidence | LIBPAGE_REVIEW.md; tools/libpage_range_reference.json |
| **A `file` input always sends an empty filename and no content.** No face can pick a file, so the entry the standard sends for a field with none is what goes out, and the multipart part carries `filename=""` with nothing between the blank line and the part's CRLF. That is the correct answer for today, and the shape the content slots into | Feature | M | the graphical browser's file dialog | LIBPAGE.md § D, `encode.c` file part |
| **A submission the model validates alone judges no `pattern`** (booked 2026-10-28, 0f's P4a). HTML's pattern is the script engine's regular expression, and libpage has none: a page whose scripts run validates a person's submission with the engine before its submit event (`os64_dom_form_validate`), and the model then does not validate again; but a page whose scripts are off, or run with the Modern Web switch off, or that never ran a script and carries no `oninvalid` or `onsubmit`, and an image button anywhere, submit a value the pattern refuses | Compatibility | S | a page whose scripts do not run that needs its pattern kept | `submit.c` `p_validate` |
| **With the Modern Web switch off, `requestSubmit()` with no submitter is validated by nobody** (booked 2026-10-28, 0f's P4a's cold read). The binding validates it only with the switch on; with it off the ask reaches the model as a form submitting itself, which HTML's `form.submit()` rule leaves unvalidated, so a classic-mode page whose script calls `requestSubmit()` sends an empty required field.
